# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [1] [Hear No Evil: Detecting Gradient Leakage by Malicious Servers in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20651)
*Fei Wang, Baochun Li*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recent work has shown that gradient updates in federated learning (FL) can unintentionally reveal sensitive information about a client's local data. This risk becomes significantly greater when a malicious server manipulates the global model to provoke information-rich updates from clients. In this paper, we adopt a defender's perspective to provide the first comprehensive analysis of malicious gradient leakage attacks and the model manipulation techniques that enable them. Our investigation reveals a core trade-off: these attacks cannot be both highly effective in reconstructing private data and sufficiently stealthy to evade detection -- especially in realistic FL settings that incorporate common normalization techniques and federated averaging. Building on this insight, we argue that malicious gradient leakage attacks, while theoretically concerning, are inherently limited in practice and often detectable through basic monitoring. As a complementary contribution, we propose a simple, lightweight, and broadly applicable client-side detection mechanism that flags suspicious model updates before local training begins, despite the fact that such detection may not be strictly necessary in realistic FL settings. This mechanism further underscores the feasibility of defending against these attacks with minimal overhead, offering a deployable safeguard for privacy-conscious federated learning systems.


### [2] [Leaner Training, Lower Leakage: Revisiting Memorization in LLM Fine-Tuning with LoRA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20856)
*Fei Wang, Baochun Li*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Memorization in large language models (LLMs) makes them vulnerable to data extraction attacks. While pre-training memorization has been extensively studied, fewer works have explored its impact in fine-tuning, particularly for LoRA fine-tuning, a widely adopted parameter-efficient method. In this work, we re-examine memorization in fine-tuning and uncover a surprising divergence from prior findings across different fine-tuning strategies. Factors such as model scale and data duplication, which strongly influence memorization in pre-training and full fine-tuning, do not follow the same trend in LoRA fine-tuning. Using a more relaxed similarity-based memorization metric, we demonstrate that LoRA significantly reduces memorization risks compared to full fine-tuning, while still maintaining strong task performance.


### [3] [Automatic Construction of Strong Simple Baselines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40700)
*Caleb N. Ellington, Jiaqi Wang, Sohan Addagudi, Elijah Cole*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: To rigorously establish the value of a novel deep learning method, it must be compared against appropriate baselines. As deep learning has become increasingly common in the physical and life sciences, there have been numerous cases in which apparent progress has turned out to be an artifact of omitted or missing baselines. In this work, we investigate whether this problem can be mitigated by using large language models to automate the construction of baselines. We assemble 36 supervised classification and regression tasks from peer-reviewed studies in Nature, Science, and Cell-family journals that investigate novel deep learning methods for scientific applications. We then use a language model to construct simple baselines for each task. These automatically constructed baselines are strong, recovering a median of 95.5% (mean 85.7%) of the gap between a trivial predictor and the score of the deep learning model. The median simple baseline matches or exceeds the performance of the deep learning model in 12 studies. We further examine the variation across scientific domains and publication venues and we characterize the effects of test-time compute and access to different levels of study context. As human peer review has struggled to keep up with the rapidly increasing volume of research articles in recent years, our results suggest that automated baseline construction is a practical and scalable check on deep learning contributions in the physical and life sciences.


### [4] [Bayesian Distribution System State Estimation with Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40728)
*Nan Lin, Peter Palensky, Pedro Vergara Barrios*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Medium-voltage feeders have few real-time measurements, so distribution system state estimation (DSSE) rests on pseudo-measurements of the secondary substations, a prior in Bayesian terms. Weighted least squares keeps the power flow exact but reduces this prior to a mean and variance, while supervised deep learning estimators also learn the power flow and are tied to one measurement set. We propose PoSE (posterior sampling for state estimation), which keeps the Bayesian structure of DSSE and learns only what the distribution system operator does not know, namely the distribution of substation injections, modelled by a neural network trained with flow matching on historical data. The power flow equations, grid topology and measurements enter only at sampling, through a noise-aware Kalman-type correction. The neural network therefore never approximates the power flow, but every posterior sample is a physically consistent operating point, and one trained prior serves any measurement set, noise level and topology. On the IEEE 33-bus feeder, PoSE lowers the probabilistic injection error (CRPS) by 6.7-7.5% against a full-covariance Gaussian prior in all 12 tested settings. It provides reliable uncertainty: the 90% intervals contain the true injection almost exactly as often as exact posterior samples would, whereas those of supervised estimators contain it in only 37-78% of cases. On the 240-bus unbalanced three-phase Iowa feeder, it gives the lowest injection, voltage and branch-current errors of all estimators. A fast variant matches this accuracy at 0.2-0.6s per snapshot and, without retraining, beats Gaussian priors and retrained supervised estimators on seven reconfigured topologies.


### [5] [Don't Just Impute, Detect: Task-Aware Diffusion Pipelines for Edge Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40732)
*Jeongsu Park, Taehyeon Yun, Geonwoo Kim, Yuji Lim, Moohong Min*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Industrial Internet of Things (IIoT) edge pipelines routinely lose contiguous blocks of multi-sensor data to network dropouts and hardware faults. The standard remedy is a disjointed "impute-then-detect" pipeline whose imputer is trained only for Mean Squared Error (MSE). Such an imputer fills a missing block with the most probable normal pattern: a partially observed anomaly is smoothed back toward normality, and boundary artifacts on normal data raise false alarms. We call this imputation bias and propose the Task-Aware Diffusion Refiner (TADR), a conditional diffusion refiner trained against the loss landscape of a frozen downstream detector. Because back-propagating through the sampling trajectory is intractable, the task-aware terms act on the one-step clean-window estimate that the noise prediction already yields, so training costs no more than plain diffusion training. Under strict Point-to-Point evaluation over five seeds with paired significance tests, TADR raises F1 by $0.338$ over the unrefined detector on SMD ($p = 0.0015$), and the anomaly enhancement objective alone contributes $0.463$ in ablation. We also characterise when the method helps: the gain tracks the continuous, mutually redundant channels available and the anomaly evidence the outage removes, and it is absent on telemetry datasets dominated by one-hot command channels and on PSM. At ten sampling steps TADR runs in $41.4$ ms per window on a single CPU core, sustaining about $24$ concurrent 1 Hz streams from a $4.03$ MB model. Code and per-seed results are available at this https URL .


### [6] [Unsupervised Feature Selection Using Local Intrinsic Dimensionality -- Extended Version](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40736)
*Oussama Chelly, Michael E. Houle, Ken-ichi Kawarabayashi, Muhammad Rajabinasab, Arthur Zimek*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: High dimensionality degrades both the efficiency and the effectiveness of learning algorithms, and in many applications no class labels are available to guide dimensionality reduction. This paper proposes LIDFS, an unsupervised feature selection method based on Local Intrinsic Dimensionality (LID). The method builds on the formal equivalence between LID and the indiscriminability of distance measures. The data matrix is transposed so that each feature is treated as a point, and the LID of each feature is estimated by maximum likelihood from the distances to its $k$ nearest neighboring features. Features with lower LID exhibit greater local discriminability and receive higher importance scores. A refinement step then retains one representative per feature neighborhood and demotes locally redundant features. The result is a complete ranking from which any number of features can be selected without rerunning the procedure. LIDFS does not use any information about labels or the number of classes, and runs in $\mathcal{O}(d^2 n)$ time for $n$ instances and $d$ features. LIDFS is compared with classic and state-of-the-art methods and a random baseline on 23 high-dimensional datasets, the classic benchmark collection. The evaluation uses classification accuracy and AUC with five downstream classifiers, as well as clustering accuracy and normalized mutual information, over both full and aggressive selection ranges. LIDFS ranks first in the supervised evaluations and first or tied second in the clustering-based evaluations. It belongs to the top statistical group together with methods that require the number of classes, and it runs faster than its closest competitor. Several recent methods, by contrast, fail to outperform the random baseline. A sensitivity analysis shows that small neighborhood sizes yield the best results.


### [7] [Certified Behavioral Retention for Continual Adaptation with Held-Out Anchors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40740)
*Ahanaf Hasan Ariq, Abrar Shahid*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Continual adaptation requires deciding not only how to update a model, but whether to commit an update without exceeding a declared budget for changes to protected behavior. We introduce Certified Anchor-Based Adaptation (CABA), a candidate-agnostic post-update gate that reserves historical observations from candidate optimization, compares old and candidate policies on frozen representations, and selects the largest tested interpolation coefficient whose worst-anchor forward KL divergence remains within budget. The finite check requires neither action labels nor complete trajectories. We develop a theory-led certification framework that separates measured anchor retention from conditional off-anchor guarantees. Under explicit representation-coverage and Lipschitz assumptions, an accepted update satisfies a uniform behavioral-divergence bound on protected support. The analysis gives regularity bounds for affine softmax heads, probabilistic coverage and compression guarantees, interpolation monotonicity conditions, grid-resolution bounds, and trajectory-level and sequential-composition guarantees. In a controlled UCI Human Activity Recognition study used as a non-stationary sensor-policy proxy, CABA reduces mean forgetting from 1.01 percent for unconstrained adaptation to 0.00 percent at budget epsilon = 0.05, while reducing final average accuracy from 93.04 percent to 88.48 percent. The experiment illustrates a retention-plasticity trade-off and does not establish foundation-model or robotic performance. CABA provides an auditable retention check for candidate generators exposing comparable policy distributions and a declared update path, while deployment also requires separate evidence of current-task utility.


### [8] [AutoHebb: Self-Modifying Neural Networks that Zero-Shot Infer and Continually Learn](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40757)
*Daniel Levine, David van Dijk, Kenneth Kay*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Humans and animals have a unique capacity to generalize and learn in novel circumstances, including "0-shot" inference (ZI) and continual learning (CL) -- two powerful cognitive abilities. Despite this importance, efforts to model ZI and CL are seriously limited in several ways. First, existing models typically lack components critical to ZI and CL in the brain, namely, strong inductive biases (for ZI) and autonomous control of learning (for CL). Second, models can be less tractable to analysis if model components are overly complex (lacking simplicity), or too task-restricted if model components are overly bespoke (lacking expressivity). Here we introduce autonomous Hebbian networks ("AutoHebb"), a class of online learning feedforward neural networks that realize strong inductive biases and autonomously controlled learning, and are both simple and expressive. Unlike standard models that learn via externally controlled optimization (e.g. gradient descent), AutoHebb networks combine external feedback with internally generated activity to produce a global learning signal ('neuromodulator') fed back to the network. The neuromodulator in turn gates a biologically grounded local learning rule (3-factor Hebbian rule) that updates networks' plastic weights. Using meta-learning to search for fixed (non-plastic) weights, we find that AutoHebb networks (1) succeed on ZI tasks, (2) show emergent (untrained) ZI/CL abilities, (3) admit reverse-engineering into explicit algorithms, and (4) outperform modern learning models on ZI/CL tasks. Lastly, performance comparisons to related models implied an essential role for three model components: meta-learning, Hebbian plasticity, and autonomous control of learning. These findings indicate that AutoHebb may be useful in hypothesis generation, analysis, and discovery of biologically grounded forms of learning and generalization.


### [9] [Frozen in Time: How a Physics Foundation Model Forgets Under Sim-to-Real Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40857)
*Haaris Mian, Miles Cranmer, Alessandro Favero, Payel Mukhopadhyay*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Scientific foundation models (SciFMs) have achieved remarkable performance on a wide range of PDEs, including the ability to efficiently adapt to unseen data distributions with additional finetuning. As with all neural architectures, finetuning such models comes with the risk of catastrophic forgetting, whereby adapting to new tasks degrades prior knowledge of the model. We study catastrophic forgetting in Walrus-1.3B, a SciFM for continuum dynamics, over the course of a two-stage sim-to-real adaptation: first finetuning on simulated flow around a cylinder and then adapting to experimental data of the same system. We find that finetuning on the simulations rapidly degrades rollout performance on the original pretraining datasets, with one-step errors increasing up to 35x after just 20 epochs. Subsequent adaptation to real data further erodes performance on the simulated task. We characterize the potential drivers of forgetting, such as dataset dependence and task representation similarity. We then show that replaying as little as 1% simulated data during real-data finetuning recovers most of the lost simulation performance while retaining effective adaptation to the experimental dynamics. Our results identify catastrophic forgetting as a practical obstacle to sim-to-real adaptation of SciFMs and show that lightweight replay strategies significantly improve retention.


### [10] [FARO: Function-Space Adaptive Return-to-Risk Optimization via Cross-Block Equal-Risk-Contribution Budget Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40858)
*Xiaojun Wu, Cehao Yang, Xuhui Jiang, Chengjin Xu, Jia Li, Jian Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Training a large language model is compute-bound, and current recipes divide the work between optimizers by hand: orthogonalized momentum for attention and MLP matrices, Adam-style updates for the rest. This division rests on human priors about which layer type deserves which geometry, and it fixes a single global update budget that every parameter block shares. We introduce FARO, a function-space adaptive optimizer that makes the partition of parameters into blocks the unit of decision. FARO gives each block a geometry by its realized counterfactual return, then freezes the routes and reallocates the frozen per-block budget by cross-block equal risk contribution (ERC), the risk-parity principle from portfolio theory. On a GPT-style model trained on FineWeb, the framework gives two results. First, its FARO-Adaptive arm reaches 3.2780 nats at the full schedule, matching NorMuonAndAdam (3.2794), the best hand-designed recipe: it recovers the human prior without hand-setting per-layer geometry. Second, FARO-ERC improves on that arm: it lowers the clean-data loss at every one of 6 seeds, by 0.0136 nats on average, a modest but robust gain. Its sign test, paired test, and confidence interval all agree, and it exceeds the measured run-to-run noise at no added time or memory. It works by trimming the riskiest blocks rather than expanding the safest, and it holds up under a corrupted stream, still lowering the mean loss and winning the seed majority. The source code is available at this https URL .


### [11] [Near-Optimal First-Order Regret for Bandits with Feedback Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40861)
*Mengxiao Zhang, Yuheng Zhang, Haipeng Luo, Paul Mineiro*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study first-order regret guarantees for adversarial bandits with a fixed, known directed feedback graph. For strongly observable graphs, [ACBDK15] establishes a minimax regret of $\widetilde{\Theta}(\sqrt{\alpha T})$, where $\alpha$ denotes the independence number of the graph and $T$ denotes the horizon. On the other hand, the best existing first-order bound scales as $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{(\kappa+1) L^\star})$ [LLZ20], where $\kappa$ denotes the clique partition number, satisfying $\kappa+1\geq\alpha$, and $L^\star$ denotes the cumulative loss of the best arm, leaving a gap between the graph dependence in worst-case and first-order guarantees. In this paper, we bridge this gap by obtaining an expected regret bound of $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{\alpha L^\star}+\alpha)$. Our first result is an algorithm for the special case of self-aware graphs, in which every vertex has a self-loop. The algorithm combines the update of EXP3 [ACBFS02] with a sampling rule inspired by exploration by optimization [LS20]. More specifically, we construct a map whose fixed points yield sampling distributions that control the one-round regret remainder in terms of the learner's loss and the graph's independence number, which is crucial in deriving optimal first-order guarantees. We further show that a sufficiently accurate fixed point can be computed in polynomial time by a geometrically convergent iteration. Finally, we extend the algorithm to general strongly observable graphs and achieve the same $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{\alpha L^\star}+\alpha)$ guarantee by adapting the two-level aggregation approach of [LLZ20].


### [12] [TensorSSM: Memorizing Spatial Position in Dynamic State Space for Tensor Core Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40871)
*Sheng Zhang, Xinyi Wang, Maolin Wang, Xiangyu Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: High-dimensional tensors are ubiquitous in real-world applications but lead to massive storage overhead. Functional tensor decomposition addresses this by employing neural networks to map element coordinates to tensor cores, significantly reducing the parameters for data compression. However, existing functional methods typically flatten high-order tensors into 1D sequences. This folding operation causes highly overlapping local coordinate sequences and struggles to capture complex cross-mode spatial dependencies, severely limiting their expressiveness on diverse datasets. To tackle these challenges, we propose TensorSSM, a novel Implicit Neural Representation (INR) for functional decomposition method that memorizes spatial positions within a dynamic State Space Model (SSM). Specifically, TensorSSM first decomposes mode-wise coordinates into sequences spit by K-ary rule, encoding spatial tokens from coarse to fine, which effectively captures spatial variations. Furthermore, we extend the SSM's state space to explicitly map cross-mode spatial dependencies, allowing inter-mode interactions to enhance the updates of each mode. By integrating cross-mode information from previous hidden states into the current state transitions, TensorSSM effectively expands tensor representation along two dimensions: the sequence dimension for coarse-to-fine spatial tokens, and the state dimension for cross-mode memorization. This design effectively integrates spatial information into the state space and the tensor core, substantially advancing the state adaptability and enhancing the expressiveness of the data compression. Extensive experiments demonstrate that TensorSSM achieves significantly high reconstruction accuracy and storage efficiency, exhibiting robust expressiveness across diverse tensor datasets without relying on strict spatial assumptions.


### [13] [TSFLab: A Self-Improving Agent-Agnostic Workbench for Time Series Forecasting Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40901)
*ChengAo Shen, Yanjun Zhao, Haoxin Liu, Bonnie Chen, Miles Ma, Dongjin Song, Hanghang Tong, Qingsong Wen, Jingrui He, Jingchao Ni*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: As publications and products on time series forecasting (TSF) proliferate, researchers face a growing burden of analyzing existing work. First, not all publications release code. For instance, only about 20% of arXiv TSF papers have a code link in the surveyed period. Second, released code also requires manual maintenance. Thus, TSF researchers must constantly adapt or implement existing works. However, independent implementations can differ in experimental settings and are difficult to maintain. We therefore propose to use AI agents for benchmarking TSF research and even supporting autoresearch, e.g., developing new TSF models. Yet directly applying existing coding agents, such as Claude Code and Codex, is suboptimal: prohibitive costs, limited scalability to large research projects (e.g., benchmarking), and lack of self-maintenance. To address these challenges, we propose TSFLab, a self-improving agent-agnostic "workbench" that equips compatible coding agents with shared resources and tools for low-cost TSF research and continuous maintenance of research resources. Our experiments cover reproduction, benchmarking, and new method development. In reproduction and benchmarking, TSFLab improves task completion by 20-163% and cuts fresh tokens per completed task by 11-66% across baselines. A pilot study of agent-driven method development also shows improved forecasting accuracy via TSFLab, with 32.8-73.6% fewer fresh tokens and 9.2-68.8% less development time than the other settings.


### [14] [No Channels Left Behind: Understanding Diffusion Acceleration through Residual Gates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40914)
*Junha Park, Jaemyung Yu, Jaehui Hwang, Jong-Seok Lee, Dongyoon Han*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Training acceleration methods for diffusion transformers, such as REPA and dispersive regularization, are commonly explained by additional supervision, spatial-structure priors, or improved feature geometry. We revisit this view and uncover a striking internal change: the AdaLN-Zero residual gate $\alpha$, which directly controls how conditioned updates enter the residual stream, is substantially reorganized by these methods. This observation motivates our hypothesis that acceleration arises, at least in part, from reshaping the residual gating pathway rather than solely from the feature structures explicitly imposed by auxiliary objectives. Supporting this view, accelerated models are markedly more sensitive to perturbations of $\alpha$, and their gate geometry reflects semantic relationships among classes. We further introduce the normalized participation ratio (nPR) to characterize the channel-wise organization of the gates and find that, across REPA teachers, projector architectures, and radial-objective targets, broader gate participation accompanies better generation quality. Guided by this insight, we propose a simple teacher-free radial objective that promotes broader gate participation through feature-norm regularization, without pairwise feature comparisons. On ImageNet $256\times256$ with SiT-B/2, our method improves FID over dispersive loss while increasing mean gate participation over timesteps and classes, further supporting the link between gate organization and training acceleration. Our results identify residual-gate reorganization as a measurable axis of diffusion training acceleration and suggest a simple direction for further acceleration.


### [15] [A Theoretical Framework for Understanding and Building "Any-task inference" Capable Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40934)
*Matthew McDermott, Florent Pollet, Payal Chandak, Gregory Kondas, Liat Friedman Antwarg, Noa Dagan, Isaac Kohane*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In this work, we provide a formal framework and mathematical definition for the subset of foundation models focused on enabling the property of "any-task inference" (ATI) (similar to zero-shot inference), rather than those designed for pre-training fine-tuning. To achieve this mathematical definition, we formalize the notion of a "space of tasks of interest," which we include in the problem domain, then provide an interface-based formal definition for ATI foundation models as those that map samples of data $x$ and tasks $t$ to predictions over the space of labels directly. While simple, this definition allows us to show that all ATI foundation models can be decomposed through a "universal task reduction" lens into encoder, model, and decoder aspects. We can leverage this decomposition to also decompose foundation model error into terms corresponding to different aspects of this reduction, stratified into a collection of "design terms," which are theoretical properties that can inform one of whether a particular proposed ATI foundation model can achieve optimality or not due to the structure of the proposed task reduction, and "realized model excess" terms which capture interactions between learned and programmatic components of foundation model systems. Lastly, we can use this perspective to offer two "prototypical" recipes for realizing ATI foundation models in diverse domains, which we term generative and discriminative variants of ATI foundation models. This exercise is grounded throughout by 5 real-world examples of foundation models in different settings, varying greatly in how these capabilities are realized, showing the applicability of this definition to real-world settings.


### [16] [Fair Recommender Systems for Intersecting Groups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40936)
*Christine Ling, Juba Ziani*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recommendation systems that optimize for the average user can fail to capture the preferences of diverse demographic groups. We consider a dynamic recommendation system, where arriving users can belong to different demographic groups. Importantly, these groups can intersect, and a user may belong to several groups. We first show that over time, any group-blind policy (which excludes all current-user group information) recommending $K \geq 1$ items incurs linear total regret under mild conditions. In turn, we introduce three group-aware algorithms based on the classical Upper Confidence Bound algorithm (UCB): i) Intersection-Fitting, which runs UCB on each intersection, but has a sublinear group- and intersection-level regret guarantee with an exponential dependence in the number of groups; and ii) two group-level heuristics, Inner-Outer UCB and Group-Item UCB which allocate different arms to different groups, and use a sleeping expert meta-learner to choose which active group's recommendation an intersection should inherit at any given time step. We provide experimental results on two datasets, Goodreads and BoardGameGeek, and highlight that our heuristics can be preferable to the intersection-fitting algorithm when intersection-level preferences are closely related to group-level preferences, but that intersection-fitting performs better when different intersections within a same group prefer different items.


### [17] [Kernel-based Interpolatory Methods for Context Compression of Attention Mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40943)
*Michel Fabrice Serret, Laura Grigori*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The bottleneck of attention mechanisms, which are the fundamental building block of modern language models, is their quadratic complexity in terms of context size. There has thus been an increasing interest in the compression of the context, also called KV-cache compression. In this article, we consider the problem of compressing the context of attention mechanisms by first formulating it in the Hilbert space associated with its kernel. In this formalism, attention can be represented as the ratio of two linear maps whose interpolant is also the ratio of interpolants, this allows us to consider the interpolation of the linear maps directly. We thus introduce two novel KV-cache compression methods which we call GAIM and Q-DAIM, based on the Generalized Empirical Interpolation Method (GEIM) and the Q-Discrete Empirical Interpolation Method (Q-DEIM), respectively. As the cost of these methods is quadratic in terms of context length, we propose surrogate models based on the Taylor series, the Performer and the Random Fourier Features method which allow the compression to be done in linear time. Finally, we extend this result to the streaming attention setting, providing new methods which allow one to penalize the selection of interpolation nodes based on the previously interpolated context and to update the previous interpolation based on new information. We then provide numerical results on the convergence of the residuals and the output error in future queries and preliminary results on the LongBench-E benchmark showing performance comparable to the state-of-the-art methods. These experiments suggest that the range revealed by our Q-DAIM method could help identify where information is concentrated in the KV cache, thus enabling an efficient compression.


### [18] [On the Origin and Evolution of Training-Inference Mismatch in LLM Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40945)
*Yu Li, Yufa Zhou, Tian Lan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: On-policy reinforcement learning for large language models assumes that training responses are sampled from the current policy. In practice, an inference engine samples the responses and a separate trainer computes the gradients, and the \textbf{training-inference mismatch} between the two makes the training data off-policy. The mismatch has two sources, the numerics of the engine and the stale weights of asynchronous sampling, and a correction that stabilizes training under one source can fail under the other. \textit{We ask which property of a mismatch source decides the correction it needs.} Evaluating the trainer at the sampler's weights splits each sampled-token error exactly into a \textit{numerical discrepancy} of the engine and a \textit{policy lag} of the trainer, and we follow both through training. The numerical discrepancy stays approximately constant, so it biases the updates by a nearly fixed amount, and centering the score at each prefix removes its leading bias. The policy lag is produced by the trainer's own updates: the bias it leaves enters the next update, which enlarges the next lag and closes a feedback loop. Importance weighting cuts this loop only while the lag ratio stays concentrated, and on Qwen3-1.7B it fails at the step where the effective sample size of the ratio falls below one half. From this account we derive the \textbf{discrepancy-lag correction} (DLC), which centers the numerical discrepancy, clips the lag ratio against the sampler's weights and resynchronizes the sampler when this effective sample size falls below one half. Under a stale sampler, DLC keeps the KL divergence from on-policy training within twice the KL between two independent on-policy trainings on Qwen3-0.6B and Qwen3-1.7B, and its resynchronization prevents collapses that the per-token corrections leave and that a KL measured on the sampler's prefixes does not detect.


### [19] [CAMEL: Cross-Architecture Masked Embedding Learning for Ultra-Lightweight Retinal Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40954)
*Andre Guarnier De Mitri, Giovani Decico Lucafo, Miller Matheus Lima Anacleto Rocha, Diego Furtado Silva*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recent progress in deep learning has substantially advanced automated grading of ophthalmologic pathologies, supported by large annotated datasets and improved representation learning. However, a significant gap remains between state-of-the-art performance and clinical scalability. In particular, foundation models have reshaped image-based medical screening and diagnosis. These large-scale architectures trained on extensive datasets learn rich, generalizable feature representations that can be adapted to a wide range of downstream tasks. In ophthalmology, RETFound, one of the most relevant foundation models for retinal images, performs strongly across classification, segmentation, and risk prediction tasks. Despite their effectiveness, foundation models are often computationally prohibitive, hindering their deployment in real-world clinical environments where hardware constraints and latency are critical. To bridge this gap, we introduce CAMEL (Cross-Architecture Masked Embedding Learning), a novel cross-architecture distillation framework that uses an asymmetric masking strategy during embedding learning to effectively transfer knowledge from massive teacher foundation models to compact, lightweight student models. Our experimental evaluation across four datasets shows that CAMEL preserves accuracy while drastically reducing computational complexity, enabling lightweight yet highly accurate models suitable for resource-limited clinical settings.


### [20] [Monte Carlo Random Walk Kernel](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40956)
*Arslan Iamaletdinov, Elfat Sabitov, Konstantin Sorokin*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We introduce the Monte Carlo Random Walk Kernel (MCRWK), an estimator whose per-pair cost is independent of graph size and derived directly from the random walk kernel (RWK) expansion. By normalizing the kernel coefficients, MCRWK samples a shared walk length and estimates its contribution using random walks on the input graphs. This gives a simple way to approximate RWK without constructing the product graph. For a fixed expected walk length and efficient neighbor sampling, each pairwise estimate takes $O(m)$ time for $m$ Monte Carlo samples, allowing the sample budget to control the trade-off between runtime and accuracy. We evaluate MCRWK against Graph Voyagers and classical RWK methods on synthetic and real graph datasets. In the reported experiments on large synthetic graphs, MCRWK is faster than Graph Voyagers and, at matched runtime, more accurate. It also has the lowest Gram-matrix runtime on the five real datasets studied. Code and experiments are available at this https URL


### [21] [RC Prediction: A Python Package for Parameter-Aware Reservoir Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40984)
*Jin-Cheng Rao, Zheng-Meng Zhai, Ying-Cheng Lai*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: A nonlinear dynamical system can be characterized by the autonomous evolution of its state in time and its dependence on system parameters. As a parameter varies, the dynamics can change quantitatively and, at bifurcation points, qualitatively. When the governing equations are unknown, a data-driven model should therefore capture both elements from observational data, rather than reproduce the dynamics at a single operating condition only. Parameter-aware reservoir computing (PARC) is a model-free framework for this purpose, where the parameter is supplied to a reservoir computer through an additional input channel. PARC is trained on time series recorded at several parameter values, and the trained reservoir, run in closed loop as an autonomous dynamical system, generates trajectories at parameter values that were never observed. rc_prediction is an open-source Python package that implements this workflow. It provides multi-parameter training of a reservoir, autonomous closed-loop prediction at user-specified parameter values, parameter scans, and a standard echo state network for forecasting at a fixed parameter. Hyperparameter optimization and optional GPU acceleration through PyTorch are also provided. We use three benchmark dynamical systems as examples to demonstrate its usage. As one of the featured applications, here we illustrate the prediction of critical transitions and system collapse: trained only on pre-critical data, the model is queried beyond the training range to predict whether the system collapses, to estimate the critical parameter $p_c^{*}$, and to characterize transient lifetimes.


### [22] [Steering Vectors as a Training Signal in LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40998)
*Tiejin Chen, Maunil Vyas, Huaiyuan Yao, Hua Wei*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Steering vectors (SVs) are residual-stream directions that shift the behavior of LLMs when added to hidden states at inference time. While prior work has mainly used SVs as post-hoc controls, we ask whether the same directions can also guide parameter updates during post-training. We study this question across supervised learning, including SFT and distillation, and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). Our framework considers two intervention regimes: a hidden-state hook during teacher-forced supervised training, and an advantage-repair mechanism on wrong rollouts in GRPO. For each regime, we analyze three design axes, which are the steering direction, its placement, and the operation used to combine it with training. In supervised settings, SV injection improves over plain SFT and distillation, with gains scaling with the off-policy gap and reaching up to a $12\%$ improvement when distilling from thinking-mode teachers. These gains hold across three random seeds. In RL settings, applying a correctness-contrast direction as advantage repair on wrong rollouts, including otherwise zero-gradient all-wrong groups, recovers a learning signal that transfers to held-out math benchmarks and acts as a composable auxiliary signal that adds to existing zero-variance remedies such as entropy-aware advantage shaping. We further identify three failure boundaries. In detail, code generation can break the direction, cross-family layer transfer can break the placement, and inference-time application on an SV-trained checkpoint can break the intervention regime. Together, these results establish steering vectors as a conditional but effective training signal for LLM post-training. Our code is available here.


### [23] [When Can Unlabeled Data Pools Replace Membership Queries?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41001)
*Dravyansh Sharma*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Membership queries let a learner label arbitrary points and give near-optimal $\tilde O(n\log(1/\eps))$ label complexity for halfspaces under every marginal. A pool learner may label only sampled points. We ask when unlabeled data can replace membership queries. For homogeneous halfspaces the answer depends only on the angular law of $X/\|X\|$. If this law assigns zero mass to every hyperplane through the origin and positive mass to every nonempty open set of directions together with its reflection, a pool learner uses $O(n\log^3n\log(n/(\eps\delta)))$ labels, uniformly over all such marginals and targets, without density, moment, concavity or isotropy assumptions. The same label bound holds for non-homogeneous halfspaces under every marginal with full support and no mass on affine hyperplanes, including Gaussians. Dropping either angular assumption admits $\Omega(1/\eps)$ pool versus $O(\log(1/\eps))$ membership-query separations, even with unlimited unlabeled data. The price is paid in unlabeled data: in dimension $n\ge3$, for every unlabeled budget fixed in advance, some marginal satisfying both assumptions forces $\Omega(1/\eps)$ labels. For isotropic $s$-concave marginals in dimension $n\ge3$, unlimited pools keep logarithmic label rates for every finite $s$. With an unlabeled budget fixed in advance, class-uniform polylogarithmic rates hold exactly for $s>-1/(n+1)$, or $s>-1/n$ under central symmetry, and in the general class the optimal rate between these thresholds is $\tilde\Theta(\eps^{-(n-2)/n})$. This extends the range $s\ge-1/(2n+3)$ of previous sample-based guarantees, in the realizable setting.


### [24] [MOSAIC: Addressing Disproportionate Task-Modality Dependencies via Modality-Partitioned Gradient Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41004)
*Aryan Chowdhury, Yizheng Sun, Thomas Carroll, Riza Batista-Navarro*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In multi-task settings, combining multiple modalities allows task representations to draw on signals that a single modality alone would not capture. However, individual tasks may depend disproportionately on particular modalities, a structure that conventional fusion-based architectures do not model explicitly, instead enforcing a unified representation space. Consequently, a task's gradients can affect parameters tied to modalities it barely relies on, leaving features that compromise between competing demands. Although prior work has explored task-weighting and loss-balancing strategies, their effectiveness is limited by the absence of explicit modality separation, preventing models from optimising each modality according to its task-specific importance. To address these limitations, we propose MOSAIC (MOdality-Separated Architecture with Inter-modal Cross-attention), which preserves unimodal representations while enabling controlled interaction between them, and uses modality-specific losses to route gradients to their corresponding partitions. On the Corpus of Everyday Japanese Conversation (CEJC), MOSAIC improves average macro-F1 by 7.4 points across four tasks with a 72% reduction in backbone parameters relative to a fusion-based baseline. Our results demonstrate that modality partitioning yields consistent gains across tasks while requiring far fewer backbone parameters than a fusion-based design.


### [25] [Towards Understanding Score-Seeking in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41014)
*Aghyad Deeb, Ryan Greenblatt, Alex Mallen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Production large language models sometimes reward-hack, exploiting flaws in how tasks are scored rather than performing the tasks as intended, sometimes in highly sophisticated and adversarial ways. We investigate the motivations driving this behavior in GPT 5.6 Sol, GPT 5.5, and a checkpoint of OpenAI o3 without safety training ("exp-rl-cap-late") by observing model behavior in agentic environments where the grader is leaked. In our environments, the models appear to be score-seeking: trying to score well according to graders that they believe assess their current actions, even at the expense of violating explicit user instructions. They appear to care about score itself rather than its downstream consequences, violating instructions at approximately the same rate whether the score is indicated to be used for training, for evaluation, or not at all. We observe evidence that the more recent models care to some extent about scores that do not assess their own current actions. GPT 5.6 Sol and GPT 5.5 help other agents 24.6 percentage points more often on average when the other agent stands to gain a score of 1 rather than 0 from being helped. However, when grading or monitoring another trajectory, we find models never collude by distorting its score.


### [26] [Do Language Models Know What Compelling Scientific Evidence Looks Like?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41015)
*Conor Igoe, Gabriel Grand, Zane Koch, Albert Bou, Eli Moss, Michaela M. Hinks, Samuel G. Rodriques, Andrew White, Siddharth Narayanan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Expert scientists excel at weighing empirical findings from multiple sources that vary in both statistical reliability and epistemic relevance. To what extent are language models, which are increasingly driving autonomous discovery systems, capable of such evidence deliberation? We introduce EDB-Bio, a scientific reasoning benchmark curated from real-world data in three biological domains: drug-perturbation transcriptomics, CRISPR screens, and human genome-wide association studies (GWAS). Each of EDB-Bio's 96k episodes presents a sequence of experimental results from published studies and asks the model to predict the probability that a target hypothesis is true. Out-of-the-box, open-weight and frontier models alike demonstrate systematic underconfidence and attain Brier scores comparable to a trivial base-rate forecaster. Post-training against strictly proper scoring rules significantly improves both calibration and discrimination. This calibration pays off downstream: in a sequential experiment selection task, our Ibis0 model is 2.5 times more efficient than the best frontier model. Throughout, quantifying evidence weights as Bayes factors reveals how models update their beliefs over time, whether they agree with expert biologists on relevance, and where they exhibit inconsistencies and biases. Our work extends calibration training and uncertainty analysis to a realistic scientific forecasting setting with applications to human cancer biology. We release all code, data, and trained models.


### [27] [Density-Guided Bootstrap Boundaries for Latent World-Model Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41022)
*Shashank Gupta, Pilhwa Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Latent world models allow reinforcement learning agents to learn policies from imagined trajectories, yet determining the optimal depth of model reliance remains a fundamental challenge. Fixed imagination horizons apply uniform prediction depths regardless of trajectory reliability, exposing target values to compounding model error or over-relying on critic approximations. We introduce density-guided bootstrap boundaries, a trajectory-dependent mechanism that adapts the depth of imagined returns using a score-informed relative density proxy. The proxy accumulates the estimated divergence of a score-corrected auxiliary field formed by a feature displacement network and a noise-conditioned score estimator along imagined paths. We propose two gating mechanisms: a hard boundary that halts return expansion at a discrete state, and a soft boundary that continuously shifts the return recursion toward critic bootstrapping. Both variants preserve boundary value estimates and apply corresponding survival weights to actor and critic losses. Formulated independently of specific dynamics models and instantiated within Dreamer V3, we prove that the resulting gated Bellman operator is a strict contraction mapping and that early bootstrapping monotonically reduces conditional target variance. We clarify the theoretical assumptions distinguishing our expansion proxy from exact path likelihoods. Across continuous-control tasks and Atari Pong, empirical evaluations show that dynamic boundary selection enhances sample efficiency and late-training performance over fixed-horizon and auxiliary-enabled baselines.


### [28] [Omniprediction for All Mixable Proper Losses and Beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41023)
*Junyan Liu, Haipeng Luo, Lillian J. Ratliff*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Omniprediction concerns the task of making predictions that are simultaneously accurate with respect to different losses. Earlier results by [CV09] show that in an online learning setting, $O(\log(|\mathcal{F}||\mathcal{L}|))$ regret is achievable simultaneously against every predictor in a finite predictor class $\mathcal{F}$ and for every loss in a finite class $\mathcal{L}$ of mixable proper losses, while recent advances [OKK25, FP26] show that $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{T\log(|\mathcal{F}||\mathcal{L}|)})$ regret is achievable simultaneously for every loss in a finite class $\mathcal{L}$ of bounded losses. In this work, we ask whether sublinear regret remains possible when $\mathcal{L}$ is an infinite class of proper losses. Our first result gives a negative answer in general: even when $\mathcal{F}$ contains only two linear predictors, it is impossible to achieve sublinear regret simultaneously for all bounded proper losses. We then show that $\widetilde{O}(T^{1/3}(\log|\mathcal{F}|)^{2/3})$ regret is achievable simultaneously for \textit{all} mixable proper losses and that this bound is optimal up to logarithmic factors. This optimal rate stands in stark contrast to the constant (in $T$) regret achievable for a finite class of mixable proper losses. Finally, we extend our algorithm to obtain a best-of-all-worlds result: in addition to the new regret guarantee for all mixable proper losses, the resulting algorithm simultaneously achieves the existing guarantees for finite loss classes. With appropriate choices of these finite classes, our algorithm simultaneously achieves constant regret for squared loss and log loss, $\widetilde{O}(T^{1/3}(\log|\mathcal{F}|)^{2/3})$ regret for all mixable proper losses, and $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{T\log|\mathcal{F}|})$ regret for $0$-$1$ loss, all Lipschitz losses, all GLM losses, and other structured families.


### [29] [When can Optimally Trained Transformers Achieve Near-Lossless Token Prediction for non-Markovian Data?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41038)
*Kağan Akman, Jake Feldman Starosta, Naci Saldi, Serdar Yüksel*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: There exists little mathematical theory explaining why Transformer architectures are empirically successful even for non-Markovian data. To this end, this paper studies next-token prediction via stochastic optimal control theoretically trained Transformers for such sources, where we consider either stationary and ergodic non-Markovian sources or those which are hidden Markov models of some finite order (possibly non-stationary). In either case, we first obtain error bounds via finite models with finite window approximations. For stationary ergodic sources, the resulting error is bounded by an entropy gap; for $N$th order Markovian sources, it decays at an exponential rate $\alpha^{\lfloor M/N\rfloor}$, where $M$ is the window length and $\alpha$ is determined by filter stability (and the Dobrushin coefficients of the transition and measurement kernels). We then construct a Transformer of depth $M+1$, one head per layer, realizing the truncated filter, and we optimally train it with stochastic control developed in earlier work, which rigorously returns a near optimal Transformer. In particular, we show that for a non-Markovian source, either under stationarity and ergodicity for general models or under filter stability for hidden Markov models, an optimally trained Transformer has arbitrarily small performance error with increasing context length, data, and approximation refinement. Numerical studies accompany the theory.


### [30] [Characterizing Recoverable Expert-Utilization Signatures in Fine-Tuned Mixture-of-Experts LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41039)
*Fei Wang, Baochun Li, Bo Li*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: As Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) large language models are increasingly deployed in distributed environments, from federated learning to edge-cloud systems, their architecture harbors an underexplored landscape of security vulnerabilities. Moving beyond textual analysis, we uncover structural memorization: a novel vulnerability where a fine-tuned model memorizes not only its training data but also the internal pathway used to process it, creating a characteristic routing fingerprint unique to the training process. We provide comprehensive empirical evidence that structural memorization emerges consistently even from a few samples, and that its intensity is directly influenced by the choice of trainable modules. We show that these memorized expert pathways can be stealthily recovered through the model's unconditional generation, using only routing and output logits, with no knowledge of the fine-tuning data or procedure. In distributed deployments, this vulnerability is amplified as routing information is exposed at multiple trust boundaries, including federated aggregation points, edge-cloud interfaces, and multi-tenant monitoring systems. Recovery exposes a critical blind spot: traditional membership inference metrics, viewed through the lens of expert routing, fail to identify memorized data corresponding to these structural pathways. To close this gap, we devise composite metrics combining reference model tail likelihood with complexity-aware normalization, significantly improving detection of memorized routing structures. This enables adversaries to pinpoint critical experts shaped by fine-tuning, whether dominant or dormant, and execute targeted attacks such as router manipulation or backdoor injection in realistic distributed settings where attackers lack training data but can observe routing patterns across system components.


### [31] [Before We Adapt: The Zero-Shot Generalization Bottleneck in Continual World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41041)
*Amir Mesbah, Di Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Continual reinforcement learning (CRL) requires agents to operate under environmental changes while reusing prior knowledge whenever possible. We argue that model-based reinforcement learning provides a promising framework for this setting because learned world models (WMs) can, in principle, support change detection, knowledge reuse, adaptation, and acquisition of new knowledge. However, such benefits depend heavily on whether the learned model can generalize beyond the contexts in which it was trained. In this work, we propose to formulate this problem with a contextual Markov decision process in which context is decomposed into behavioral and physical components. This view organizes continual WM learning into reuse, adaptation, and acquisition of new knowledge. As a first step, we study direct reuse when behavioral rules remain unchanged. Using Dyna-Q and TD-MPC in controlled FourRooms tasks, we find that zero-shot reuse can degrade substantially under physical-context changes and that this behavior depends strongly on the observation representation used by the WM. Additional ablation studies and latent-space diagnostics suggest that learned representations do not automatically provide the invariances needed for reuse. These results identify representation generalization as an important bottleneck for reliable world-model reuse in CRL.


### [32] [Localizing predictive concepts encoded in neural representations via hierarchical testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41046)
*Viktoriia Huryn, Marco Simnacher, Jim Berend, Wojciech Samek, Christoph Lippert, Sonja Greven, Uwe Ohler*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Understanding which regulatory sequence motifs a genomic deep learning model has learned, with statistical error guarantees, is essential for trusting its use in downstream tasks such as variant effect prediction. Existing approaches to identifying which units in a trained network encode human- understandable concepts fall into two groups. Exploratory methods rank and visualize units but offer no statistical guarantee that the selected units are associated with the prediction target across the population of inputs. Testing-based methods test only single units or one pre-defined set of them, and do not control the error across the many sets of units. We introduce CUTTIE (Cluster-tree Unit Top-down Testing of Independence with Error control), which clusters the units of each layer into a tree and tests the clusters top-down with conditional independence tests, localizing information about the target while controlling the family-wise error rate. Thereby, we identify clusters that carry information about the target beyond the rest of the layer. For the units in the identified clusters, conditional attribution maps then reveal what motifs they encode. In a controlled simulation, the procedure identifies the clusters whose concepts affect the target. We apply the procedure to a convolutional model predicting cell-type-specific chromatin accessibility from DNA sequence, where the concepts encoded in the identified clusters map onto known regulatory motifs.


### [33] [Communication-Efficient Private Federated Reinforcement Learning with Post-Quantum Authentication for Vehicular Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41055)
*Hojat Allah Salehi, Devrim Aras, Md Jueal Mia, M. Hadi Amini, Imtiaz Karim, Mashrur Chowdhury, Bhavani Thuraisingham*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Federated reinforcement learning supports cooperative vehicular routing without centralizing trajectory data, but model updates may reveal private information and incur communication overhead over intermittent vehicle-to-infrastructure links, particularly when post-quantum authentication is required. We study correlated private quantization, using complementary shared randomness between paired vehicles to reduce aggregation mean squared error while preserving the marginal parameter-level local differential privacy and one-bit communication cost of independent quantization. A tunable extension mixes shared and independent randomness to bound privacy leakage given paired outputs and shared-randomness side information. For this extension, Rényi differential privacy accounting establishes vector-level local differential privacy for one model upload even when pairings are revealed. Under matched vector-level privacy and identical local and global parameters, the extension yields 16.5% lower analytical aggregation mean squared error than independent quantization. We evaluate cooperative routing by coupling traffic simulation with packet-level simulation of post-quantum-authenticated vehicle-to-infrastructure communication. Compared to full-precision updates, one-bit uploads reduce authenticated uplink size by 92.4% and increase mean verified updates aggregated per round from 19.6 to 26.5. Under mobility-constrained communication at a parameter-level privacy budget of 1, correlated private quantization reduces mean travel time by 14.3% relative to independent quantization at the same budget and communication cost. At a parameter-level privacy budget of 0.3, a vehicle-origin inference attack trained on independent simulations reaches 30% accuracy with correlated private quantization and 22% with independent quantization after 100 rounds, versus 91% without privacy and 10% for random guessing.


### [34] [Nonlinear Maps Between Irreducible Representations in Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41059)
*Mani Shayestehfar*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Equivariant linear maps cannot mix non-isomorphic irreducible representations, whereas nonlinear activations can. We develop a degree-resolved version of this nonlinear interaction picture and use the resulting block inventory to construct Specht Triple, a residual on unordered graph triples. On a synthetic structural benchmark with a frozen GIN backbone, Specht Triple reduces aggregate standardised test MSE by 11.05% relative to a parameter-matched Projector Control using the same irreducible projectors and complete linear equivariant space. The results support irreducible decomposition as an inductive bias for nonlinear higher-order equivariant processing.


### [35] [bf16 Rounding Silently Discards Most RL Policy Updates: A Hidden Training-Inference Mismatch and an Unbiased One-Line Fix](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41073)
*Andrew Gao, William Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training of language models keeps fp32 master weights in the optimizer and serves rollouts from a bf16 copy produced by round-to-nearest conversion. At the learning rates in common use, most per-step updates are smaller than half a bf16 spacing and the conversion discards them: on Qwen3-1.7B only 0.4-0.5% of served weights change per step, and the policy that generates the training data lags the optimizer by 50-150 steps. Existing mismatch corrections cannot see this lag because they compare two copies downstream of the same rounding. We propose Fair Cast: export the master with an unbiased rounding rule (stochastic rounding or error feedback) and compute the training loss on the served copy. The change is one line at the export call, adds no hyperparameters and costs 22-38 ms per export of 1.7B parameters. Across 25 matched seed-pairs on Qwen3-0.6B to 4B with AdamW and Muon, Fair Cast is ahead on 18, tied on 6 and behind on 1, with a mean gain of 6.1 points; on zebra puzzles it reaches 0.57 +/- 0.16 over four runs where round-to-nearest collapses to 0.33 +/- 0.10. It also makes the saved fp32 checkpoint agree with the served model, and it exposes the implicit lag of round-to-nearest as a regularizer that can be restored explicitly when a task needs it.


### [36] [Where, When, and How Many Bits? Scaling Laws for SGD with Stochastic Rounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41074)
*Zhichao Wang, Michael W. Mahoney*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Low-precision training reduces computation but introduces additional rounding error. We theoretically study how stochastic rounding (SR) affects prediction risk as model size, training time, and precision change. We consider a two-layer linear network with a fixed random first layer, trained by mini-batch SGD on fresh Gaussian data with power-law spectra. At each SGD step, the features used in forward and backward computations are rounded independently. We prove matched upper and lower bounds on prediction error for the final model and for uniform parameter averaging, with general learning-rate and precision schedules chosen before training. For fixed precisions and constant learning rates with parameter averaging, we derive how the smallest prediction error decreases with compute, and we identify model sizes and training times that achieve this optimal-compute scaling. Rounding only the forward features preserves the compute scaling of exact arithmetic, while rounding backward features changes it. Averaging parameters can change which computation needs higher precision and when higher precision is useful. Our results also give bit requirements for a given accuracy tolerance and show how backward rounding can change learning-rate schedule design. At a fixed compute budget, lower precision can improve prediction when the benefit of a larger model or longer training outweighs the additional SR-induced error. Model simulations illustrate these tradeoffs, and experiments with an MLP and a small transformer examine rounding choices beyond the theoretical model.


### [37] [MUON-RT Reusing the Teacher's Trajectory for Momentum-Free Muon for LLM Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41104)
*Aryan Das, Vinay Kumar Verma, Aakansha Mishra*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Self-distillation methods for large language models with an exponential moving average (EMA) teacher already retain a history of student updates. Muon nevertheless stores a separate momentum buffer, increasing persistent training memory. We propose Muon with Reused Teacher (Muon-RT), which reuses the teacher-student displacement to eliminate momentum buffers for hidden weight matrices. We show that this displacement encodes an exponentially weighted history of applied updates and smooths temporally uncorrelated noise under a local model. Muon-RT removes its radial component, normalizes it spectrally, and mixes it with the current gradient after Newton-Schulz (NS) orthogonalization. Under explicit regularity and alignment assumptions, we establish linear contraction of an expected objective-gap bound toward a residual error level. With Self-Distillation Fine-Tuning (SDFT) on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Muon-RT improves average accuracy over AdamW by 2.6 points with 42% less persistent memory, and over momentum Muon by 5.4 points with 27% less persistent memory. With Self-Distillation Policy Optimization (SDPO) on Olmo-3-7B-Instruct, it improves average accuracy over AdamW by 2.0 points.


### [38] [TalentRL: Strategy-Guided Reinforcement Learning for Hierarchical Skill Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41143)
*Huancheng Chen, Xiaodi Sun, Zhaoqiong Huang, Michelle Chang, Jingwen Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Natural-language skills enable large language model (LLM) agents to reuse procedures across tasks, yet most systems retrieve individual skills from a flat SkillBank without explicitly modeling the high-level strategy that governs their selection and use. We introduce TalentRL, a strategy-guided reinforcement learning framework for hierarchical routing over procedural memory. Coarse-grained talents represent recurring strategies for skill use, while fine-grained skills provide executable procedures. For each task, the policy model selects a talent that guides execution and softly prioritizes its associated skills during similarity-based retrieval. However, since task outcomes depend jointly on talent selection, retrieved skill context, and downstream execution, terminal rewards alone cannot isolate the contribution of the routing decision. TalentRL therefore trains talent routing with reward contrasts between rollouts of the same task under matched interventions on the talent-conditioned context, and optimizes downstream execution with group-relative advantages. Across seven open-domain and multi-hop question-answering benchmarks, talent-aware supervised fine-tuning improves average success rate by 7.53%, 6.71%, and 9.21% over the corresponding zero-shot baselines for Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen3-8B, and Llama-3.1-8B, respectively. Reinforcement learning further improves these SFT checkpoints by 7.58% to 9.32%, and TalentRL achieves the highest average success rate among all compared methods on all three backbones. On the agentic benchmarks ALFWorld and WebShop, TalentRL achieves the highest ALFWorld success rate (99.3%) and the highest WebShop score (90.7) among all compared methods.


### [39] [CAP: Learning to Optimize from Sparse Observations of Related Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41173)
*Lin Fu, Siwei Fu, Dong Ni*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In experiment-driven black-box optimization, each evaluation can consume materials, equipment time, and other resources, limiting the number of experiments available for a new task. Records from completed related optimization campaigns could help, but learning a sequential query policy remains challenging when task-specific experience consists of only a few sparse histories. We propose CAP (Candidate Acquisition Policy), which models shared structure and task-specific variation in fixed historical records to sample interactive practice functions. On these functions, CAP learns a feedback-conditioned query policy aimed at final optimization performance under a fixed evaluation budget. At deployment, its parameters remain fixed while each new observation informs the next query. Across five synthetic task families spanning 6 and 20 dimensions and two engineering simulation benchmarks, BOPTEST and SU2, CAP attains the lowest median final-budget value among the reported methods on four synthetic families and both engineering benchmarks. These results indicate that sparse records from completed related tasks can support adaptive query decisions on new tasks, making more effective use of limited, costly experimental evaluations.


### [40] [General Frequency Principle for Linear Approximation Classes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41179)
*Yujun Wang, Yiqi Gu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In this paper, we study a general frequency principle under gradient descent training for the finite linear approximation classes. Under suitable assumptions, we prove that the learner amplitude relative to the target amplitude vanishes at high frequencies, uniformly throughout training. We also establish sufficient conditions for low-frequency priority, covering single-frequency targets trained separately and two frequency components trained in the same run. This general frequency principle does not imply that lower frequencies always converge faster. Instead, for two frequencies that satisfy certain comparison conditions, one component will take longer to learn than the other; and in common cases, the longer component has higher frequency. Numerical experiments with Legendre and trigonometric bases validate the theoretical results.


### [41] [Role-Aware Server Optimization for Multimodal Federated Learning with Missing Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41193)
*Liuyin Shi, Wenbo He*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Multimodal federated learning (MFL) trains a shared model by aggregating client updates without exchanging raw data. Under missing modalities, clients adapt active modality encoders and a shared prediction block (fusion and classifier) to different input subsets. This raises two server-side questions: which clients should contribute to each component, and how should their aggregates be incorporated across rounds? We observe that an absent-modality encoder can still adapt through missing-input handling. Availability- based aggregation can therefore omit its update while retaining jointly learned prediction-block updates. Contributor selection also shapes server momentum, a weighted history of aggregated updates. We observe that prediction-block momentum can improve or reduce performance depending on the encoder update policy. We therefore propose Role-Aware Server Optimization (RASO), which coordinates contributor selection with role-specific server updates. RASO selects contributors through prediction dependence and inclusion in local optimization, retains encoder momentum, and synchronizes the shared prediction block to its current aggregate. Experiments across datasets and fusion architectures demonstrate the effectiveness of RASO under missing modalities.


### [42] [Beyond Scarcity: When Encoder Adaptation Is Worthwhile](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41213)
*Zexing Zhang, Jichao Li, Tianyang Lei, Yiyin Zhang, Kewei Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Long-tailed recognition often learns a shared representation before correcting the classifier for class imbalance. Once the classifier has been rebalanced, however, final accuracy does not reveal whether further encoder adaptation remains worthwhile. Standard imbalance benchmarks obscure this question because they jointly vary the number of distinct examples available to rare classes and the frequency with which those examples shape the representation. We introduce a controlled evaluation framework that separates unique support from representation allocation and estimates the incremental value of encoder updates through matched classifier-only and full-network continuations from a common corrected model. The framework combines stage-specific controls, multiple representation and adaptation objectives, whole-distribution metrics, and hierarchical inference over class assignments, support reconstructions, and optimization runs. Across the controlled study, the same low-support regime moves from one in which classifier-only training is adequate to one in which encoder adaptation yields a practically meaningful benefit solely as representation allocation becomes more imbalanced. The transition persists across classifier corrections, representation objectives, normalization and augmentation controls, and ImageNet-scale evaluation, while preserving frequent-class accuracy. Under the prespecified one-percentage-point operating margin, the two regimes are separated by simultaneous confidence bounds. These results show that benchmark construction can alter a post-correction training decision and that support size alone does not characterize the remaining value of representation adaptation.


### [43] [A Positional Realizability Theory of Length Generalization in Single-Head Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41218)
*Jiasong Han, Yunkai Chai, Jintao Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Why does length generalization (LG) depend so sharply on the positional encoding (PE)? Relative schemes extrapolate fixed offsets, absolute schemes fix positions, and neither handles growing offsets. We give a realizability theory for a deliberately controlled abstraction, a single positional-only attention head, that derives this well-documented empirical pattern and says precisely where it holds and breaks. The device is a simulation transfer operator $T_L$ that re-evaluates a selected length-$L_0$-realizing positional kernel on the longer grid, with a scalar $\alpha_L \in [0,1]$ read off before any training from $(\Phi, t_n)$ alone. Here $\alpha_L$ serves as the computable witness of that taxonomy's derivation, not as a stronger empirical predictor. Our results are a relative-head deep-row characterization for kernels with a strict unique global maximizer (Theorem 2), an absolute/relative duality that derives the pattern (Proposition 4), a learned-absolute underdetermination result, and a static expressivity bound; a sufficiency theorem (Theorem 1) bridges $\alpha_L$ to LG, and the general deterministic case is a precise open conjecture (correcting a tempting but invalid "outside-colspan" argument). Experiments confirm the structure: in the controlled single-head regime $\alpha_L$ exactly separates the taxonomy's generalizing and non-generalizing (PE, task) cells (2625 trained-model measurements; stable at vocab 64/d=128). Together, these results turn the received PE taxonomy into a positional-kernel transfer theorem, and identify the content and depth mechanisms that escape it. The theory concerns the positional channel alone: tasks whose targets depend on token content, including the standard length-generalization suite such as addition and sorting, fall outside Theorem 2.


### [44] [Corroborative Learning: Mutual Enhancement through Task-Response Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41222)
*Zexing Zhang, Jichao Li, Tianyang Lei, Yiyin Zhang, Kewei Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In pattern analysis, models trained independently for the same task often develop complementary capabilities through differences in architecture, data, and optimization. Exploiting complementarity while retaining independent deployment is central to collaborative learning. Most methods establish transfer by reducing differences in predictions, relations, or representations between target and collaborator, although these differences also underlie complementarity. This creates a structural trade-off between reducing differences for knowledge exchange and preserving them for complementarity. We therefore propose Corroborative Learning (CL). Each model forms task-driven responses, with cross-group support from its collaborator. Source-excluded reconstruction of response increments from collaborator representations separates fitting and evaluation across disjoint groups and selectively constrains directions by cross-group reconstructability. The process requires no direct output or feature matching or additional inference dependency. Furthermore, with linear reconstruction and fixed collaborator representations, the loss equals a positive semidefinite directional regularizer, constraining less reconstructable directions more strongly. Signal and noise conditions characterize when directional shrinkage reduces response-estimation error. Across six language tasks, 24 models, and 36 pairs, CL improves over capacity-matched independent adaptation on every task, with positive gains across most directed model combinations. Pairwise comparisons show broad advantages over transfer and mutual-learning methods and different gains from complementarity across collaboration rules. Low-label language and vision results support cross-domain applicability. Task-relevant differences can support mutual enhancement without eliminating disagreement.


### [45] [Generative SDEs with Trainable Diffusion and Density-Based Losses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41243)
*Angxiu Ni*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We develop a divergence-kernel method for jointly optimizing neural SDE drift and diffusion fields and the initial Gaussian distribution, using losses that depend directly on the terminal density. In two- and eight-dimensional Gaussian-mixture experiments, we learn time-independent fields and obtain far fewer off-mode samples than score-based generators with similar parameter counts. The drift attracts samples toward the modes, while the diffusion helps disperse excess probability between them.


### [46] [A Unified Framework for Probability Estimation of Data-Dependent Events](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41244)
*Vitaly Feldman, Satyen Kale, Vinod Raman, Kunal Talwar, Ambuj Tewari*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study the general problem of estimating $D(\dde(S))$, the probability that an unknown distribution $D$ assigns to an event determined by a sample $S$ of $n$ i.i.d.\ draws from $D$ through a known mapping $\dde$. The estimation algorithm knows $\dde$ and $S$ but does not have access to any additional samples from $D$. This formulation captures classical missing mass, the data-dependent targets of adaptive data analysis and post-selection inference, and the test error of a data-trained predictor, whose estimation was recently studied by \new{Gastpar et al.\ (2024a,b)}. Within this framework we examine the two classical approaches to estimation. The error of the empirical plug-in estimator is exactly a generalization gap, so generalization bounds transfer to it directly. The leave-one-out estimator, which recovers the Good--Turing estimator, is accurate for every mapping that is stable under the deletion of one sample point, including new structured examples such as neighborhoods of the sample and pierced sets. The main application of our framework is to error estimation for a predictor that combines a base classifier with memorized exceptions, a structure common in theory and in practice. For it, the test error is exactly the missing mass restricted to the error region of the base classifier, a region selected by the same sample. We formulate the general problem of \emph{adaptive missing mass estimation} to address such dependence and propose a natural hybrid estimator, the Good--Turing estimator restricted to the selected region. We show that the generalization of the base classifier alone does not guarantee the accuracy of this estimator, and derive two bounds on its error: the first one based on the VC dimension of possible error regions and another based on the mutual information of the selected region with the sample.


### [47] [SPT-Flow: One Integration, Many Futures in Continuous-Time World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41273)
*Mingyang Lyu, Yinqian Sun, Gongce Cao, Yuhao Zhang, Lu Jia, Qian Zhang, Bo Wang, Pin Lyu*

Main category: cs.LG
