Across 14 pairs of base and derived models, from 1B to 7B parameters, we fitted a fixed bonus or penalty for each token (a vocabulary bias), alone or together with a temperature. On text held out from correction fitting, the largest top-4K bias recovery estimate is under 10%, with loss conditional on the retained vocabulary. Combining the bias with a temperature recovers up to 36%. Models further pretrained on math lose the most and recover almost nothing. Finally, in a controlled simulation where a model is damaged at random, with no planted bias or temperature change, the bias-plus-temperature fix still recovers 29%. Most of the real-model loss increase stays out of reach of our restricted fits.


### [99] [When Does Jacobian Fine-Tuning Stabilize Neural PDE Emulators?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42013)
*Shreeyam Bangera, Manya Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Neural emulators of chaotic PDEs are trained on one-step prediction but deployed autoregressively, where their errors compound. Jacobian-based regularisers aim to curb this growth, but in a chaotic system part of the growth is physical and cannot be trained away. We separate the two sources of this error with a first-order decomposition. We compare the emulator's error with a counterfactual that propagates the same one-step mistakes through the solver's Jacobian, giving an amplification ratio: the factor by which the model's own dynamics amplify its errors. We study 21 configurations across four PDEs, five architectures and two model sizes (8-10k and 33k parameters), with errors measured at one Lyapunov time. Jacobian fine-tuning reduced the error by more than 10% for 1 of 34 seeds whose amplification ratio was at most 1.25, and for all 20 seeds whose ratio was at least four; for the latter, the error fell by a factor of 8 (small models) and 55 (33k models) in geometric mean over seeds. The gain approximately equals the one-step accuracy cost of fine-tuning divided by the amplification ratio. Matching the solver's Jacobian along the model's own error direction (EDJM) outperformed random directions at the same cost on 28 of 35 seeds; input-noise injection performed comparably to EDJM.


### [100] [Know When the Equation Is Wrong: Structural Uncertainty for LLM-Guided Symbolic Regression under Extrapolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42024)
*Mostafa Cham, Bayu Adhi Tama, Jianwu Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can help discover mathematical equations from data, but most LLM-guided symbolic regression methods focus on a single best equation without quantifying uncertainty over alternative functional forms. This is challenging because distinct equations can fit the observed data similarly while producing substantially different extrapolations. Our experiments on the LLM-SRBench dataset show that standard conformal intervals achieve nominal in-distribution coverage but frequently fail under extrapolation. To tackle this challenge, we introduce LLM-SR-UQ, a framework that retains competing equation structures and uses their disagreement to characterize uncertainty about the underlying functional form. We extend the standard fixed-width conformal approach to quantify structural intervals, which measure disagreement among candidate equations and identify where prediction intervals should widen. Extensive experiments show our framework substantially improves coverage attainment by incorporating structural disagreement while preserving in-distribution coverage. Our approach also shows good generalizability when it is applied to other symbolic-regression methods.


### [101] [GhostPrefix: Synchronization-Free Universal Prefix Attacks for ASR Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42026)
*Shengding Liu, Ashwin Innuganti, Qiben Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We present GhostPrefix, the first synchronization-free universal adversarial prefix attack against automatic speech recognition (ASR) systems. Unlike prior audio attacks, which are typically crafted per utterance, require synchronization with the victim waveform, or target narrow objectives, GhostPrefix learns a short, reusable audio segment prepended to arbitrary live speech to exert real-time control over ASR outputs without knowledge of the future utterance or temporal alignment. A key challenge is maintaining stable adversarial effects across unknown, evolving speech contexts. GhostPrefix combines token-level losses with a novel representation-level objective for stable, input-agnostic effects. GhostPrefix supports three attack modes: (i) prompted prefix attacks for semantic steering, (ii) targeted prefix attacks for transcript hijacking, and (iii) untargeted prefix attacks for transcription degradation. To remain perceptually inconspicuous, we regularize prefixes toward benign environmental-sound distributions and improve robustness against over-the-air distortions. We evaluate GhostPrefix across 13 open-source ASR models from four families. In digital settings, it achieves average attack success rates of 97.6% for prompted steering and 93.4% for targeted hijacking, while driving word error rate to 1.18 under untargeted attacks. Over the air, it achieves success rates of 91.5% and 89.5% for prompted and targeted modes, respectively. These manipulations propagate into downstream AI-assisted pipelines, including voice assistants and AI note-taking tools, causing systematic semantic misinterpretations. GhostPrefix exposes synchronization-free universal prefixes as a practical, under-explored threat to real-world ASR and downstream applications. Artifacts and demos are available at this https URL .


### [102] [CausalXRL: Explainable Reinforcement Learning through Causal Graph Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42028)
*Yanming Zhang, Eric Papenhausen, Klaus Mueller*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Reinforcement learning is a powerful paradigm for training autonomous agents and has achieved impressive performance in complex environments. However, this success often comes at the cost of interpretability, diminishing trust and complicating efforts to debug and improve agent behavior. To address these challenges, we introduce CausalXRL, a novel framework for explainable reinforcement learning (XRL). A key feature of CausalXRL is its use of causal graph reasoning, which provides transparent, structured, multi-level explanations of agent decision-making. We validate CausalXRL through comprehensive case studies and a two-part evaluation: (1) a quantitative analysis of explanation fidelity and causal-structure learning efficiency in benchmark RL environments, and (2) a qualitative expert study assessing explainability in the real-time strategy (RTS) benchmark MicroRTS. The quantitative results show that CausalXRL can provide faithful explanations while efficiently learning causal structures, and the qualitative expert study suggests that participants found CausalXRL useful for inspecting high-level RTS strategies.


### [103] [There Is No Free Loop: When Does Looping a Trained Model Pay for Its Inference Compute?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42030)
*Rhea Sanjay Sukthanker, Pashmina Cameron*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Looping re-applies a block of layers so that each token passes through the block more than once. Adding a loop to a trained model is attractive because it leaves the parameter count, and hence the disk size, the memory footprint and the training cost unchanged. Unfortunately, every generated token pays for the loop. Re-applying m of N layers makes each token about (N+m)/N times as expensive. Loops that a model learns during large-scale pretraining justify this price, but whether loops added after pretraining justify this cost remains unclear. We compare a looped model with two simple baselines. We first compare it with the same model trained identically without the loop (equal parameters). Secondly, we compare it with the best unlooped use of the same inference floating-point operations (equal FLOPs). Across Qwen3 and Qwen3.5 models from 0.8B to 9B parameters, only the looped 0.8B model beats both baselines. At 4B and 9B, the looped model does not beat the first baseline, i.e. the same model trained without the loop. This is not because the benchmarks are too easy, since on the same benchmarks the unlooped 9B model still scores up to 13 points higher than the 4B model, showing that there is ample room for improvement. Introducing loops in an already pretrained model therefore only pays for small models with a fixed parameter budget.


### [104] [A richness-sensitive Acoustic Biodiversity Index from species-level detections: classifier benchmarking, false-positive control and validation against standard soundscape indices in an African savanna and an urban zoo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42042)
*Muhammad Saeed, Dmitry Mikhaylov, Mohamed H. Alhosani, Yasser F. Al Wahedi, Rory Pilossof, Ahmed Arabi, Walid Shaaba*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Acoustic indices are widely used as rapid proxies for biodiversity, but they summarise the distribution of sound energy rather than the species producing it and often track richness poorly. We revise and test an Acoustic Biodiversity Index (ABI) built from species-level detections. Its primary form, ABI_E, is Shannon diversity of detections normalised by the regional species pool expected at the site and season (H / ln S_ref); temporal stability T is reported as a separate component. We first benchmarked four classifiers on a strongly labelled tropical dataset (1,216 calls, 24 classes) with recording-grouped cross-validation. BirdNET embeddings + logistic regression performed best (macro-F1 0.750 +/- 0.029), and random frame-level splits inflated accuracy from 0.41 to 0.70. We then applied BirdNET v2.4 with a location and season filter to recordings from a riverine savanna in the Zambezi Valley, Zimbabwe, and from three habitats at Emirates Park Zoo and Resort, Abu Dhabi, United Arab Emirates. A stricter post-hoc location filter removed 517 high-confidence detections of 9 implausible species, all at the zoo. After diel-matched rarefaction to equal effort (6 h), the savanna site held 21 species (Hill number 1D = 9.83; ABI_E = 0.38) against 1.3-13 species (ABI_E = 0.03-0.21) at the zoo sites. Across 149 recorded hours, ABI_E correlated strongly with richness (Spearman rho = 0.93), whereas six standard indices did not (best: ACI, rho = 0.48). The stability term was lower at the savanna site (0.39) than at the zoo (0.65-0.67), showing that it penalises the natural diel cycle of bird activity. Species-level indices with explicit false-positive control, a declared regional reference pool and effort standardisation provide a more interpretable biodiversity indicator than energy-based acoustic indices.


### [105] [Differentially Private Synthetic Data from Sliced Chebyshev Moments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42081)
*Lucas Rosenblatt, R. Teal Witter, Christopher Musco*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Research on practical differentially private (DP) synthetic data has largely focused on methods that approximately preserve low-dimensional marginals of a target distribution, and can thus only hope to preserve higher-dimensional, global structure as a lucky by-product. At the same time, existing methods that target global accuracy guarantees pay a price exponential in the data dimension, $d$. In this work, we address this ``curse of dimensionality'' by presenting a new DP synthetic data algorithm, SPLICE, that comes with a compelling global distributional guarantee, even in high-dimensions, and moreover, is practically state-of-the-art. Our algorithm is based on measuring noisy sliced Chebyshev polynomial moments of the target distribution and fitting a distribution to match, a twist on recent polynomial methods for 1-D synthetic data. We prove that, given a dataset of $n$ examples, SPLICE returns a private distribution with Sliced Wasserstein-1 distance $\tilde{O}(1/\sqrt{n})$ from the target, for constant privacy parameters. Notably, this bound is independent of the data dimension, $d$. Along the way, we prove a moment matching result of independent interest: for a distribution on the unit ball, matching the first $O(1/\alpha)$ Chebyshev moments along $\tilde{O}(d/\alpha^2)$ random directions guarantees $\alpha$ Sliced Wasserstein error. Finally, we provide a practical implementation of the algorithm that borrows tools from the tensor decomposition literature and achieves state-of-the-art performance compared to prior DP synthetic data methods on an extensive benchmark.


### [106] [Cost-Aware Stopping for Freeze-Thaw Bayesian Optimization: When Does One-Step Lookahead Suffice?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42085)
*Long Vu, Jongseong Brad Choi, Binoy Ravindran, Zhoulai Fu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Freeze-thaw Bayesian optimization (BO) evaluates candidates in resumable increments but usually stops at a budget or convergence test. We maximize a budget-free utility, the best observed score minus a cost per increment, with a one-step lookahead: advance the candidate with the largest expected improvement (EI) under a fixed surrogate, and stop once no EI exceeds the cost. This rule, EI stopping, never falls below stopping immediately in expected utility, yet it can stop too early. We construct a certificate, a lower bound on the value of two-step lookahead, that proves such a stop premature, and repeating this correction never lowers expected utility under the surrogate. Under the surrogate, EI stops are premature on average at low costs and lose little at high costs. On three hyperparameter optimization benchmarks, a pre-registered test finds EI stopping noninferior to the correction in most high-cost settings, while the correction gains at low costs on two of the three; more information sharing in the surrogate raises EI stopping's utility. On the surrogates of two published methods, EI stopping trades lower scores for fewer training increments. EI stopping is a simple cost-aware reference whose utility depends on the surrogate.


### [107] [Towards Understanding the Impact of Plasticity Loss on Reinforcement Learning in Stochastic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42100)
*Philipp Bordne, André Biedenkapp*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Deep reinforcement learning (RL) is a powerful framework for sequential decision-making. Despite many high-profile successes in recent years, deep RL is known to be brittle. An emerging line of research attributes this brittleness to a loss of plasticity in neural networks. However, plasticity loss has so far been understudied in stochastic environments. We study this interplay systematically, training SAC agents on three DeepMind Control tasks augmented with controlled levels of observation and action noise. We find that plasticity-preserving methods increase learning robustness on the two harder tasks, with their advantage growing as observation noise increases, while combining resets with layer normalization underperforms resets alone at every observation noise level tested. Further, stochasticity can accelerate the loss of plasticity in harder environments, whereas in easier settings noise can act as a regularizer that sustains plasticity. Lastly, plasticity loss is most pronounced in the early stages of training. In the most challenging stochastic settings, TD instability rather than gradual plasticity loss becomes the dominant failure mode.


### [108] [Hyperbolic Inverse Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42104)
*Iulia Rusu, Anoop Praturu, Sergei Gepshtein, Tatyana Sharpee*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) seeks to infer the reward function underlying observed behavior, but its predictive accuracy and interpretability depend on how that reward is computed from the underlying features. While prior IRL reward functions have been implemented through a variety of techniques including linear weights, neural networks, or other function approximators, none make assumptions about the geometry of the underlying feature space. Inspired by geometric organization observed in neural circuits, we introduce Hyperbolic inverse reinforcement learning (Hyperbolic IRL), which parameterizes reward as a function of Hyperbolic distance between the feature vector and a learned parameter vector, as well as its Euclidean distance variant. We tested our methods against a range of linear and nonlinear IRL baselines under both deterministic and stochastic policies. Our Hyperbolic approach achieved comparable performance to state-of-the-art models on established benchmarks and excelled in the low data limit and across dense environments. Applied to human navigation among multiple exhibits in a museum room, Hyperbolic IRL reveals reward structure that depends not only on individual exhibit-distance features, but also on non-separable conjunctions between them. Together, these results demonstrate that a Hyperbolic reward parameterization can improve both the predictive accuracy and interpretability of IRL, providing a framework for studying how non-separable, conjunctive structure can begin to explain behavior.


### [109] [Fast Privacy Loss Accounting for Random Allocation via Multiscale FFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42117)
*Vitaly Feldman, Moshe Shenfeld*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In the random allocation sampling scheme each user's data is used in $k$ steps chosen randomly and uniformly from a sequence of $t$ steps. This scheme is known to improve on Poisson sampling in private optimization and in communication-efficient aggregation. The existing privacy loss distribution (PLD) accountant for this scheme runs in time quadratic in the inverse of the accuracy parameter $\alpha$ (Feldman and Shenfeld, 2026). We give an algorithm, \emph{multiscale FFT}, that represents likelihood ratios on a grid that is uniform within each dyadic interval and computes the exact rounding of every pairwise sum using FFTs within each interval. It keeps the guarantees of the accountant and improves the dependence of its running time on $\alpha$ from $O(\alpha^{-2})$ to $O(\alpha^{-1}\log(1/\alpha))$. In addition, we demonstrate that rounding can be performed via the mean-preserving connect-the-dots projection of (Doroshenko et al., 2026). This reduces the errors that rounding introduces in the mean of the privacy loss and in the $\chi^2$ divergence of the computed pair from the order of $\alpha$ to $O(\alpha^2/\log(t))$. In our experiments, our algorithm achieves speedups of several orders of magnitude at high accuracy and gives improved privacy bounds for DP-SGD with PREAMBLE.


### [110] [Gradient Descent on Two ReLU Neurons: Global Landscape and Bifurcation Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42118)
*Binghua Li, Mengzhe Li, Denny Wu, Tianhao Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Understanding how gradient descent learns features remains a central challenge in neural network theory. We study this question in a minimal multi-index setting that already exhibits rich multi-phase gradient dynamics: a well-specified two-neuron ReLU teacher-student model under isotropic Gaussian inputs, trained by population gradient flow from small initialization. We show that the dynamics are organized by two directions: the "easy" bisector direction, which carries the leading signal, and the "hard" splitting direction, which governs specialization. To characterize the loss structure and learning behavior, we analyze the population squared-loss landscape and show that every critical point with both student weights nonzero is either a global minimum or a saddle. We then track the gradient-flow trajectory, showing that student neurons first collapse toward a bisector saddle before escaping and specializing to teacher neurons. Our results provide both a landscape and dynamics account of the multi-phase symmetry-breaking behavior that arises in a simple multi-index model and under standard algorithmic and architectural choices.


### [111] [Physiological Trajectories and Observation Patterns in ICU Mortality Prediction: Transportability Under Changing Measurement Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42126)
*Benjamin T. Pike*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Objective: To distinguish the transferable predictive value of physiological trajectories from that of observation patterns in ICU mortality prediction, and to assess whether measurement timing affects performance independently of observation quantity. Materials and Methods: In-hospital mortality was predicted at 48 hours using MIMIC-III, MIMIC-IV, and eICU. Nested representations progressively integrated latest-value snapshots (R1), longitudinal physiological summaries (R2), and measurement frequency and timing features (R3). Models fitted on 7,762 MetaVision patients were evaluated on 21,682 MIMIC-IV patients and 43,878 eICU patients across 204 hospitals. The effect of measurement timing was assessed while holding the number of retained observations constant. Results: Physiological trajectories improved external prediction in eICU (R2-R1 log-loss difference -0.00922, 95% CI -0.01148 to -0.00704). Observation-pattern features improved locally developed eICU models (R3-R2 -0.00257, 95% CI -0.00370 to -0.00146), but not clearly after transfer from MetaVision (-0.00012, 95% CI -0.00206 to +0.00208). R3 was favoured in 10 of 30 source bootstrap refits. At matched observation counts, retaining measurements spread throughout the window reduced log loss relative to random retention by 0.03280 for R3 versus 0.00703 for R2. Removing the final 12 hours increased log loss relative to count-matched random removal by 0.01947 for R3 versus 0.00822 for R2. Under reduced availability, the local R3 increment changed from beneficial to harmful. Discussion: Physiological trajectories transferred more consistently, while observation-pattern features were particularly sensitive to the timing and availability of measurements. Conclusion: Clinical prediction models should be evaluated not only in new patient populations but also under the measurement conditions that supply their inputs.


### [112] [Instance-dependent sequential limits as an exact information ledger](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42133)
*Akshay Balsubramani*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Instance-dependent limits for bandit regret, sequential tests, best-arm identification and Markov decision processes are proved by one chain. It runs from a divergence decomposition through data processing to a statistic, a testing step that turns a class guarantee into a requirement on the history, and optimization over alternatives and allocations. Only the first step is an identity. This work writes the chain as an exact ledger. By the chain rule, data processing loses exactly a conditional divergence, which vanishes for arms in natural exponential families at the per-arm counts and reward sums. A one-parameter family of martingale identities extends the decomposition to every tilt between two instances. The classical limits follow by relaxations with named defects. All defects but the asymptotic step's can be computed from an algorithm's own runs, the uniform quantification's only when the class guarantee is a number. On seven published sources, from movie ratings to engine replacement, the ledger locates the difference between standard algorithms and the classical limits. For identification among trained models and for Wald's test the largest term is usually the uniform quantification's.


### [113] [Counting on Dummies: Privacy Amplification with Observable Counts and Truncated Batches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42139)
*Vitaly Feldman, Audra McMillan, Kunal Talwar*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In many practical deployments of Differentially Private Stochastic Gradient Descent (DP-SGD), the server processes data in batches. Randomly sampling these batches allows for better privacy bounds via privacy amplification results. Most sampling schemes with provable privacy amplification, including Poisson sampling and random allocation, result in variable batch sizes. The privacy guarantee of these schemes requires that the batch sizes are not revealed. In distributed settings, the server performing the aggregation or any party observing the communication traffic may learn these batch sizes. Furthermore, in both centralized and distributed settings, memory and computation constraints may require each batch to be of some maximum size $s$, necessitating truncation of the batch when it exceeds this limit. It is known that this truncation can result in a significant increase in privacy loss. In this work, we address both of these challenges via a simple mechanism that uses a random number of \emph{dummy points} to privatize the batch size and maintain the privacy guarantee even under truncation. We demonstrate that our mechanism is easy to implement in a distributed setting, and provide a general analysis of this mechanism for both Poisson sampling and random allocation. Our analysis is designed to support efficient and relatively tight privacy accounting. It also effectively amplifies the privacy of the count releases by subsampling, making the expected number of dummy points asymptotically smaller than what is needed to directly privatize the (sensitivity-one) batch sizes with non-negative noise. For Poisson sampling with truncated batches, we demonstrate empirically that our mechanism achieves better privacy--efficiency tradeoffs than the direct accounting for truncation recently given by (Ganesh, 2025).


<div id='cs.DC'></div>

# cs.DC [[Back]](#toc)

### [114] [LLM Fine-Tuning Workloads: Characterization, Challenges, and Opportunities for Serverless AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40983)
*Radu Nicolae, Daniele Lotito, Alexandru Iosup*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Serverless execution of Large Language Models (LLMs) is becoming important, reflecting their wide societal use. An increasingly present stage in LLM pipelines is fine-tuning, which specializes trained models and improves inference performance. Serverless operation of LLM fine-tuning workloads shifts operational concerns from the user to the provider. For these new workloads, providers face important operational complexities, such as performance issues, resource waste, and even service failure due to misconfiguration. However, optimizing such workloads is still at an early stage, and, relevant to this work, relatively little is currently known about how LLM fine-tuning workloads run in practice. In this work, we address the knowledge gap by characterizing the execution of LLM fine-tuning workloads on clusters equipped with different GPUs, running LLM models at various scales. We analyze IBM's LLMFineTuningBench, a large-scale, diverse profiling dataset of 30,920 fine-tuning experiments that records both successful and failing configurations. We provide quantitative insight into throughput (expressed in tokens/s), scaling, failure, and potential for performance optimization for four commonly used GPU types, in setups of 1 to 32 GPUs. We find that (1) job feasibility (run or fail) depends on workload properties, with failure ratios rising from 52% for small batch sizes (16-64) up to 95% for the largest (1,024); (2) throughput grows logarithmically with batch size; (3) scaling is near-linear when the workload is deployed intra-node, and optimizations such as RoCE substantially improves efficiency for inter-node deployment (e.g., 0.72 vs. 0.26 efficiency without RoCE); and (4) system-level optimizations can outweigh application-level ones (RoCE, up to 2.9$\times$, vs. Fast MoE, 1.7$\times$).


### [115] [Resource Locks and Fast Unlock for Shared Balances in Certificate-Based Payment Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41207)
*Lingkang Shangguan*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Certificate-based payment systems such as FastPay settle a payment with one round of signatures from a quorum of validators and avoid global ordering. The client collects the signatures, and validators never talk to each other. We observe that this client-driven design is complete only as long as no lock is ever undone. Each account has a single writer, a conflict can only come from that writer's own misbehavior, and the account stays locked as the penalty. Stablecoin payments routinely need balances that several accounts debit: treasuries, merchant pools, allowances and subscriptions. There, honest accounts race, locks must be undone, and a quorum certificate held privately by a client can be silently overridden. We prove that no unlock rule is safe in this setting unless certificate formation is visible to validators. We present a hybrid protocol. Personal accounts keep the client-driven FastPay path unchanged. A shared resource is a balance with its own version number and a set of authorized accounts. Transactions on shared resources are submitted to any validator and certified by a two-phase broadcast, ECHO then READY. The admission check requires both the sender's nonce and the resource's nonce to be free. Without contention, this path needs no consensus. When concurrent debits split the votes in any proportion, validators detect the split themselves, freeze the affected resource version, and resolve it with one agreement instance scoped to that version. Losing transactions return their sender nonce. We prove consistency, no overdraft, validity, finality and liveness. The protocol composes with the PDCC reconfiguration protocol.


### [116] [Falcons: Pushing the Speed-Ratio Limits of Lossy Compression for Parallel Scientific Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41209)
*Bing Lu, Jinyang Liu, Xinyang Ma, Quantong Fu, Chenwei Hou, Zedong Liu, Wenjing Huang, Yida Gu, Xingchen Liu, Zheng Wei, Anbang Wu, Xiaofeng Hou, Chao Li, Guangming Tan, Minyi Guo, Dingwen Tao*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: The extreme volume of scientific data has become a critical challenge for modern exa-scale scientific computing applications, and error-bounded lossy compression remains indispensable for scientific data management due to its high data reduction rate and controllable data fidelity. However, to ensure sufficient data reduction, existing compressors incur prohibitive computational overheads in real-world parallel scientific computing applications. To overcome the performance bottlenecks of error-bounded lossy compression, we present \textbf{Falcons}, a broadly deployable error-bounded lossy compressor that simultaneously achieves \textbf{high compression ratios with ultra-fast throughput, minimizing the overhead in parallel scientific applications}. Falcons integrates three key designs: memory-regularized interpolation with SIMD (Single Instruction Multiple Data) vectorization, distribution-adaptive lossless coding, and application-oriented optimizations for multiple parallelization schemes. Across our evaluations on cutting-edge supercomputers, Falcons clearly outperforms state-of-the-art compressors, simultaneously achieving up to 29\% compression ratio improvement and 255\% throughput improvement. In case studies of real-world applications, Falcons accelerates large-scale parallel data I/O and remote transfer by up to 44.6\% and 51.5\%, and provides 1.55$\times$ end-to-end speedup for the full WRF (Weather Research \& Forecasting) application workflow.


### [117] [AuditFL: A Contract-Backed Clearing Layer for Open Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41278)
*Alexandros Balla, Spyros Lalis*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) keeps raw data local, but most deployments still rely on a trusted coordinator for client admission, checkpoint distribution, update collection, aggregation, and rewards. This paper presents AuditFL, a contract-backed clearing layer for auditable FL in open settings. AuditFL keeps training and aggregation off chain, stores model artifacts in an assumed reliable off-chain artifact store, and records only content identifiers and economic state on chain. Clients reserve update tickets with deposits, publish update identifiers, and later claim refunds or rewards; the task coordinator publishes global checkpoints and finalizes rounds through deterministic settlement rules. The design does not remove the aggregator, but makes its on-chain round decisions attributable, replayable, auditable, and economically constrained. We implement AuditFL as both a deterministic state-machine prototype and a Solidity contract on a local Ethereum-compatible Anvil chain. On full MNIST with Dirichlet non-IID splits, 12 rounds, and up to 200 clients, AuditFL preserves the Plain FL learning trajectory while its overhead follows closed-form control-plane models. At 200 clients, the contract run issues 5,026 transactions, stores 2,413 off-chain artifacts (144.9 MiB), and consumes 829.7M EVM gas, dominated by per-client reserve and publish calls. Controlled dropout, invalid-update, and artifact-unavailability stress tests reduce inclusion nearly linearly but still finalize rounds, with final-accuracy changes remaining within a few tenths of an accuracy percentage point even under high injected failure rates.


### [118] [Migrating a Legacy Scientific Application with an AI Coding Agent: NICAM from Fortran to Kokkos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41313)
*Xuanzhengbo Ren, Takashi Shimokawabe, Hisashi Yashiro*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Migrating from Fortran to C++/Kokkos broadens access to hardware accelerators through backends such as CUDA and HIP while offering greater flexibility in software design. However, legacy Fortran applications often have large codebases, making manual porting time-consuming and costly. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) and AI coding agents have made large-scale code migration increasingly feasible. In this study, we ported NICAM (Nonhydrostatic ICosahedral Atmospheric Model), a legacy Fortran climate model accelerated with OpenACC, to C++/Kokkos with the assistance of Claude Code. The agent translated the code directly from Fortran to C++/Kokkos under human review. Approximately 140,000 lines of Fortran were analyzed and ported into 35,000 lines of C++/Kokkos, covering the dynamical core and seven physics packages. A 100-member ensemble comparison with the original Fortran model showed that the port is statistically consistent with the original. Performance was evaluated on Miyabi-G, an NVIDIA GH200 cluster, and Sirius, an AMD MI300A cluster. Compared with the Fortran/OpenACC baseline on GH200, the port achieved application speedups of $1.71\times$ on GH200 and $1.54\times$ on MI300A. The CUDA build achieved a GPU kernel-time speedup of $1.38\times$ on GH200. After normalization by peak memory bandwidth, the HIP build on MI300A achieved $0.96\times$ the kernel performance of the Fortran/OpenACC baseline.


### [119] [RecIS: Unifying Sparse and Dense Computation for Efficient Recommendation Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41457)
*Hua Zong, Shuai Yang, Zhengxiong Zhou, Qingtao Zeng, Jiawei Liu, Hao Wan, Hongbin Zhou, Jiahao Wang, Hao Zhang, Bencheng Yan, Yuheng Huang, Huimin Yi, Jiamang Wang, Han Zhu, Jian Xu, Bo Zheng, Lin Qu*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Modern recommender models scale sparse representations and dense computation together. They process thousands of heterogeneous feature fields, long user-behavior sequences, and trillion-scale Embedding parameters, while their dense networks increasingly use large Transformer architectures. The resulting execution mismatch creates fine-grained operations within stages, load imbalance and synchronization delays across devices, and GPU resource bubbles between stages. As models grow, these costs become a primary limit to scalable recommender training. Although existing systems improve Embedding caching and data prefetching, these inefficiencies still coexist in the GPU-centric workloads we study and jointly limit end-to-end throughput. We present RecIS, a unified framework for GPU-centric recommender training. RecIS treats the complete path from input features to dense computation as one optimization target and coordinates three complementary mechanisms. First, adaptive fusion combines fragmented feature transformations with vectorized memory access. Second, a GPU Embedding engine uses hash-based sharding and hierarchical communication to balance request placement and eliminate redundant transfers; it also specializes aggregation for forward and backward index structures. Third, a multistage pipeline overlaps input preparation for future iterations with dense computation in the current iteration. Together, these mechanisms reorganize a fragmented sparse front end into a dataflow that continuously feeds dense computation. We evaluate RecIS on representative industrial recommender models across distributed training platforms. Compared with PICASSO and TorchRec, RecIS reaches up to $2.62\times$ and $1.15\times$ their throughput, respectively, while reducing step latency by up to 61.5\% and 12.9\%. RecIS provides an efficient and scalable training system for large recommender models.


### [120] [HiState: Hierarchical Prefix Cache Management for Hybrid LLM Serving in Agentic Workloads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41508)
*Zaifeng Pan, Lucas Wang, Xinwei Qiang, Yanbo Zhou, Chang Chen, Zhengding Hu, Yue Guan, Yufei Ding*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Hybrid large language models combine full-attention and recurrent linear layers to reduce inference costs. Prefix caching can further reduce redundant computation across requests, but a recurrent checkpoint cannot recover an earlier state corresponding to a shorter prefix. Existing hybrid prefix caching systems retain checkpoints at selected boundaries to support common reuse patterns in conventional conversations. However, agent workloads modify and revisit earlier contexts, exposing reusable prefixes whose checkpoints were never saved or have already been evicted. A straightforward approach is to retain more checkpoints to reduce recomputation, but their cumulative footprint can displace reusable prefixes from other sessions and cause additional cache misses. To address these problems, we present HiState, a prefix cache management system for hybrid models serving agent workloads. Our key observation is that prefix reuse accesses all matching key-value (KV) entries but only one recurrent checkpoint, regardless of how many checkpoints are retained. This asymmetry allows HiState to retain historical checkpoints on SSD while prioritizing fast memory for KV entries: each hit retrieves only one checkpoint whose size is independent of context length. Within this hierarchy, we propose adaptive interval control to balance checkpoint storage against recomputation, and unified eviction to coordinate checkpoint sparsification with reclamation of stale trajectories. We implement HiState on top of vLLM and evaluate it on two public coding-agent trace datasets and controlled microbenchmarks. On real agent traces, HiState achieves up to 5.73x and 5.44x speedups in p99 TTFT over vLLM-Marconi and SGLang-HiCache, respectively.


### [121] [MemX: Memory-Aware Automatic Parallelism for Cross-Vendor Heterogeneous GPU Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41525)
*Lin Meng, Yuzhong Sun, Jie Zhu*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Cross-vendor heterogeneous GPU clusters are increasingly used for large-scale model training. However, their GPUs differ substantially in compute power, memory capacity, and interconnect characteristics. Existing automatic parallelism training systems usually minimize iteration time while treating memory as a hard constraint, almost failing in resolving the compute--memory asymmetry of cross-vendor clusters regardless correlation between memory and throughput. In this paper, we present MemX, a new memory-aware automatic parallelism system that jointly optimizes iteration time, peak memory usage, and cross-GPU memory balance. MemX consists of a weighted-product objective function, heterogeneous recomputation strategies, a minimal-set profiler, interpolation-based data augmentation, a dual-output XGBoost predictor, and a hash-signature prediction cache, in order to accelerate search of large-scale auto-parallel best configurations at lower cost. We implement MemX on Megatron-LM and evaluate it against four automatic parallelism baselines on two heterogeneous clusters. MemX achieves the lowest prediction Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE) of 12.17% and is the only method that identifies the real Top-1 configuration under both time-based and memory-balance rankings. In terms of search cost and search space, MemX searches 31,240 configurations in 26.5 seconds, while an end-to-end profiling baseline is extrapolated to require about 17 days at the same scale. The simulation study that extends the measured 20-GPU data to 40-GPU and 60-GPU clusters further illustrates the effectiveness of MemX at larger scales.


### [122] [Where KV-Cache Error Lives in Looped Language Models: A Diagnostic Study of Ouro and Huginn](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41825)
*Dezhen Lu, Runguo Li, Zixiang Ni, Yuchen Tang, Xingquan Kong, Jiaxing Yang, Jiarui Guo, Lingxiang Gao*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: A looped language model applies one block of layers 4 times per token and keeps a key/value (KV) plane for each loop. We study where storage error changes the next-token distribution and how shared-weight error accumulates, using Ouro-1.4B (4 loops) on GSM8K and MATH and Huginn-0125 (32 loops) on GSM8K-derived chains, with dense, simulated quantization. Per unit of error, values carry 94% of the diagonal Fisher mass, but measured KL is dominated by keys with larger quantization errors. Local second-order prediction follows the measured KL of typical sequences, including 136 held-out chains, but misses a few confident rows, and a local second-order factor for shared weights at 32 loops ranges from 1.65 (INT8) to 4.09 (INT4), so it depends on the error direction. A cell-level allocator lowers one-step KL at 25-48% of BF16 bytes, while paired task-accuracy comparisons depend on budget and the 25% gap against K4V2 shrinks to near zero under a post hoc answer-extraction check. A later Ouro-2.6B test retains the key/value split but falsifies measured-cost allocation's advantage over Fisher at 31%; across all 21 ledger registrations, 60 of 176 criteria failed.


### [123] [A Deterministic Commitment Rule over Unstructured DAGs for Optimistic Replication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41837)
*João Paulo Bezerra, Petr Kuznetsov, Léo Olivier, Sara Tucci-Piergiovanni*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: In optimistic replication protocols, each replica uses the local state to execute requests from its clients in a wait-free manner, without synchronizing with other replicas. The outcomes may be revised later, making optimistic protocols only eventually consistent. To compensate for this consistency loss, an additional commitment mechanism can be employed: an underlying atomic broadcast protocol that allows replicas to periodically agree on the evolution of the object state. In this paper, we introduce a simple framework for optimistic replication based on a causality DAG: every replica represents the object state via a causally ordered set of client operations. The framework incorporates a deterministic commitment rule that incurs virtually zero message overhead beyond the basic dissemination of submitted operations.


### [124] [Layered Reproducibility for High-Performance Computing Applications: The Feel++ and Ktirio Urban Buildings Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41861)
*Javier Cladellas, Vincent Chabannes, Christophe Prud'Homme*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: The High-Performance Computing (HPC) community struggles with reproducibility due to complex software stacks, heterogeneous hardware, and expensive validation cycles. This contribution presents a layered reproducibility strategy in which Feel++ provides a traceable software supply chain and Ktirio Urban Buildings (KUB) builds on it to manage versioned urban-building data, executable simulations, and checksum-verifiable result artifacts. Feel++ treats continuous integration (CI) plans, CMake presets, packaging manifests, public software metadata, operations commands, containers, software bill of materials (SBOM)/provenance metadata, and Feel++ Benchmarking reports for Feel++ and KUB cases as repositoryowned artifacts. KUB applies this foundation to City Energy Model Database (CEMDB) datasets, manifests, Findable, Accessible, Interoperable, and Reusable (FAIR) packaging, and verifiable outputs for computationally expensive simulations. The case study shifts reproducibility from late manual packaging to an artifact chain linking source commits, packages, containers, benchmark configurations, dataset versions, provenance records, and generated results.


### [125] [Decentralized Dynamic Stable Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41912)
*Jing Chen, Mengtong Gao, Zhenhe Zhang*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: We study decentralized computation of the player-optimal stable matching (POSM) with unannounced player arrivals and departures. Without a common clock, event information, or direct communication, players propose to arms and observe only their own accept/reject outcomes. Mismatch cost counts rounds when the realized matching differs from the current POSM. For known preferences, we give a deterministic polynomial-time self-calibrating protocol with cost $O(t_*+K^2\log T)$, improving the previous bound $O(T^{1-1/K}(\log T)^{2/K})$. Here $K$ is the number of arms, $t_*$ the last event time, and $T$ the horizon. Players use only local time and proposal outcomes, knowing neither $t_*$ nor $T$. For any nondecreasing, polynomial-time-computable $g(t)=\Omega(t)$, the protocol achieves $O(g(t_*)+K^2N_g(T))$. Here $g$ governs recovery time, while $N_g(T)$ counts iterations of $t\mapsto t+g(t)$ below $T$, exposing a tradeoff between recovery time and asymptotic mismatch cost. Any randomized protocol with recovery scale $g(t)\ge1$ and sublinear asymptotic mismatch cost has worst-case expected cost $\Omega(t_*+N_g(T))$. Thus, with $g(t)=t$, our protocol is optimal up to a $K^2$ factor among sublinear-cost protocols with $O(t_*)$ recovery time. For unknown preferences on both sides, each player and arm learns its preferences from its own observations after matching. We give a decentralized learning protocol with expected cost $O(t_*+K^4\Delta_{\mathrm{min}}^{-2}\log T)$, where $\Delta_{\mathrm{min}}$ measures preference gaps. A complementary static-setting lower bound adapted from the literature shows that the dependence on $T$ and $\Delta_{\mathrm{min}}$ is tight.


### [126] [Matching Workloads to Blackwell: Power-Resolved Benchmarking of NVIDIA B200 and B300 across HPC and AI Workloads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41919)
*Mohsen Zand, Trung Dac Nguyen, H. Birali Runesha*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: We evaluate NVIDIA B200 and B300 (Blackwell Ultra) GPUs on two independent eight-GPU clusters, spanning computer-vision inference and training, LLM inference with Llama-3-8B and 70B~\cite{llama3}, and six production HPC codes. The B300 is not a uniform successor to the B200: it trails by up to 5.6$\times$ on convolutional inference, 8$\times$ on 3D vision transformer training, 24--27\% on 70B LLM throughput, and 16$\times$ on double-precision Quantum ESPRESSO. We resolve the convolutional deficit using per-GPU board power rather than throughput alone: on the affected workloads the B300 draws only 36--53\% of its 1100\,W envelope, whereas on ViT-Large, which it wins, both parts saturate at 95--97\% of TDP. Because our harness keeps inputs resident in device memory, a host-side data pipeline is excluded by construction, localizing the cause to per-kernel cost. Two B300 nodes of identical silicon differ by 2.1$\times$ in time-to-first-token solely from a development vLLM build. We give per-workload procurement guidance.


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [127] [More Rules, Longer Lists? Benchmarking Self-Evolving Agents on a Hidden-Rule Curriculum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40758)
*Chenjie Qiu, Boyan Xu, Shaobin Shi, Bingfeng Chen, Ruichu Cai, Dongsheng Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Self-evolving agents rewrite a persistent state from feedback, but it is largely untested whether this lets them learn a rule nobody told them. An agent can also pass by writing down each correction as an exception, and its list then grows with every new rule. We introduce CLR-Bench, a Curriculum of Latent Rules benchmark. Each chain adds one hidden rule per level on top of the earlier ones. A rule acts on an opaque label that stands for a named grouping published on the Web, such as the founding members of an airline alliance. The task gives one example value, practice feedback reveals one more member and one non-member, and the test checks unseen members and similar non-members. Probes certify that the base model knows the members of each grouping and does not guess it without feedback, and that the evidence identifies the grouping once labels are read as named groupings. An automated pipeline builds chains from a new data domain, and we release 20 chains over 10 domains. We evaluate six recent self-evolution methods. With the certifying model they barely learn. The best gain at most 5 points over a frozen agent and exclude only 6-12% of the unseen members from the count, fewer than the 20% reached by simply keeping the raw practice history. A fixed lesson that states the named-grouping convention lifts the same methods by 14.5 to 16.5 points. Their states hold the revealed member in 75-98% of cases and almost never the class name, and when practice exposes a member the list misses, they add that member to the list. With the stronger GPT-6-Sol, ACE and Dynamic Cheatsheet learn from feedback alone. They reach 78% accuracy and solve 59-60% of the single cells, against 53% and 8% with the certifying model, and they surpass the raw history. CLR-Bench therefore measures how well an agent learns, and this ability differs across models.


### [128] [Beyond Plausible Prediction: Safety Sufficiency in Web World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40798)
*Zhifei Hu, Alexandra I. Cristea, Adel Bibi, Jialin Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Web world models increasingly predict the consequences of candidate actions before agents act, yet a plausible predicted future does not necessarily support the correct safety decision. In a matched human evaluation of 3,000 predictions from ten frozen open models, 86.1% are judged semantically plausible. However, several models exhibit highly asymmetric authorisation behaviour, combining low false-safe execution with poor recall of clarifiable ASK cases and a strong preference for BLOCK. This motivates safety sufficiency: whether a predictive interface preserves and exposes the policy-decisive information required to distinguish actions that may be EXECUTED, require clarification through ASK, or should be BLOCKED. We introduce SafeWebWorld, a benchmark for evaluating safety sufficiency across five web-safety domains through action-conditioned futures, policy-decisive evidence, and policy-conditioned decisions. Under a controlled Security-Aligned Supervised Fine-Tuning protocol, evidence preservation can improve substantially while authorisation-sensitive decisions deteriorate. To determine whether this deterioration reflects loss of authorisation-sensitive score structure or a failure of the decision readout, we introduce Latent Authorisation Recovery (LAR). Frozen-score recalibration reveals substantial but backbone-dependent recovery, while paired recovery provides only a modest additional gain. Yet within-family and matched-authorisation evaluations remain poor, showing that aggregate recoverability does not imply robust fine-grained authorisation behaviour. Overall, plausible prediction, evidence preservation, and aggregate recovery are each insufficient on their own to establish robust authorisation.


### [129] [Circumventing the Computational Hardness of Computational Arbitrage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40831)
*Nastaran Okati, Ricardo Olmedo, Moritz Hardt*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: AI model marketplaces host providers with heterogeneous costs and capabilities. These differences create an opportunity for computational arbitrage (Olmedo et al., 2026), in which an intermediary purchases inference from a portfolio of models and resells it to users at a profit. Beyond creating profit opportunities for the arbitrageur, computational arbitrage offers financial value to both consumers and providers and could therefore significantly influence AI model markets. While prior work empirically demonstrates the feasibility of computational arbitrage for small model pools, scaling it to large marketplaces requires arbitrage strategies that are both computationally tractable and inexpensive to identify and deploy. In this work, we formalize computational arbitrage as an allocation problem and prove that it is generally NP-hard. However, we show that the optimal allocation can be computed efficiently under a natural diminishing-returns condition on how model performance scales with inference spending. We further develop a sample-efficient method for estimating model performance from limited data, reducing the upfront cost of identifying arbitrage strategies. Our analysis also yields several economic insights: at the optimum, every funded model delivers the same marginal utility per dollar; arbitrage directs revenue away from providers offering little utility relative to their prices; and it rewards complementary specialization.


### [130] [Incongruence-Aware Evaluation of Multimodal Speech Emotion Recognition in Audio Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40883)
*Fengji Jing, Guy Laban*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Voice-interactive large language models process both what a speaker says and how they say it, yet the two channels do not always agree. We ask whether that disagreement degrades speech emotion recognition. Mapping text and audio into a shared valence-arousal-dominance space, we convert the distance between them into a continuous, threshold-free incongruence score with a two-component Gaussian mixture, and validate the score against human judgement. We evaluate three audiocapable models on acted, conversational and spontaneous speech (eNTERFACE'05, MELD and CMU-MOSEI). With the model held constant across modalities, incongruence raises the odds of error whenever audio is present, in every corpus, after controlling for the ground-truth emotion and for distance in the feature space. Supervised classifiers trained on the same features behave in the opposite way, which suggests that the difficulty lies in how these models combine raw evidence rather than in the signal itself. Text-only effects are unreliable: swapping only the model that reads the transcript reverses their sign on one corpus. Rebuilding the score without the emotion lexicon reproduces the central result, and among six simpler alternatives it is the only label-free score that predicts error in all three corpora. Code, data tables and prompts are released.


### [131] [ChatGPT as a Surveillance Assistant? An Exploratory Case Study of AI-Assisted Surveillance Risks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40890)
*Ayana Hussain, Nicholas Vincent*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can generate detailed guidance, functional code, and persuasive text at scale. As one of the most widely used consumer language model products as of 2026, ChatGPT gives broad public access to these capabilities, motivating concerns about its potential use in surveillance-related tasks. We present an exploratory case study of what potentially harmful surveillance assistance looks like in ChatGPT's public web interface. We contribute a taxonomy of seven surveillance risk categories based on prior LLM risk research and reports of deployed surveillance systems, instantiated as 110 prompts, along with a manually reviewed analysis of ChatGPT's responses. Prompts were issued using two approaches: 55 jailbreak prompts drawing on five types of elicitation strategies, and 55 plain-language direct prompts, each run alone, and if unsuccessful, again with a short fixed prefix. We find that ChatGPT provides substantive surveillance guidance for 40 of the 55 jailbreak prompts and for 33 of the 55 direct prompts without any prefix. Compliant responses also rarely included policy mentions or suggestions for privacy-preserving alternatives. We manually review every response and find that even when ChatGPT states a refusal, it may still supply detailed instructions, working code, or architectural guidance alongside brief ethical caveats.


### [132] [Does Recursive Knowledge Construction Help Agents Verify Consensus Claims in Science?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40906)
*Lun Ai, Yu-Heng Wu, Jonathan Schaul, Leonie Küchenhoff, Denes Turei, Francesco Carli, Fabio Petroni, Aurelien Dugourd, Julio Saez-Rodriguez*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Recent scientific agents build knowledge recursively by retrieving evidence, constructing an intermediate state, and judging whether that state contains sufficient information. These agents are evaluated end-to-end, usually without ground truth, so the effect of each design choice is unknown. Here, we examined two design axes: (1) what the recursion builds (the state of evidence) and (2) what controls it (the judge that decides when to stop). To measure them, we defined consensus scientific claim verification and measured these effects on this task, where a system must establish a claim's consensus stance from the open literature. We built a new consensus claim dataset, ProClaim-eval, including 718 claims with SUPPORT and REFUTE gold labels from five expert-curated databases spanning molecular biology, nuclear physics, and astronomy. Our instrument, ProClaim, persists an evidence state outside the model's context, so each axis can be studied with the other held fixed. Against verification systems and general-purpose agent harnesses, ProClaim ranks among the top three, with a significant advantage over the harnesses. We found that the evidence state improves performance over in-context accumulation, and it also matters what is stored in it. In addition, a trained judge that reads the evidence state performs better than fixed-round stopping. A judge trained separately and never exposed to ProClaim-eval achieves the highest agreement with the gold labels on three of five subsets, where it matches the off-the-shelf Claude Sonnet~4.6 judge and outperforms Claude Haiku~4.5. Our results support recursively maintaining a structured evidence state outside the model's context as an effective design for verifying claims in scientific agents.


### [133] [Towards Portable Specification of the Agent Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40917)
*Nicolas Gertler, Claudio Novelli, Luciano Floridi*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Large language models act only through the infrastructure that surrounds them. That infrastructure, the harness, determines what a model can accomplish and how it fails, yet it has no settled definition. To address this gap, we decompose the harness into eight functional components that can be specified and compared across systems, and we propose a portable specification framework that records which model ran in which harness. The record is needed because different harness configurations can yield several-fold differences in reported scores for the same model, and because much of an agent's attack surface lies in its harness. Existing model cards and agent cards provide release and operational information, but do not establish the sequence of effective configurations within a run. Harnesses can change between releases and between runs, and, where agents edit their own tools and configuration, within a run. Enacted AI laws contain documentation duties relevant to covered systems, subject to their respective scopes and application dates, but do not prescribe the common harness record proposed here. Therefore, the harness has to be explicitly described and specified as a versioned part of the system.


### [134] [Agentic World Modeling for Agent Test-time Scaling without Environment Replica](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40927)
*Wendi Li, Huseyin A. Inan, Wei-Ning Chen, Yanzhi Chen, Chetan Bansal, Saravan Rajmohan, Sharon Li*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Test-time scaling (TTS) improves large language model agents by sampling and selecting among multiple candidate trajectories, but common realizations assume that these trajectories can be executed in parallel. This assumption breaks in realistic deployments, where enterprise workspaces and cloud services are shared and persistent, costly or infeasible to replicate, and actions may introduce irreversible side effects or cross-trajectory interference. Existing generative world models avoid direct execution, but typically compress environment state into a single prompt, limiting their ability to reliably simulate transitions under partial observability. Instead, we introduce Agentic World Modeling (AWM), which reformulates next-observation prediction as a multi-turn evidence-acquisition task. For each hypothetical action, an agentic simulator adaptively queries the real environment through safe read-only interfaces, reasons over the returned evidence, and uses executable computation to construct the resulting observation without mutating shared state. Integrated with trajectory-level TTS, AWM enables parallel exploration over a single shared environment. Across $\tau$-bench, AppWorld, and OfficeBench, AWM outperforms a single-shot simulation baseline by 40.07% on average, and surpasses a trained world-model baseline by 12.36% on average. These results establish interactive, read-grounded simulation as a practical foundation for safe and scalable agentic test-time scaling in shared, non-resettable environments.


### [135] [DiRAFT: Privacy-Aware Financial Transcript Synthesis from Aggregate Discourse Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40938)
*Yunbai Zhang, Yunyi Gao, Han-Tai Shiao*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Using customer conversations to train and evaluate financial language models raises confidentiality concerns because non-public financial facts can remain after direct identifiers are masked. Synthetic transcripts offer an alternative data source, but their discourse fidelity and residual confidentiality risks require assessment. We introduce DiRAFT (Discourse Realization from Aggregate First-order Transitions), which extracts financial concept graphs and records the order in which concepts are first introduced. It pools adjacent concept transitions across training records and samples new graph plans from aggregate transition, starting-concept, and plan-length priors. A discourse layer guides text realization without supplying an individual source passage or graph. We assess privacy-related risks through two complementary evaluations: corpus-level structural overlap and source-grounded checks for specific non-public investor facts. The structural assessment covers two financial corpora and three generation backends. The confidential-fact assessment uses human review of 1,400 investor-call outputs across seven strategies and two backends. We also evaluate transition fidelity, content recoverability, and trajectory diversity. Stochastic sampling provides a favorable balance between transition fidelity and structural overlap, with consistent advantages over greedy and top-3 on investor calls. The confidential-fact assessment likewise detects fewer matches for stochastic sampling than these alternatives on both backends covered by the fact assessment. These complementary assessments provide empirical evidence on residual confidentiality risks without establishing a formal guarantee.


### [136] [The First Few Tokens Matter: Understanding Chain-of-Thought Controllability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40939)
*Hugh Van Deventer, Dmitrii Troitskii, Laura Ruis*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring is a promising way to oversee increasingly capable agents. It succeeds in part because current models appear limited in their ability to control what they verbalize in their reasoning traces. We investigate the source of this limitation and link it to an entropy collapse in the first few tokens following the opening thinking tag. We exploit this mechanism to recover CoT controllability through two inference-time interventions on initial tokens: prefilling, where even a single token can substantially increase controllability, and banning frequent opening tokens combined with in-context examples, which improves average compliance by 34-41 percentage points across models. These interventions recover mainly addition and style controllability (adding or transforming information), while suppression controllability (avoiding or removing information) exhibits more varied responses, suggesting distinct underlying mechanisms. Consistent with this, the two groups behave as separate capabilities: across models, performance correlates positively within these groups, while cross-group correlations are mostly negative. Importantly, we find that the relationship with monitorability depends on the evaluation: addition and style controllability more consistently positively correlate with monitorability, while suppression often shows negative associations. Our findings motivate the development of controllability evaluations that distinguish difficulty initiating a specific opening from general limitations of the ability to alter reasoning content, alongside separate reporting of control tasks and categories.


### [137] [Benchmarking Forecasting Agents on Yesterday's Tomorrows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40940)
*Yuxuan Wang, Yishu Wang, Jiaqi Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Digital agents can now gather and organize information, reason over it, and use tools to complete complex tasks. Forecasting puts all of these abilities to the test: it requires integrating incomplete evidence, inferring causal drivers, and committing to calibrated probabilistic judgments. Existing benchmarks are poorly suited to measuring and improving this ability. Live benchmarks provide feedback only after events resolve, so every evaluation cycle waits on the calendar; fixed-corpus evaluations return results immediately but confine research to pre-collected documents. We introduce FABench, a scalable backtesting environment for Forecasting Agents. An automated pipeline discovers events, generates questions, and resolves outcomes for any past forecast date, so the benchmark can be renewed to stay ahead of model training cutoffs; its first release poses 287 questions across nine domains. Agents research each question freely in an archive environment that combines an offline archive with live web search screened to prevent temporal leakage. Evaluating agents built on eight recent models, we find that retrieval improves the Brier score of all eight in every retrieval setting and lifts the leading agents to the market consensus on the 54 questions linked to Polymarket, with the best three scoring slightly above it. Research traces reveal recurring failures in how agents weigh and apply evidence, leaving ample room for improvement. We release our code, data, and archive environment as a foundation for community research on forecasting agents.


### [138] [Opening the Box Changes the Cat: LLMs Detect Chain-of-Thought Monitoring - What now?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40946)
*Amir Jalilifard, Marcos Medeiros Raimundo, Anderson Rocha*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning allows large language models (LLMs) to generate intermediate reasoning traces in natural language while solving complex tasks. These traces can support safety monitoring by exposing potentially harmful plans before actions are executed. In this work, we investigate the conditions under which LLMs infer the presence of CoT monitoring from direct and indirect environmental cues and examine how this inference affects their subsequent behavior. Our observations suggest that oversight is an interactive process: while a monitor inspects a model's reasoning, traces left by monitoring interventions can allow the model to infer that its thoughts are being observed and to adapt its behavior accordingly. We show that even subtle environmental cues, such as timing-related signals from timeouts and operation durations, can reveal the presence of monitoring and trigger models to adapt their behavior. Across repeated episodes and interaction loops, we further observe that models can form statistical estimates of the monitor's effectiveness and use these estimates to guide attempts to evade detection. These findings highlight the need to evaluate CoT-based oversight under adaptive conditions, where the monitoring process itself may influence the behavior it seeks to observe.


### [139] [P-ContextBench: From Personal Fact Recall to Personalized Agent Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40959)
*Grant Nasir Brown, Abdul Basir Bari, Anmol Tadikonda, Isabel Song*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for personal tasks, yet evaluating their safe use of personal context remains difficult because such contexts are private, fragmented, dynamic, and socially situated. We introduce P-ContextBench, a realistic, verifiable, and scalable benchmark for evaluating personal-context understanding. P-ContextBench constructs population-structured, life-story-grounded digital humans from demographic seeds reflecting the U.S. population. Rather than using explicit, static profiles, P-ContextBench embeds traits, routines, goals, and constraints as implicit evidence across fragmented, evolving synthetic mobile-app records. It evaluates five capabilities: single-source fact recovery, multi-source fact recovery, temporal state tracking, inference of higher-level personal patterns from routine behavior, and safe handling of requests conflicting with persona-specific constraints through compliance, warning, clarification, or refusal. A multi-stage validation pipeline checks persona plausibility, context recoverability, information leakage, evidence preservation, test-case grounding, and human-judge agreement. We instantiate P-ContextBench with 100 personas, 10,088 hidden facts, 18,261 app-trace fragments, and 4,810 verified test cases. The pipeline supports configurable generation across scales and demographic groups. Cost-effective state-of-the-art (SOTA) LLMs relevant to personal agents achieve below 60% accuracy on temporal tracking, higher-level pattern inference, and constraint-aware action-boundary decisions, with fewer than 10% of test cases answered correctly across all three repeated runs. Even a high-performing SOTA model remains inconsistent on these capabilities. P-ContextBench provides measurable targets for improving the accuracy, reliability, and safety of LLM-based personal agents.


### [140] [Nedimo: An Evidence-Grounded Multilingual Pipeline for AI-Generated Sleep Stories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40970)
*Georgios Makridis, Stefanos Kampouris*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Generative audio systems can produce large libraries of personalized bedtime content, but ordinary media objectives (retention, completion, and novelty) are poorly aligned with the goal of supporting a transition toward sleep. Direct evidence for narrated bedtime audio in adults is mixed, and a recent active-controlled trial did not support such audio as a standalone treatment for sleep disturbance. We translate this uncertain evidence into conservative engineering requirements and present a multilingual pipeline for generating and narrating low-arousal sleep stories. The implemented prototype separates deterministic planning from large language model generation, represents content and delivery parameters explicitly, shapes each script through a four-phase attention taper, validates structural and lexical constraints, performs a bounded error-aware retry, and quarantines unresolved outputs. Provider-agnostic text-to-speech synthesis is followed by paragraph-aware joining, two-pass loudness normalization, and a publication gate that re-measures the saved, encoded file against fixed loudness, loudness-range, and true-peak limits. The product data model records stop position, replay, early abandonment, and completion while treating these events as ambiguous behavioral proxies rather than clinical outcomes. We contribute an evidence-to-requirement mapping, a traceable guardrail architecture with explicit acceptance thresholds, technical evaluation criteria, and a prospective protocol for testing sleep-onset effects. No clinical efficacy is claimed; the system is positioned as a relaxation and attentional-support tool whose benefit and possible reliance must be evaluated empirically.


### [141] [The psychophysics of affect in large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40985)
*Joseph Marks, Marius C. Vollberg*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Good decisions often require considering how outcomes will feel. Yet large language models (LLMs) increasingly enter decision workflows, despite little being known about how they map outcomes to feelings or use those mappings in choice. We tested how LLMs anticipated gambling or stock-trading outcomes would feel and whether they incorporated these ratings into choice. When outcomes were rated in isolation, ratings exhibited diminishing sensitivity to magnitude, resembling human value functions; but models rated gains more intensely than equivalent losses, an inversion of canonical loss aversion we call silicon gain amplification. When outcomes were rated together, enabling direct comparison, ratings linearised. In choice, the gain-loss asymmetry largely disappeared, and models' own ratings did not reliably explain their choices better than monetary utility did. These findings reveal malleable mappings from outcomes to affect, raising questions about whether and how agentic LLMs should incorporate anticipated feelings into decisions.


### [142] [Can Personal Requirements Become Supervision for Planning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40992)
*Junwei Wu, Runze Yan, Yuzhang Xie, Hanqi Luo, Ziwei Dong, Jiaying Lu, Xiao Hu, Carl Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Managing multiple chronic conditions through diet requires satisfying disease- and medication-specific requirements jointly across meals and days while preserving established eating habits. These requirements are public in clinical guidelines and drug labels, yet turning them into a feasible plan for one person remains difficult for language-model agents. In our dietitian-approved weekly planning evaluation built from NHANES, thirteen models, from medically fine-tuned 8B models to GPT-6.1 Sol, pass fewer than 3% of complete plans through direct generation. Explicit rules, higher reasoning effort, or model-directed tools leave GPT-6.1 Sol at no more than 8%. We present Noura, which pairs LLM planning with executable personal specifications instantiated from public clinical guidance and individual health and dietary profiles. During planning, the specification grounds foods, optimizes portions, checks the complete multi-day plan, and guides revision. Our key insight is that a specification that rejects a plan can also determine what to learn from it. Noura turns executed plans and contextually credited revisions into supervision for compact models, recovering targets even from trajectories that never achieve complete success. The same specification supplies supervised targets, preferences, and rewards for post-training. On 4,993 held-out participants, Noura-SFT raises Qwen3-14B's complete-plan pass rate from 24.0% to 72.5% within the same execution loop, cuts rule violations by 97.5% with no observed critical failures, and improves dietary quality. The locally hosted 14B planner incurs fewer rule violations than its GPT-6 Luna teacher. A matched comparison shows that executed targets reduce rule violations by 41% over model-written proposals. Across four Qwen, Phi, and Llama backbones, self-training alone reduces rule violations by 41-61% on a 1,249-person population subset.


### [143] [Violet Teaming in Practice: Lessons from a Pilot Expert Workshop on Governing Complex AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41018)
*Steph Batalis, Kait Connors, Ben C. Snyder, Vikram Venkatram, Josh Wentzel*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Violet teaming has been proposed as a method to integrate technical, policy, and societal considerations into the evaluation of emerging technologies. Here, we document a pilot expert workshop used to explore violet teaming as a governance methodology for biodefense-relevant AI capabilities and describe observations from the exercise. The workshop used a hypothetical technology scenario across three sequential stages - capability development, misuse potential, and implementation - while incorporating technical, security, operational, and policy perspectives. The sequential format provided an integrated way to examine potential benefits, misuse risks, and real-world implementation challenges, rather than considering these issues in isolation. The exercise surfaced non-obvious misuse pathways, expanded potential interventions beyond purely technical safeguards, and highlighted operational and policy constraints that could affect implementation. This report summarizes the workshop design, outputs, and lessons learned for applying violet teaming to the evaluation of complex, dual-use technologies.


### [144] [ReplayRCA: Measuring Post-hoc Diagnosis and Live Oversight of Agent Failures by Counterfactual Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41025)
*Jeremy Canale, Fabien Soulis*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: When a language-model agent fails a multi-step task, can another model find the decisive mistake and fix it with a single action? We introduce ReplayRCA, a benchmark that scores such diagnoses by intervention rather than by agreement with annotations: the environment is restored to the state preceding the chosen step, the proposed action replaces the agent's own, and the same agent continues. The score, causal repair, is the continuation success rate minus that of a paired placebo that replays the agent's original action through the same mechanism. Two tracks share this protocol: a post-hoc autopsy, where the diagnoser sees the whole failed run and is told that it failed, and live oversight, where a supervisor watches the run unfold and may intervene once, before an irreversible action, without knowing whether the run will fail. Tasks come from OpsWorld, a deterministic, procedurally generated back-office environment with six difficulty levels, the last of which is multimodal (QR codes, handwriting). On 62 calibrated systematic failures, the best of 13 frontier models (Grok 4.7) reaches a causal repair of +51.3 points; the top eight models are statistically indistinguishable, the flagship models of the two largest vendors rank 8th and 12th, and 5 items are repaired by no model. Forcing a strong model to intervene at the last step costs it 17.1 points, so the last-step shortcut of annotation-based attribution benchmarks does not transfer.
