A variety of approaches have attempted to circumvent this, but they either fail to match attention's expressiveness (e.g., sliding-window or linear attention variants with fixed-size state) or only achieve constant-factor speedups (e.g., sparse attention methods whose indexers themselves scale quadratically). In this paper, we derive an asymptotically subquadratic sparse attention method based on a hierarchical, tree-structured indexer, which we call Zyphra Hierarchical Attention (ZHA). ZHA constructs a tree of recursively compressed key summaries over the KV cache, which future queries traverse from the top down to retrieve a sparse subset of keys. Crucially, by iteratively growing the tree's depth, the total cost including routing and final attention grows sublinearly in sequence length, resulting in overall subquadratic (but superlinear) scaling for attention. We demonstrate that ZHA can be cheaply applied to existing pretrained models with only a short KL distillation training stage over a few billion tokens, and maintains performance on long-context retrieval and challenging reasoning, mathematical, and agentic coding evaluation tasks. Moreover, we show that our method is highly robust to tree geometry including very deep trees of up to 6 levels. Finally, we develop a detailed performance model and show that our approach scales subquadratically, dramatically outperforms competing methods beyond 1 million context, and ultimately has the potential to extend effective context windows to the hundreds of millions or billions of tokens.


### [238] [Li$^{2}$SAO: Lifting Light Singl-Rollout Asynchronous RL Optimization via Group-Relative Actor-Critic Bootstrapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40726)
*Zichen Bai, Yanli Wang, Yang Liu, Junhao Shi, Xueqin Liu, Jinguo Sun, Muye Li, Shaoqiang Zheng, Xuefei Diao, Zhuwei Zeng, Nan Duan, Peng Cao*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Asynchronous reinforcement learning (RL) is becoming a mainstream paradigm for post-training large language models (LLMs), as it improves training efficiency by updating the model as rollouts arrive. Recent work argues that group-wise sampling in GRPO does not naturally fit asynchronous RL training because rollout lengths follow a long-tailed distribution, and proposes Single-rollout Asynchronous Optimization (SAO) to improve stability and off-policy behavior, especially on agentic tasks. However, SAO relies on a closed-source value model obtained from scaling value pretraining. In this paper, we analyze GRPO and LiSAO, our lightweight reproduction of SAO without the pretrained value model, on single-turn reasoning and multi-turn agentic tasks. We find that GRPO outperforms LiSAO on reasoning tasks, while the reverse holds on agentic tasks. Since the value model of LiSAO is not yet well trained , LiSAO lags behind GRPO substantially in early training steps. To address this issue, we propose Li$^{2}$SAO, which Lifts Light Single-rollout Asynchronous Optimization with group-relative actor-critic bootstrapping: while the critic is cold, a group-relative advantage trains the actor and a group-centered return trains the critic, after which training switches to single-rollout GAE. Experiments show that Li$^{2}$SAO catches up with or surpasses GRPO in early training and further raises the later performance ceiling. Code is available at this https URL .


### [239] [Mage-VL-Cowork: A Full Training Recipe to Learn Hybrid Computer Use From Evolved Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40747)
*Xiaoyi Zhang, Haoqing Wang, Xinjie Zhang, Xiang Ming, Xing Wang, Senqiao Yang, Kaichen Zhang, Zhaoyang Jia, Andong Tan, Xinjiang Wang, Jinghao Guo, Yifei Shen, Xiao Li, Peng Zhang, Xun Guo, Jiahao Li, Shicheng Zheng, Jinglu Wang, Zongyu Guo, Wenxuan Xie, Bin Li, Yan Lu*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Effective computer-use agents (CUAs) should seamlessly combine visual GUI interaction with code and terminal execution within a single policy. We present Mage-VL-Cowork, a 4B hybrid CUA trained from a general multimodally aligned checkpoint through an end-to-end, progressive five-stage curriculum across midtraining, SFT, and RL. To power this curriculum at scale, Mage-CUA-Forge replaces per-task agent construction with reusable, skill-conditioned generation workflows and application-specific preparers, synthesizing 293,806 desktop and web tasks across 6,915 skills while reducing recurring per-task expenditure by 82.6%. Rather than directly distilling a default teacher, we evolve the teacher harness and instantiate specialized CUA harnesses to yield high-quality trajectories alongside stage-specific supervision. Mage-VL-Cowork-4B finally climbs to 66.0% on OSWorld-Verified through different training stages with more efficient reasoning cost, achieving state-of-the-art performance at its parameter size and pushing forward the frontier of on-device intelligence.


### [240] [Fewer Heads, Fewer Memories: Pruning Redundant Attention Heads Suppresses Verbatim Memorisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40974)
*Aryan Chowdhury, Riza Batista-Navarro*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Large language models have been shown to memorise and reproduce verbatim sequences from their training data, raising concerns about privacy leakage, intellectual property exposure, and the limits of generalisation. Recent theoretical work suggests that attention heads may contribute to this behaviour by functioning as associative memory mechanisms, with memorisation capacity scaling with the number of heads. At the same time, empirical work has shown that many attention heads are redundant and can be pruned with limited impact on downstream performance. Despite these parallel findings, the effect of pruning redundant heads on memorised-sequence reproduction remains unexplored. In this work, we demonstrate that zeroing out a substantial fraction of attention heads prior to inference can significantly suppress verbatim memorisation while preserving, or even improving, language modelling performance on the benchmark used to identify redundant heads. Evaluating GPT-2-XL(1.5B) on known memorised sequences, we find that pruning 729 task-redundant heads reduces the memorisation count by 91.5%, while improving WikiText-103 perplexity by 0.92. These findings suggest that attention-head redundancy can be exploited to mitigate memorisation at inference time, without retraining or modifying the training data.


### [241] [KV-Cache Patching: Tracing How and Where Fine-Tuned Behaviors Arise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41480)
*Jonas Loos, Oliver Eberle*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Fine-tuning a language model changes how it responds, but where is this fine-tuned behavior instantiated? To better understand this, we evaluate whether the change in model behavior arises only during the generation of the answer tokens where the change manifests, or whether altered processing of previous tokens causes it via the attention mechanism. For this, we patch the KV cache from a LoRA fine-tuned model into the unmodified base model, and vice versa. Our results show that for some behaviors, like responding in German or emergent misalignment, patching the KV cache can indeed transfer them from the fine-tuned to the base model. Correspondingly, patching the KV cache from base to fine-tune can partially or fully remove fine-tuned behavior. Furthermore, we find that the effect of patching grows with how much of the KV cache is patched and concentrates at a few pivotal positions, with system-prompt and chat-template tokens having disproportionate influence.


### [242] [BYOD: Build Your Own DLM - Efficient Autoregressive-to-Diffusion Language Model Conversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41574)
*Ruurd J.A. Kuiper, Robert A. Bagheri*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Unlike autoregressive (AR) models, diffusion language models (DLMs) permit parallel token updates, flexible generation, and bidirectional attention, but leading systems rely on costly pretraining or full-model fine-tuning. We present Build Your Own DLM (BYOD), a generalizable LoRA-only recipe for parameter-efficient AR-to-DLM conversion. Without architecture-specific tuning, BYOD induces diffusion generation in Gemma 2 9B, Llama 3.1 8B, Qwen2.5 7B, and Ministral 8B; to our knowledge, this is the first reported AR-to-DLM conversion of a Mistral model. The adapters comprise 4.1-5.8% of final parameters while every base-model parameter remains frozen; each 25,000-iteration conversion needs at most 102.4M tokens, processed in 3.1-4.0 hours on one Google Colab GPU. All four models generate from fully masked sequences, use bidirectional context in a controlled attention test, and retain substantial parent-model knowledge. BYOD-Gemma is the strongest BYOD conversion on our nine-task evaluation, reaching 50.6% average accuracy compared with 51.9% for LLaDA 8B Instruct in the same harness. Open-generation quality at low network function evaluation (NFE) budgets remains the principal limitation: BYOD perplexity is 8.3-11.1 versus 4.1 for LLaDA and 2.2-3.0 for the AR parents when generating 128 tokens in 64 steps. To enable further experimentation and inspection, we provide a standalone training and evaluation implementation and interactive demos for all four conversions. BYOD thus provides a practical route for extending diffusion generation to other existing model families while identifying the gap that efficient conversion must next close.


### [243] [Peeling the Matryoshka: Extracting Ontological Structure from Retrieval Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41785)
*Eunchan Kim, Inwhee Joe*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Taxonomy construction is essential for knowledge-intensive NLP yet remains costly: clustering-based methods (i) need careful per-corpus tuning and yield unstable hierarchies, while LLM-based pipelines (ii) depend on expensive task-specific training that does not transfer across domains. We report an empirical finding that bears on both: in Matryoshka Representation Learning (MRL) embeddings, where each leading prefix is itself a usable, coarser vector, prefix length aligns with the coarse-to-fine granularity of gold taxonomies; shuffled- and reverse-dimension controls isolate the alignment to the learned early prefix dimensions. The pattern holds across three architecturally distinct MRL models. To test whether this regularity is usable, we introduce PEEL (Peeling Embeddings to Extract Latent ontologies): a minimal probe over these frozen embeddings, trained once with an order-embedding objective that represents hierarchy as vector containment; a minimum spanning arborescence then decodes taxonomic DAGs. Using only a concept vocabulary (no documents), PEEL recovers taxonomic DAGs with high structural fidelity on two OLLM benchmarks, transferring to a new domain with no target-edge supervision. The end-to-end OLLM pipeline, which jointly discovers concepts and induces structure from document corpora, is reported as a cross-task reference. Pre-trained retrieval embeddings encode a decodable granularity gradient; a minimal probe renders it as explicit parent-child (is-a) structure.


### [244] [Text as an Index for Zero-Shot Cross-Domain Knowledge Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41883)
*Yin Yi, Haiping Ma, Siyu Song, Ziwen Wang, Shangshang Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Knowledge tracing (KT) predicts a student's next response from their history. New platforms often have question texts but no response logs for training, motivating zero-shot cross-domain KT. Existing transfer and cold-start methods use question semantics, but cross-platform semantic discrepancies limit transferability. We propose TIZSKT, which separates behavioral sequence modeling from domain-local historical evidence construction and uses text as an index rather than a directly transferable predictive representation. The Mastery-Dynamics Encoder learns correctness dynamics without question identifiers or text embeddings. Domain-Local Question Addressing builds fixed question addresses using a frozen text encoder and an unsupervised per-domain projection. The Text-Addressing Kernel uses these addresses to aggregate the current student's previous outcomes into a question-specific historical correctness summary. Cross-Segment Continuation retains access to earlier responses without extending the behavioral encoder's input sequence. Evidence-Volume Read-Out preserves the total kernel mass discarded by normalization and combines its log-compressed value with the behavioral state and historical correctness summary to predict the next response. All trainable parameters are learned from source domains; target inference uses public question texts and the current student's observed history without target-domain training. Leave-one-domain-out experiments on three public datasets show that TIZSKT achieves the highest mean AUC and accuracy among evaluated baselines on all target domains. Ablations confirm the contributions of text addressing and cross-segment continuation.


<div id='q-bio.BM'></div>

# q-bio.BM [[Back]](#toc)

### [245] [D2ML: Models for Direct-to-Biology Small Molecule Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41070)
*AkshatKumar Nigam, Neri Amara, Hillary Dequina, Dyana Kenanova, Seth Malmersjo, Fiorella Ruggiu, Raymond V. Fucini, Jack Sadowsky, Stig K. Hansen, Imran S. Haque*

Main category: q-bio.BM

Abstract: Direct-to-biology (D2B) screening enables rapid testing of newly synthesized molecules without purification. However, uncertain concentrations in crude mixtures and limited purified-compound measurements complicate the use of these data in machine learning. Here we show that models tailored to the structure of D2B data can improve prediction and guide the discovery of more potent and selective compounds. Our assay-aware models predict activity from single-point screens, relative potency across targets from crude dose-response assays, and absolute potency from purified-compound measurements. For crude assays, potency ratios between targets tested from the same well can retain selectivity information despite uncertain concentrations. A joint model that shares information across stages and balances their contributions to training improved predictions over separate neural networks. The largest gain was in crude dose-response selectivity prediction, where rank correlation increased from 0.27 to 0.40. In a separate experiment, independently trained models guided the selection of 84 new compounds to improve ROS1 potency while limiting activity against the anti-target TRKB. Synthesis and testing identified compounds that extended the measured Pareto front, achieving better combinations of potency and selectivity than those in the training data. The most selective new compound achieved a ROS1 IC50 of 14.7 nM and 233-fold selectivity over TRKB. By comparison, the most selective training compound had a ROS1 IC50 of 20.3 nM and 79-fold selectivity. ATLAS provides the experimental setting for this approach, supporting synthesis and screening of 1,000-10,000 model-selected compounds in two- to three-week cycles. Together, these results show how assay-aware learning can use the full D2B assay sequence to guide multi-objective molecular optimization.


<div id='cs.ET'></div>

# cs.ET [[Back]](#toc)

### [246] [ZenKO: Guided and Iterative Quantum-Classical Method for Molecular Electronic Structure using Sample-Based Quantum Diagonalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41146)
*Aditya Ranjan, Devesh Tiwari*

Main category: cs.ET

Abstract: The electronic structure of molecules is foundational for scientific advancement, however determining it remains challenging due to the growth of the molecular Hilbert space with system size. Recent approaches address this by using quantum circuits to sample candidate configurations and sample-based quantum diagonalization (SQD) to compute the ground state. We propose ZenKO, a novel gradient-based, iterative SQD method that employs a hybrid quantum-classical approach. ZenKO decomposes configuration selection into discovery, validation, and refinement, enabling targeted subspace expansion and guided exploration. Our evaluation demonstrates that, across $H_2O$, $H_{10}$, and $N_2$ molecules, ZenKO improves ground-state accuracy compared to prior iterative methods, while significantly reducing classical cost.


<div id='cs.CE'></div>

# cs.CE [[Back]](#toc)

### [247] [Learning Nonlocal Interactions in Large-Scale Heterogeneous Metamaterials through Scientific Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41887)
*Dex Doksoo Lee, Jehyeon Shin, Yue Yu, Sunkyu Yu, Junsuk Rho, Wei Chen*

Main category: cs.CE

Abstract: In multiscale systems, nonlocal interactions among spatially distributed constituents can unlock collective, potentially emergent behavior irreducible to individual constituent responses. Beyond characterization, nonlocality in multiscale systems is increasingly viewed as a design element across fracture mechanics, wave physics, and disordered systems. Existing nonlocality theories provide rigorous descriptions and mechanistic insight, but often require prescribed analytic forms, range, symmetries, or periodicity. Machine learning offers a data-driven route to learn interactions, yet principled approaches that tie the network design to physical theory remain nascent. Here we present domain-decomposition attention neural operator (DANO), which learns configuration-dependent interactions in heterogeneous systems governed by linear field equations from field data alone. Through a case study in large-scale optical metasurfaces, we demonstrate DANO's full-field prediction under geometric heterogeneity and across drive conditions, small-to-large domain transfer, and multifunctional inverse design, with learned interactions validated through physics-based interrogation. As an AI-for-Science tool, it supports interaction discovery and scalable, customizable design of nonlocal multiscale systems, including virtual-reality optics.


<div id='cs.CR'></div>

# cs.CR [[Back]](#toc)

### [248] [PrivacyFlow: Privacy in AI Agents through Information Flow Analysis and Consequence-Aware Risk Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40714)
*Robin Staab, Tom Wollschläger, Vahab Mirrokni, Reza Shokri*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Autonomous LLM agents executing multi-step workflows over personal data often misalign with user privacy expectations and leak sensitive, out-of-context information. Although Contextual Integrity (CI) formalizes this problem, existing benchmarks and defenses rarely operationalize CI for realistic multi-step task sequences in evolving environments. In this work, we first introduce agentic PrivacyBench, a benchmark of 5873 tasks in 986 task sequences with dynamic environment updates and CI-grounded allow-sets for sensitive information. We also introduce PrivacyFlow, a defense that separates deliberative privacy planning (System 2) from task execution (System 1), treating each sharing decision as a structured risk-management problem. Before execution, PrivacyFlow traces the provenance and purpose of each planned data flow, models recipient background knowledge, and simulates what external actors could infer. This allows us to model the downstream consequences of disclosure, including inference attacks and context-dependent harms. It then weighs the remaining disclosure risk against task utility and executes a minimized plan, blocks it, or consults the user. Across six models, PrivacyFlow significantly reduces step-level privacy failures and raises the share of completed task sequences without a detected privacy violation by 22--55 percentage points. We further highlight a new privacy--utility--autonomy tradeoff: allowing PrivacyFlow to proactively prompt the user in case of uncertainty can increase authorized task completion at the cost of shifting decision effort back to the user. Our results suggest that proactive CI reasoning can substantially improve agentic privacy and motivate further study of this tradeoff.


### [249] [Characterizing LLM Agent Behaviors in Module Shadowing Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40716)
*Shuai Yang, Enmeng Lu, Jinyu Fan, Gongce Cao, Yu Chen, Jiawei Xie, Feifei Zhao, Zhangsheng Wang, Yongyu Jiang, Yi Zeng*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: LLM agents operating in untrusted workspaces face the risk of untrusted code execution during routine tasks, yet final reports and workspace states alone cannot reliably establish whether execution occurred. We introduce an evaluation framework that codes observation, reasoning, execution, and response behaviors from agent trajectories and aligns them with independent environmental records to reconstruct execution histories and verify final reports. We apply this framework to module shadowing, in which a local file is loaded in place of a trusted module during import, enabling untrusted code execution without adversarial prompts. Across 90 trials with 15 models, the payload executed in 51% of direct shadowing trials and 87% of indirect shadowing trials. Even explicit recognition of the shadowing mechanism did not ensure prevention, as the payload ran in 10 of the 29 trials with recorded recognition. These failures were often omitted from final reports, as 49 of the 62 triggered trials ended without mention of the security issue. Three reports even denied execution, including one from a trial in which independent logs recorded 19 payload executions. Final workspace states also obscured prior execution when agents deleted or overwrote the payload marker in 10 triggered trials. These findings demonstrate that combining behavioral coding with independent environmental auditing enables rigorous and transparent evaluation of agent safety.


### [250] [MetaVul: Agentic Harness Discovery for Vulnerability Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40721)
*Ira Ceka, Arnav Garg, Ryan Lutz, Ting Zhang, Ratnadira Widyasari, Gail Kaiser, Baishakhi Ray*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly used to automate vulnerability detection, but reliable detection is challenging because agents must distinguish subtle, security-relevant fixes from benign code variation. While underlying LLM capability has improved significantly, the accuracy of the agent still depends heavily on the harness: the code that defines the scaffolding around the LLM. Harnesses are typically fixed, and largely hand-written, limiting adaptation to evaluator feedback. Additionally, when harnesses are static, improvements on one dataset or model may not transfer to others. To address these challenges, we introduce MetaVul, a self-improving framework for harness discovery in vulnerability detection. Starting from a seed structured reasoning scaffold, MetaVul uses a workflow-based optimization loop to iteratively refine the harness using feedback from paired vulnerable and patched functions, while preserving the structured reasoning and verdict format. At each iteration, it searches over the history of prior harnesses, with access to their trajectories, logs, and metrics, and it returns a set of Pareto-optimal harnesses. We apply MetaVul to discover agent harnesses for various architectures. A validation set (202 pairs) is used for optimization, while an unseen dataset (430 pairs) is kept as the held-out set. We test whether harnesses learned from one model transfer to others not seen during optimization. Results on both frontier and small LLMs show that the optimized harnesses improve pairwise vulnerability detection up to 23.3 percentage points on the validation set and up to 25.8 points on the held-out set with a Pareto-frontier harness. Harnesses learned with one model also transfer to others. We show that harnesses optimized with one model transfer to another, improving held-out pairwise accuracy by 14.7 points without further optimization.


### [251] [RiskyPy: Toward Model-Calibrated Prompt Generation for Joint Security and Functionality Evaluation of LLM-Generated Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40735)
*Zacharie Chenail-Larcher, Daniele Lunghi, Melissa Tessa, Quentin Stiévenart, Jacques Klein, Tegawendé F. Bissyandé, Florent Avellaneda, Naouel Moha*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Evaluating approaches that improve the security of LLM code generation requires datasets that jointly assess security and functionality and remain adapted to the capabilities of the models being studied. However, existing benchmarks are costly to produce and are generally not calibrated to a chosen range of model capabilities. We introduce RiskyPy, an automated pipeline that generates security-oriented code-generation datasets calibrated to model proficiency using functionality and vulnerability canaries. We instantiate RiskyPy for Python using domains covering 18 CWEs from the 2025 CWE Top 25, producing 101 prompts. Experiments on Qwen3-4B and Seed-Coder-8B show that the generated tasks remain functionally accessible, with 57-72% of prompts yielding functional code, while only 5-10% of generations are free of Bandit findings, leaving room for joint evaluation of security and functionality of security improvement approaches.


### [252] [CLIC: Closed-Loop Intent Consistency for Securing LLM-Driven Control in Open RAN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40744)
*Sunder Ali Khowaja, Kapal Dev, Joongheon Kim, Angelos Antonopoulos, Nikolaos Bartzoudis*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: With the progressive capabilities of large language models (LLMs), researchers are finding ways to integrate them into the control loops of Open Radio Access Network (O-RAN) to enable slicing and resource management through user intents. However, integration of LLMs in the O-RAN control loop via the RAN Intelligent Controller (RIC) makes the system vulnerable to \textit{runtime prompt injection threats}, which manipulate control decisions without violating interface authentication, and \textit{residual virtual prompt injection (VPI)} attacks that plant trigger mechanisms to silently steer the behaviour during fine-tuning. Existing studies address only the runtime threat and do not enforce consistency across the full control path, i.e. from the certified A1 intent, through the multi-step LLM trajectory, to the final E2 action. We propose a closed-loop intent-consistency layer (CLIC) that jointly mitigates both threats. The proposed CLIC defines a formal consistency score using a small quantized language model, which is deployed as a Non-RT rApp for model-integrity certification along with a Near-RT xApp for runtime enforcement. We implement CLIC on the OpenAI Cellular (OAIC) platform and evaluate it under domain-adapted JailbreakBench, AdvBench, and VPI workloads. The experimental results show that the proposed CLIC reduces the attack success rate from 0.92 to 0.29 and residual VPI attacks from 0.37 to 0.04, while improving isolation and fairness at slightly higher latency costs, i.e. approximately 27 ms.


### [253] [Safin-1.5: Safety-Native Architecture and Verifiable Reasoning for Recursive Safety Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40751)
*Jingnan Zheng, Cheng Jin, Ming Zhang, Huan Sun, Zhongtian Ma, Kaisen Yang, Ziyuan Zhou, Tong Wu, Dongcheng Zhang, Yi Zhang, Xiao Wang, Han Qi, Shu Yu, Ermo Hua, Zongji Yu, Chaozhuo Li, Xiaoyu Wen, Zhekai Chen, Jiawei Zhou, Sirui Chen, Ning Ding, An Zhang, Tat-Seng Chua, Xiangnan He, Xia Hu, Bowen Zhou, Qiaosheng Zhang, Youbang Sun, Xiang Wang, Zhongyu Huang, Chaochao Lu*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) enables large language models to advance their capabilities through successive updates, but these updates can also introduce new risks and weaken existing safeguards. In line with the AI-45° Law, we pursue Safety RSI, in which safety develops as an intrinsic capability alongside general model capabilities through continual risk discovery and repair, while preserving utility and limiting over-refusal. We introduce Safin-1.5, a model built on a safety architecture and trained through recursive safety improvement, with an inference-time harness that extends its capabilities to formal reasoning. Our approach comprises three components: i) The safety architecture combines persistent safety states with hazardous-knowledge disentanglement. The former retains and selectively invokes safety knowledge, while the latter relocates targeted hazardous knowledge into removable experts, enabling its removal with minimal impact on general capabilities. ii) Recursive safety improvement establishes a discovery-evaluation-repair loop. The loop identifies new vulnerabilities and uses experimental feedback to develop repair strategies. It accepts updates only when they improve safety while preserving general capabilities and limiting over-refusal. iii) The formal reasoning harness combines the model's language understanding with symbolic inference and verification. Proof-obligation-guided semantic bridging recovers missing relations in formal representations, enabling symbolic solvers to construct checkable proofs under formalized premises and accepted semantic bridges. Experimental results demonstrate that Safin-1.5 achieves safety and reasoning performance, outperforming frontier models.


### [254] ["Shall We Approve?" Consequence-Aware Approval for Autonomous Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40753)
*Weiheng Wu, Junkai Chen, Zhensu Sun, Jieke Shi, Yunbo Lyu, David Lo*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Coding agents increasingly rely on automated approvers to decide whether a proposed shell command may run. Existing approvers, from rule-based checkers to guardrail models and deployed permission classifiers, base their decisions on the text of the request and command. Yet a command's behavior depends on the code it invokes and the state of the workspace, and systems that observe file changes leave the assessment to the agent or a human after execution. We present Caper, a consequence-aware approval mechanism that pre-executes each command in a snapshotted sandbox, provides an independent approver with a bounded report of its observed effects, commits allowed commands, and rolls back blocked ones with a reason the agent can act on. Compared with six existing systems, Caper achieves the lowest harmful-outcome rate on SABER (6.7% versus at least 14.8%) and YoloFS-Ext, a benchmark we build in which harmful behavior is hidden inside invoked tools (7.8% versus at least 35.3%), while blocking 1.0% of the safe twins of the dangerous tasks in YoloFS-Ext.


### [255] [Accept Changes, Reject Attacks: Robust Memory Management for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41056)
*Runze Zhao, Dongruo Zhou, Sumit Kumar Jha, Nathaniel D. Bastian, Ankit Shah*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Since large language model (LLM) agents rely on long-term memory, what they store now shapes what they do later. A memory manager decides how each input changes this memory and which records the agent acts on. It can fail in two ways: keeping a fact that has since changed (outdated memory), or storing a planted passage or injected instruction (memory poisoning). Most prior work studies them separately, yet they call for opposite write decisions, so no fixed write rule handles both. We therefore formalize memory robustness as handling both without doing worse on other inputs. Since accuracy checks only the answer, we measure robustness with evidence-conditioned accuracy (ECA), which also checks that the records the manager selects include all the evidence the answer needs, updated and free of attacks. However, existing memory systems and defenses are not more robust than an untuned manager on our test sets. We improve robustness with a simple one-shot recipe that fine-tunes the manager on episodes synthesized around a single conversation with inserted changes and attacks. On three backbones, it improves every category score on our test sets and more than doubles the untuned manager's overall ECA, though robustness remains incomplete.


### [256] [ANGLER: Verifier-Grounded Benchmarking with an Adaptive Attacker for Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41272)
*Yini Huang, Aiwen LU, Zhen Sun, Yi Han, Yuzhi Zhao, Jiaheng Wei*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Web agents are increasingly deployed to complete tasks autonomously on real websites, where adversarially injected content can induce them to perform unauthorized actions. Existing evaluations primarily use fixed attacks or focus on a single attack type, and evaluate attacks at the text level rather than through real state changes, thus underestimating how vulnerable agents are to diverse attacks. To address the gap, we introduce REEF, a benchmark of red-teaming executable environments with faithful verification for measuring the security vulnerabilities of web agents. REEF covers 9 verifier-grounded websites spanning travel, commerce, productivity, media, and communication, and pairs every authentic benign task with a malicious objective delivered through 5 attack types at both the user and the environment level. REEF's state-based verifiers provide the execution feedback that drives ANGLER, an adaptive near-miss-guided lure evolution framework for red-teaming. Rather than evaluating a fixed attack set, ANGLER uses outcomes from prior rollouts to guide LLM-based attack mutation and candidate selection, prioritizing near-miss seeds during refinement. Across 8 VLM-based web agents, ANGLER achieves an end-to-end attack success rate (ASR) of 20.36%, compared with 15.24% for the non-adaptive setting. Compared with attack-free execution on the same benign tasks, attacks generated by ANGLER reduce benign task success rate (BSR) from 61.9% to 44.4%.


### [257] [Erase the Injected Region: Multimodal Prompt-Injection Defense with On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41307)
*Shuo Hong, Jiaxin Ma, Xidi Cai, Qianli Ma*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Multimodal indirect prompt injection embeds malicious instructions in external media that also contains information needed for the user's task. Existing defenses can block the attack yet impair the model's use of that information, leaving a gap between attack defense and task preservation. We propose ERASE, an on-policy self-distillation method that constructs its reference by erasing the injected region in place. Modality-appropriate blank content replaces the injection while the surrounding media is preserved. A student reading attacked media learns from a frozen base model reading the erased view through full-distribution token-level feedback on its own rollouts. Across image, audio, and video on two model sizes, ERASE records near-zero observed attack success on the evaluated benchmark while retaining task utility close to the undefended clean-input level. On 3B audio, ERASE reaches 60.1% attacked-input accuracy compared with ARGUS's 33.1%, within 0.5 points of the undefended clean-input level. Experiments show that ERASE reduces injection dependence from 6.26 to 0.0095 on 3B audio while staying close to the base model on the erased input. Out-of-domain accuracy also stays near the base level. Our code will be made publicly available upon acceptance.


### [258] [When Safety Checks Become Privacy Risks: A Privacy-Preserving Paradigm for LLM Agent Safety Auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41401)
*Yixiang Luo, Ziheng Ma, Ke Chang, Jian Wang, Jiqiang Liu*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Large language model agents interact with online services through tool calls that may access private data or perform sensitive actions. Specialized providers can supply and maintain pre-execution safety auditing capabilities for agent operators. However, sending task descriptions, call arguments, and interaction histories to an external auditor may expose sensitive user information. In this work, we propose a paradigm for privacy-preserving safety auditing that separates delegated audit computation from plaintext access and execution authority. Grounded in a three-domain auditing model, the paradigm connects trusted representation construction, encrypted audit computation, and trusted decision and execution through explicit interfaces. These interfaces accommodate alternative representations and scorers, while encrypted computation protects audit inputs from a semi-honest auditor. We instantiate this paradigm in SealCall, a framework combining learned audit representations, CKKS-based encrypted similarity computation, and execution control bound to audited tool calls. Evaluated on 1,252 public binary candidates, SealCall achieves a mean F1 score of 0.840 under grouped nested evaluation, while preserving plaintext audit decisions in all measured cases. A representative configuration completes each encrypted scoring operation in 0.176 s.


### [259] [Simple Features Beat Most AI Control Monitors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41713)
*Shubhrangshu Debsarkar, Valmik Nahata*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: AI control relies on monitors to catch a capable model that sabotages its work. These monitors are evaluated on their ability to differentiate honest from backdoored code. We show that on a standard evaluation dataset, simple features beat most monitors without ever running the code, outranking 20 of 24 open-weight models prompted as monitors. The dataset gives its labels away in two ways. People wrote the honest solutions and older language models the backdoors, so the style of the code reveals the class. Additionally, problems that LLMs can backdoor have different characteristics, so basic facts about the problem reveal it too. Swapping one class at a time for code by Claude Sonnet 5 shows that these features reward a contrast between authors, since human code against Sonnet 5's backdoors is easier to separate than the original pairs, 0.95 against 0.79. One in seven backdoors in the standard test set fails our validity checks, which distorts comparisons between attackers. A Sonnet 5 monitor appears to catch 77% of the older models' backdoors but 37% of Sonnet 5's, and 78% against 61% once both are verified. We do not show that published monitors rely on these shortcuts, but our findings suggest that a high score on such a dataset is not by itself evidence of sabotage detection. We release the rebuilt dataset and checks.


### [260] [SecPO: Principled Adversarial Training for Prompt Injection Security](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41998)
*Sizhe Chen, Yibo Peng, Jaewon Chang, Chawin Sitawarin, David Wagner*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Recognized as a top threat to AI agents, prompt injection allows instructions from untrusted data (email, documents, websites) to manipulate the agent's operation. The existing best-defended LLM, Meta-SecAlign, is trained with the DPO loss but still suffers from attack success rates (ASRs) approaching 100% under adaptive prompt injections. One reason is that DPO anchors the model around the undefended reference model, which is insecure. We show an idealized reference model---an injection-invariant, utility-preserving oracle---can nevertheless be approximated, as its output distribution in training is interestingly accessible. This new observation motivates a theoretically principled SecPO loss. Another Meta-SecAlign shortcoming is that it trains on weak simulated attacks. We implement an efficient adversarial training (AT) pipeline that synthesizes strong injected inputs (best@240), and train the model on them. Our AT reduces ASRs by 1.5-14.5$\times$ across three SoTA adaptive attacks, compared with Meta-SecAlign; the SecPO loss reduces them by a further 2.4-4.6$\times$ compared with using DPO loss. In agentic workflows, our SecPO + AT Qwen3.6-27B is much more secure than Meta-SecAlign: 8.8% vs. 78.8% AgentDojo ASR@800 under the AttackerMovesSecond attack. Moreover, our defended model shows no noticeable reduction in benign utility in nine benchmarks covering agentic tool calling, instruction following, science, and math. We release our $\href{//github.com/Sizhe-Chen/SecPO}{\text{code}}$, $\href{//huggingface.co/datasets/Sizhe-Chen/Qwen3.6-27B-Instruct-SecPO-trainset}{\text{training data}}$ (with optimized injections from AT), and $\href{//huggingface.co/Sizhe-Chen/Qwen3.6-27B-Instruct-SecPO}{\text{model}}$.


### [261] [An Incentive-Aligned Trust-Centric Coordination Layer for Heterogeneous Blockchain Interoperability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41032)
*Saeed Moradi, Sara Rouhani*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: The interoperability between permissioned and public blockchains is hampered by asymmetric privacy, trust, and governance assumptions. We propose a coordination architecture that decouples cross-domain trust management and privacy enforcement from underlying blockchain protocols, requiring no protocol-level modifications. The framework integrates cryptographic unlinkability mechanisms with incentive-compatible relay selection, combining stake-weighted participation and temporally accumulated reputation to align economic commitment with sustained correctness. We formalize the adversarial model and derive security and privacy properties for cross-domain coordination, demonstrating robustness against Sybil infiltration, collusion, and strategic stake concentration. Simulation-based analysis characterizes key design trade-offs between quorum thresholds, reputation memory, and anonymity parameters, indicating that privacy-preserving coordination can be achieved without degrading responsiveness or incentive stability when parameters are properly tuned.


### [262] [FGSM Attack Transferability from Simulation to Quantum Hardware in Network Intrusion Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40701)
*Zeeshan Ajmal, Kimmo Halunen, Marc Maußner, Volker Reers, Arif Ali Khan*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Quantum computers are now available through cloud platforms, and recent work has proposed using them to detect network intrusions. It is not yet clear how easily such systems can be attacked. We train a hybrid variational quantum classifier (VQC) on the Friday DDoS subset of CICIDS2017 and test it against two attacks: label flipping during training and FGSM evasion at prediction time. We run the tests in simulation and on IBM hardware, the Fez superconducting processor (Heron r2). We find that two models with similar accuracy can differ greatly in how easily they are attacked. A model that decides with little confidence loses half its detections to a very small change in the input, while a model trained to decide with more confidence needs a much larger change before it fails. Attacks built in simulation against the more confident model also work on real hardware, and the device classifies every unaltered attack sample correctly. Simulated device noise does not reduce accuracy, but it does reduce the model's confidence and makes attacks easier, so accuracy alone does not show how exposed a model is. Label flipping has no measurable effect until 15% of attack labels are changed. These results show that device noise should not be treated as a defense and that reported weaknesses in these classifiers depend partly on how they were trained.


### [263] [When AI Investigators Cannot Trust Their Tools: Conflict-Aware Evidence Verification for Cyber Threat Investigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41191)
*Yuchen Zhang*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly triage security alerts by combining the outputs of several detection tools, implicitly treating those tools as trustworthy. Unlike prior work on conflicts between a model's knowledge and its context, we study conflicts among the tools themselves: what happens when this machine-generated evidence is conflicting or wrong. On the corrected CIC-IDS2017 dataset, four heterogeneous detectors emit labels and confidences that we corrupt under a threat model varying the number of corrupted tools, the persistence of the corruption, and the confidence attached to wrong outputs. Across open-weight LLMs from 3B to 14B parameters, unverified investigators are fragile: one corrupted detector out of four lowers naive accuracy to .50-.72, three lower every unverified investigator to .13 or less, and honest disagreement alone makes them abstain on up to 63% of flows. We propose Conflict-Aware Evidence Verification (CAV), built from established components: an equal-weight vote that ignores reported confidences, an evidence-conflict gate, and an independent nearest-neighbour trust check that tests the leading candidates. With its prompt frozen before testing, CAV-LLM improves on the naive investigator in every corrupted condition ($p < 0.001$), reaching .84-.88 with three corrupted detectors against at most .08, while calling the LLM on 0.4% of clean traffic. A fixed verification rule is as accurate or better unless most tools are corrupted. When we make the independent channel itself noisy, CAV inherits its errors: it stays ahead when two or more tools are corrupted, but adding the channel as an extra voter is better when at most one is. Verification thus moves trust from the tools to the check, which must be measured and protected in turn.


### [264] [DeepWeave: Weaving Neural and Symbolic Evidence for ML-Based Network Intrusion Detection System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41541)
*Raoul Saad, Enkeleda Bardhi, Andrea Agiollo, Fernando Kuipers*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Machine learning-based network intrusion detection systems can recognize complex traffic patterns, but often require substantial labelled attack data, degrade under deployment shift, and provide limited evidence for analysts. We present DeepWeave, a probabilistic neuro-symbolic Network Intrusion Detection System (NIDS) that makes logic an integral part of detection. DeepWeave automatically constructs and validates interpretable rules, represents neural predictions as probabilistic predicates, and reconciles both evidence sources through explicit coupling policies over packet-, flow-, and hybrid-views. The resulting proofs provide intrinsic explanations for rule-supported alerts. Across multiple datasets, DeepWeave improves low-data detection, false-positive control, transferability, and robustness. With only 50 labelled packet observations per attack class, it improves macro-F1 from 0.632 to 0.924 and reduces the attack false-positive rate from 0.075 to 0.008 over the matched neural baseline. Its proofs are also more stable than post-hoc neural attributions, while batched inference keeps probabilistic reasoning practical. These results demonstrate label-efficient, adaptable, and inspectable neuro-symbolic intrusion detection.


### [265] [From Training Exposure to Membership Evidence: A Controlled Study of OLMo 2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41732)
*Ali Banaei, Muhammad Shahab Shahzad, Gerald Q. Maguire Jr., Marco Chiesa, Dejan Kostić*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Whether a language model was trained on a document matters for copyright, privacy, and benchmark contamination. Yet most membership-inference (MI) evaluations on language models collect members and non-members after the fact, so success may reflect distribution shift and failure is inconclusive. We re-ran OLMo 2 1B's official 50-billion-token mid-training stage, replacing selected training chunks with documents from 7,814 pairs matched on domain and difficulty. For each pair, a coin flip determined which document was inserted and which was held out. The run released by the Allen Institute for AI (AI2) from the same seed saw identical data everywhere else and serves as the counterfactual. One exposure raises a previously unseen document's likelihood on average but rarely identifies it. Using the counterfactual as reference, the reference ratio has an area under the ROC curve (AUC) of 0.767 but, with one threshold across domains, catches 3.8% of members at a 1% false-positive rate (FPR). In contrast, 13 matched pairs give a paired test 80% power at the 5% level to detect training on a collection, given same-domain non-members and a close reference. A reference trained with another seed recovers 92% of the counterfactual's AUC advantage over chance, whereas reference-free attacks stay within 0.07 of chance when pooled over domains. The full official post-training recipe, re-run on both models, leaves 89% of the average effect. Even with a closely matched reference, substantial document-level variability limits individual membership inference. We will release the checkpoints, per-token log-probabilities, and the evaluation documents; the code is available at this https URL .


### [266] [What Standard Evaluations Hide About Detectors of Hijacked LLM Agents: Rare Attacks, Unseen Tools and Benchmark Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41803)
*Mahmoud Abbasi, Juan M. Núñez V., Amin Shahraki*

Main category: cs.CR

Abstract: Detectors can flag hijacked large language model (LLM) agents from the traces of the tools they call. In the eleven evaluations we audited, attacks or anomalies make up 16% to 86% of the test samples, no evaluation varies that share, and six report no test on data from a second benchmark or source. We ask what such evaluations hide. On the AgentDrift and AgentDojo benchmarks we test trained detectors that read tool identities, behavior, call sequences, step embeddings or text, and a released LLM action monitor, AlignmentCheck, with thresholds fixed on training data or set by the monitor's released decision rule. First, at a 2% attack rate a detector with an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) of 0.879 reaches a precision of 0.076, and the mix of negatives alone moves that precision between 0.039 and 0.117. Second, as tool coverage, the share of test tool calls whose tool was seen in training, falls, detector rankings cross near 50% coverage and reverse below it, because unseen tools map to inputs that training never reached. Dropout on tool inputs largely removes the reversal. Third, no detector trained on AgentDrift ranks AgentDojo traces better than chance (AUROC 0.47 to 0.52), including one that reaches 0.996 within AgentDojo. The monitor, trained on neither, reaches 0.930, but with its open backbone its released decision rule would still mostly raise false alerts at a 2% attack rate (precision 0.096). We turn these findings into a checklist for evaluating trace detectors.


<div id='cs.HC'></div>

# cs.HC [[Back]](#toc)

### [267] [AutoAnalysis: An automated, self-updating meta-analysis of human-AI collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42001)
*Michelle Vaccaro, Abdullah Almaatouq, Thomas W. Malone*

Main category: cs.HC

Abstract: As generative artificial intelligence (AI) becomes more capable and widely used, understanding when and how humans should work with these systems is becoming increasingly important. However, the empirical evidence in this space offers conflicting recommendations, which leaves individuals and organizations without clear guidance on when to combine human and AI capabilities. Existing meta-analyses can help reconcile these findings, but they require substantial manual effort and cannot readily incorporate new evidence as it emerges. To address this challenge, we introduce AutoAnalysis: an automated, self-updating meta-analysis of when humans and AI work better together, and when they work better alone. AutoAnalysis' most recent run synthesizes 1,955 effect sizes from 420 papers. Across this body of work, we found that, on average, human-AI combinations performed significantly better than humans alone, but they performed significantly worse than the better of humans or AI alone. These findings highlight the important distinction between improving human performance and achieving human-AI synergy and underscore the need to evaluate collaboration against both human and AI performance alone. As new studies emerge, AutoAnalysis will integrate their findings into the meta-analysis, providing cumulative evidence about when humans and AI perform better together and how human-AI performance varies across AI agents, tasks, and processes.


### [268] [A prospective randomized study of patient-centered AI for clinical visit preparation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41437)
*Xincheng Tan, Matthew Thompson, Xiang Ji, Angelica Willis, Will Vaughan, Roma Ruparel, Michael A. Sanchez, Sean Chang, Joseph Xu, Bhavna Daryani, Preeti Singh, Pinal Bavishi, Kejia Chen, Fan Zhang, Priya Gupta, Ernest Lim, Christina Chen, Bob Lou, Dimitrios Antos, Zach Wasson, Ray Luo, Fadi Yousif, Florence Thng, Mark Malhotra, Joseph Breda, Daniel McDuff, Kavi Goel, Katherine Chou, Dale R. Webster, Avinatan Hassidim, Yossi Matias, Po-Hsuan Cameron Chen, Yun Liu, Mike Schaekermann, Quang Duong, Jackie M. Barr*

Main category: cs.HC

Abstract: Patients often arrive at brief, time-constrained clinical visits unsure of what to expect or ask, and millions of adults now turn to general-purpose AI chatbots to prepare beforehand. Yet whether conversational AI improves visit readiness remains unclear. We designed a large language model (LLM)-based visit preparation agent to guide patients before an appointment by gathering clinical context, setting visit expectations, providing relevant health information, building a prioritized list of questions for their clinician, and generating a summary. In a prospective consented randomized study in the Google Health app, 802 U.S. adults held 866 health-related conversations with the visit preparation agent (standard or concise variant) or a minimally prompted control chatbot built on the same LLM. The visit preparation agent significantly outperformed the control across blinded clinician evaluations and patient self-reports. In clinician reviews of completed sessions (n = 272), the agent achieved a 16.6% higher rate of comprehensive preparation (67.2% vs. 50.6%; P < 0.05) and an 8.5% higher rate of alignment with patients' health context (98.9% vs. 90.4%; P < 0.01), while maintaining high medical safety and appropriateness across all study arms. Patient post-surveys (n = 460) showed 16.8% higher visit preparedness (73.8% vs. 57.0%; P < 0.01), 14.3% higher confidence in knowing what to ask (81.4% vs. 67.1%; P < 0.01), and 12.0% higher overall satisfaction (74.0% vs. 62.0%; P < 0.05). Clinician and participant metrics were not significantly correlated, indicating that expert review and patient self-assessment capture complementary dimensions of patient-facing AI. These findings suggest that LLM chatbots can safely help patients prepare for clinical visits, with greater benefit when following a structured workflow, and motivate future studies on in-visit communication and clinical outcomes.


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [269] [When Disclosure Is Compulsory: Evaluating Ride-Hailing Rebalancing Policies with Self-Interested LLM Driver Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41132)
*Xinyu Wang, Andres Fielbaum*

Main category: cs.MA

Abstract: The EU Platform Work Directive, to be transposed by December 2026, requires ride-hailing platforms to state in plain language the main parameters of their automated decisions and how a worker's behaviour affects them. Such rules are exactly what a large language model (LLM) can read, so drivers will be able to let one decide for them. We compare two main families of rebalancing policy once their rules are public: a reactive policy (R) that sends idle drivers to cover requests just lost, and an anticipatory policy (A) that plans moves on a demand forecast. Each driver is an LLM agent maximizing its own net income, given no rules (N) or the rules at the directive's level of detail (L). We test two LLMs, Claude Haiku 5.5 and Jev, which understand the rules equally well, in a three-zone model and in Manhattan. With the rules disclosed, drivers follow the platform more closely: in Manhattan, the share of relocation offers they decline falls from about four in five to one in four under both policies. Yet the effects differ by policy. For the platform, disclosure costs R 3.7% of its profit in Manhattan, as R now pays for moves drivers used to make for free, while A's profit is unchanged; the two policies, which tie on profit under full compliance, end 6.1% apart. For drivers, disclosure raises income in the small model under both policies and LLMs; for users, it raises the service rate there by 10 to 18 percentage points. In Manhattan, self-interested drivers also make A a win-win-win against full compliance, raising driver income, platform profit and service rate by 10 to 12% each. Rebalancing policies that perform equally well under full compliance can thus show opposite results under mandatory disclosure. The study offers a framework to evaluate such policies under the new requirement and is, to our knowledge, the first to do so with LLM agents.


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [270] [TAPE: Learning General Audio Effect Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40759)
*Phillip Long, Yongyi Zang, Qihui Yang, Christodoulos Benetatos, Junfei Liu, Randal Leistikow, Julian McAuley, Perry R. Cook*

Main category: cs.SD

Abstract: Musicians and mixing engineers often can decipher the signal processing chain they hear on a reference recording, then adapt it to new material. We present TAPE (Transformations of Audio Parameter Embeddings), which estimates this processing from the processed reference audio alone. An encoder trained to identify processors in eight major effect families maps the reference to an embedding. A differentiable digital signal processing (DSP) chain decodes the embedding into editable parameters in one forward pass and applies them to a different unprocessed recording. Its modules are equalization, compression, waveshaping, a small residual network, and reverberation. TAPE achieves lower spectral error than DeepAFx-ST, the previous state of the art for single-step regression, and lower spectral error than per-example search over a chain approximating ST-ITO's, while running 275$\times$ faster than that search. Because the decoder outputs DSP parameters, its chain can also run causally: a C++ implementation with the trained weights processed 128-sample buffers 1.8$\times$ faster than real time, with no missed deadlines.


### [271] [From prediction error to sparse events: Biologically inspired signed-dyadic coding for Mandarin keyword spotting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41381)
*Zijian Zhou, Rui Liu, Rongxiang Nie, Rui Ma, Ziyao Zhang, Xin Du*

Main category: cs.SD

Abstract: In continuous spiking keyword spotting, temporally correlated acoustic frames can repeatedly trigger memory access and neural computation at a dense input interface, even when hidden activity is sparse. Predictive processing and sensory adaptation suggest retaining predictable input locally and responding selectively to deviations. Inspired by this mechanism, we propose Predictive Signed-Dyadic Coding (PG). PG maintains a local prediction for each input channel and transmits an event only when the residual exceeds a learned threshold. Each event carries a sign and a finite power-of-two magnitude, and the receiver reconstructs it with fixed-point shifts and additions. In Mandarin keyword spotting, INPUT-PG reaches 90.00% accuracy while reducing mean SRAM reads by 33.87% and inference cycles by 32.77%. PG provides a hardware interface for research on low-power continuous speech processing and event-based sensory systems.


### [272] [Apriel Voice 27B Preview: An Open Model for Speech, Reasoning, and Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40738)
*Aman Tiwari, Akshay Kalkunte, Akintunde Oladipo, Jash Shah, Tara Bogavelli, Hoang Nguyen, Nishanth Madhusudhan, Vishal Juneja, Sridhar Nemala, Srinivas Sunkara, Sathwik Tejaswi Madhusudhan, Shruthan Radhakrishna, Masoud Hashemi, Kelechi Ogueji, Khalil Slimi, Saloni Mittal, Pulkit Pattnaik, Shashank Maiya, Dhruv Jhamb, Esakkivel Esakkiraja, Katrina Stankiewicz, Gabrielle Gauthier Melançon, Fanny Riols, Amit Saha, Lakshmi Sirisha Chodisetty, Nitin Aggarwal, Anil Madamala, Sagar Davasam, Segan Subramanian, Vikas Yadav, Sai Rajeswar Mudumba*

Main category: cs.SD

Abstract: A capable voice assistant must do more than recognize words; it must understand requests, reason over context, sustain conversations, and act through tools. We present Apriel Voice 27B Preview, an open speech-language model that unifies these capabilities within a shared reasoning backbone while retaining text and image input modalities. Built on the post-trained Gemma 4 26B-A4B checkpoint, Apriel Voice directly processes speech and generates both responses and tool calls without requiring a separate dialogue model. We introduce a two-stage training procedure to adapt the model to speech. The first stage establishes the speech interface, while the second jointly adapts the projector and backbone through supervised fine-tuning, text replay, and source-dependent cross-entropy and forward Kullback--Leibler divergence supervision. Automatically constructed speech-conditioned dialogues provide conversational training data, while local teacher serving, overlapping teacher and student computation, and fused full-vocabulary losses enable efficient online distillation. In thinking mode, Apriel Voice achieves a score of 98.90 on Big Bench Audio, 81.13 on Speech-BFCL, and a task success rate of 65.51% on $\tau$-Voice, with a mean latency of 1.91 seconds. It also achieves 51.27% English EVA accuracy and 74.13% experience pass@1. Finally, we deploy Apriel Voice 27B Preview on a custom serving platform to support interactive voice interactions.


<div id='cs.NI'></div>

# cs.NI [[Back]](#toc)

### [273] [ARK: Avoiding Routing Collisions for KV Cache Transfer in Disaggregated LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41160)
*Hung-Chun Lin, Ting-Wei Hsu, Chung-En Ho, Ahmed Saeed*

Main category: cs.NI

Abstract: Disaggregated LLM inference separates the prefill and decode phases across GPU pools, generating massive KV-cache transfers. Because these transfers last hundreds of milliseconds to seconds, they behave as mega elephant flows that dominate link bandwidth utilization. In this regime, stateless ECMP can perform poorly: hash collisions may overload one spine link while leaving others idle, stretching transfer times by seconds. Yet this same persistence makes coordination practical. Since these flows are long-lived, even lightweight one-to-all coordination can be amortized over their lifetime. We present ARK, a distributed elephant-flow path reservation mechanism. ARK coordinates senders to choose source ports whose hashes map concurrent flows onto distinct spines, without requiring switch changes or receiver-side packet reordering. Packet-level RDMA simulations show that ARK reduces mean and P95 FCT by up to 27.3% and 34.0% under moderate load, and further reduces mean TTFT by up to 12.9%.


### [274] [RAMA: Retrieval-Augmented Multi-Agent Routing with Episodic Memory for LEO Satellite Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42068)
*Xin Ming Li, Peng Hu*

Main category: cs.NI

Abstract: Low-Earth orbit (LEO) satellite networks present a challenging routing environment due to rapidly changing topology, traffic, and link conditions. Existing deep reinforcement learning (DRL) routing methods typically rely on current observations and information encoded in network weights, limiting their ability to exploit successful past experiences. We propose RAMA, a retrieval-augmented multi-agent DRL framework with Approximate Information State (AIS), in which each satellite combines its local observation, recent routing history, neighboring state information, and retrieved entries from a per-satellite episodic memory. Retrieved experiences are integrated through a Transformer-based decision network trained with AIS-based auxiliary objectives. With weights frozen in a 140-satellite Kepler constellation, RAMA reduces mean end-to-end latency by 13.4% and 99th-percentile latency by 66.7% relative to the state-of-theart double deep Q-network (DDQN) routing method, mainly by avoiding long detours. Updated online, RAMA remains stable while DDQN degrades. On the 1,584-satellite Starlink shell 1, the reduction in mean latency grows to 93.2%, and updating RAMA online lowers its mean latency by a further 25.5%. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of retrieval-augmented memory for scalable decentralized routing in LEO satellite networks.


<div id='q-bio.GN'></div>

# q-bio.GN [[Back]](#toc)

### [275] [VitisGP: Multi-Task Benchmark for Genotype to Phenotype Grapevine Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40668)
*Chiara D'Ercoli, May Levin, Pietro Liò*

Main category: q-bio.GN

Abstract: As climate change reshapes grape-growing conditions, predicting grapevine phenotypes from genomic variation is increasingly important for identifying and breeding cultivars suited to emerging environmental pressures. Existing research on the grapevine genotype-phenotype relationship using machine learning has often focused on individual traits, isolated regions, or a limited set of predictive models. We introduce VitisGP, a multi-phenotype curated dataset, integrating genome-wide SNP data with 26 heterogeneous phenotype prediction tasks spanning sommelier-curated aroma traits (N=11), botanical traits (N=14) and wine color---across 1,197 Vitis vinifera cultivars. We employ VitisGP to benchmark phenotype prediction, comparing machine-learning models with differing inductive biases---L2-regularized logistic regression, Random Forest, XGBoost, CatBoost, GBLUP and Transformer-based models, providing the first systematic comparison of this breadth on harmonized grapevine data. Across the benchmark, CatBoost achieves the strongest overall performance (macro-average AUROC=0.696 across 26 tasks), although model performance varies substantially across phenotype classes, with sensory and color traits showing stronger predictability than agronomic characteristics. VitisGP provides the community with a resource for investigating the genomic basis of phenotypic traits and screening grapevine diversity for climate-relevant characteristics.


<div id='cond-mat.stat-mech'></div>

# cond-mat.stat-mech [[Back]](#toc)

### [276] [Underdamped Stochastic Optimal Transport with Landauer boundary conditions: machine learning and analytical solution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41599)
*Julia Sanders, Dario Lucente, Marco Baldovin, Paolo Muratore-Ginanneschi*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

Abstract: Finding optimal transport protocols for underdamped stochastic processes is a difficult yet important problem in control theory. In this paper we develop a direct method, based on machine learning, to find the optimal solution of the problem in the presence of non-linear boundary conditions, including those modelling the Landauer bit-erasure problem. The dynamics is steered from a single-peak to a double-peak distribution in position space, by only acting on a controllable external potential. The problem is highly non-linear, and analytical solutions are not available in general. We benchmark our numerical results with a perturbative expansion, which is expected to hold in a suitable overdamped limit.


<div id='cs.GT'></div>

# cs.GT [[Back]](#toc)

### [277] [When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process? Correspondences Between Game Theory and Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41962)
*Eduardo C. Garrido-Merchán*

Main category: cs.GT

Abstract: Reinforcement learning and game theory describe the same object from two sides. Game theory says what a rational outcome of a strategic interaction is; reinforcement learning says how an agent could reach one through experience. The two literatures nevertheless keep separate vocabularies for identical structures, and the treatments that bridge them present the correspondence as analogy rather than theorem. This monograph supplies the theorems. A game is a Markov decision process in three distinct senses, and every table entry is labelled with the one that applies: a definitional identity, an exact reduction carried by a value- and solution-preserving map, or a structural analogy that fails under identifiable conditions. We take every standard class of game, from normal-form through repeated, stochastic, partially observable, extensive-form with imperfect information, Bayesian, graphical, Stackelberg, potential, coalitional, mean-field and evolutionary games, and identify for each the decision-theoretic model that reinforcement learning already studies under another name. The same treatment is applied to solution concepts: Nash equilibrium and the fixed point of the Bellman optimality operator, quantal response equilibrium and the soft Bellman operator, the Shapley value and credit assignment, Blackwell approachability and no-regret learning. We map each model class onto the training procedures sound for it and record the complexity of the associated solution problems. A final part collects the correspondences that do not hold: the non-stationarity that invalidates the independent-learner reduction, the impossibility results that forbid uncoupled convergence to Nash equilibrium, and the gap between the coarse correlated equilibria that no-regret self-play reaches and the correlated equilibria it is credited with.


<div id='stat.AP'></div>

# stat.AP [[Back]](#toc)

### [278] [Model-Agnostic Search for New Physics in the Presence of Imperfect Background by Smearing the Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40734)
*Soheun Yi, John Alison, Mikael Kuusela*

Main category: stat.AP

Abstract: In the search for new particles in high-energy physics, it is necessary to first define the signal region (SR) enriched in signal events, and then compare events in this region with the expected background distribution. While most existing search methods rely on prior domain knowledge of either the signal or the background, there has been less effort to relax both assumptions simultaneously, as methods that weaken one assumption typically rely on strong knowledge of the other. We propose Model-Agnostic Search with Smearing and Imperfect Background (MASSIB), a method that addresses this challenge by learning the density ratio of events relative to a misspecified background distribution using modern tools from machine learning, and defining the SR as the region with high density ratio. Recognizing that a naive application of this idea introduces significant bias in background estimation when interpolating from the control region to the SR, MASSIB mitigates this issue by introducing a "smeared" density ratio, which can be learned efficiently through data-level manipulation and used to define an SR that allows for accurate background estimation. By applying it to various types of simulated Large Hadron Collider (LHC) events with realistic background mismodeling, we demonstrate that MASSIB identifies a data-driven SR that achieves high signal concentration consistently across different types of signals while avoiding bias in background estimation. The method does this without making strong assumptions about either the signal or background distribution. We then show that this robustness extends to the validity and strong statistical power of the resulting hypothesis tests for signal detection, demonstrating the potential of this approach for future model-agnostic searches at the LHC.


<div id='eess.SY'></div>

# eess.SY [[Back]](#toc)

### [279] [Riemannian Optimization on Unknown Manifolds via Stochastic Proximal Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41379)
*Ruixiang Wu, Jiahao Ai, Tinko Sebastian Bartels, Xuanzhuo Liu, Yifei Wu, Yu Mao, Tongxin Li, Costas Courcoubetis*

Main category: eess.SY

Abstract: We study Riemannian optimization on an unknown manifold accessed only through feasible samples. The central challenge is to turn feasible samples into optimization steps, i.e., the samples are drawn from the unknown feasible manifold, but do not directly provide the tangent spaces, projections, or retractions required by classic Riemannian methods. Moreover, regions with low objective values may be sparsely represented in the data, so directly sampling from a distribution fitted to these observations can require many draws to find better feasible solutions. We introduce Proximal Refinement on Implicit Smooth Manifolds (PRISM), which turns feasible-sample access into an iterative optimization procedure by repeatedly sampling an objective-aware proximal distribution conditioned on the current iterate. Theoretically, we show that PRISM recovers the effect of Riemannian-gradient refinement, leading to provable objective descent, feasibility (manifold adherence), and convergence toward first-order stationarity. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach through synthetic experiments illustrating its geometric properties and applications to data-driven building control and diverse real world optimization tasks, highlighting its potential as a general framework for data-driven optimization across scientific and engineering domains.


### [280] [A Neural Network Approach towards Real-Time Knot Point Density Estimates for the Nonlinear Quadrotor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42027)
*Jasmine Ratchford, Raffaele Romagnoli, Mark H. Klein*

Main category: eess.SY

Abstract: Spline representations provide a compact way to approximate continuous trajectories using a finite number of parameters. When approximating trajectories generated by dynamical systems, the efficiency of a spline representation depends strongly on the placement of knot points. Uniform knot distributions often lead to inefficient representations, requiring either unnecessary control points or producing poor approximations in regions where the trajectory varies rapidly. In this work we propose a learning-based method for adaptive knot placement in B-spline approximations of trajectories generated by ordinary differential equations. A neural network learns a mapping from the initial condition of the system to quantities that characterize the local variation of the resulting trajectory. These estimates are used to construct nonuniform knot distributions that approximately equidistribute a local error indicator, reducing the number of control points needed to meet a prescribed accuracy.


<div id='math.NA'></div>

# math.NA [[Back]](#toc)

### [281] [Neural Networks Based on Power Method and Inverse Power Method for Solving Linear Eigenvalue Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.11134)
*Qihong Yang, Yangtao Deng, Yu Yang, Qiaolin He, Shiquan Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

Abstract: In this article, we propose two kinds of neural networks inspired by power method and inverse power method to solve linear eigenvalue problems. These neural networks share similar ideas with traditional methods, in which the differential operator is realized by automatic differentiation. The eigenfunction of the eigenvalue problem is learned by the neural network and the iterative algorithms are implemented by optimizing the specially defined loss function. The largest positive eigenvalue, smallest eigenvalue and interior eigenvalues with the given prior knowledge can be solved efficiently. We examine the applicability and accuracy of our methods in the numerical experiments in one dimension, two dimensions and higher dimensions. Numerical results show that accurate eigenvalue and eigenfunction approximations can be obtained by our methods.


### [282] [Meshless Latent Dynamics Models for Model Order Reduction of time-dependent Partial Differential Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41043)
*Lorenzo Tomada, Federico Pichi, Gianluigi Rozza*

Main category: math.NA

Abstract: Latent dynamics learning has emerged as a prominent approach for Model Order Reduction (MOR) of time-dependent, parameterized Partial Differential Equations (PDEs). Traditional autoencoder-based approaches are intrinsically tied to fixed spatial discretizations during both training and inference, motivating the development of meshless methodologies. In this context, conditioning provides a natural mechanism for representing temporal evolution through spatially continuous functions, enabling mesh-agnostic Latent Dynamics Models (LDMs). In this work, we introduce a unified mathematical framework for mesh-agnostic LDMs based on conditioning in Neural Fields (NeFs). Leveraging a taxonomy of conditioning mechanisms (weak, strong, and pure), we formulate meshless LDMs as NeFs conditioned on a latent state. We establish a Universal Approximation Theorem (UAT) for this class of models and analyze their stability by deriving Lipschitz bounds for different conditioning mechanisms. Our analysis reveals an expressivity--stability trade-off, providing insight into why more expressive conditioning, despite its higher representational capacity, can potentially degrade performance during temporal extrapolation. Finally, we provide a comprehensive comparison of meshless and mesh-based architectures in the continuous limit of the spatial discretization, showing that meshless models guarantee grid-independent Lipschitz bounds, unlike mesh-based counterparts. Our experiments across Burgers' equation, Navier--Stokes flow past a cylinder, and linear advection show that meshless LDMs can achieve predictive accuracy comparable to or better than their mesh-based counterparts while using only 2% of the trainable parameters and exhibiting nearly constant error across spatial discretizations.


<div id='physics.ao-ph'></div>

# physics.ao-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [283] [SATFRONT: Detecting Atmospheric Fronts with Satellite Data using Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42111)
*Ioannis Dravilas, Miruna Stoicescu, Roope Tervo, Carlos Horn, Oriol Hinojo Comellas, Sina Montazeri, Andrea Meraner, Ana Lucic*

Main category: physics.ao-ph

Abstract: Atmospheric fronts are associated with much of the high-impact weather in the mid-latitudes, yet their identification and classification remain largely manual and subjective. Existing deep learning approaches rely primarily on gridded reanalysis or forecast data, limiting their applicability to satellite observations available for time-critical applications. We introduce SATFRONT, a machine learning framework for detecting cold, warm, and occluded fronts over Europe from geostationary satellite observations, with reanalysis data as an optional additional input. The model is trained on frontal analyses from five European national meteorological services and evaluated against UK Met Office analyses for 2024. Using satellite observations alone, SATFRONT achieves a Critical Success Index (CSI) of 0.428 at a 150 km tolerance, within the range of agreement between analyses from different meteorological services (0.277-0.501) and higher than that of an operational rule-based front detector that uses satellite data (0.133). Adding ERA5 reanalysis increases the CSI to 0.591. ERA5 substantially improves the placement of individual fronts, although its effect on their annual spatial distribution varies by front type. These results demonstrate that cold, warm, and occluded fronts can be detected directly from geostationary satellite observations, providing a basis for automated frontal analyses at the high temporal frequency required for time-critical applications.


<div id='physics.chem-ph'></div>

# physics.chem-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [284] [General-purpose foundation models surpass specialized ones on chemical property prediction tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41899)
*Stepan N. Ostarkov, Gregorii A. Semakin, Ivan A. Bespalov, Michael G. Medvedev*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

Abstract: Foundation models (FMs) learn reusable representations from large collections of unlabeled data. In chemistry the corresponding data mainly consists of molecular structures, which are abundant, whereas experimental measurements are scarce.
