Chemical FMs such as ChemBERTa and MoLFormer were therefore trained on diverse molecular structures to enable fine-tuning on small experimental datasets (less than 100 datapoints in most realistic scenarios) and subsequent property prediction. At the same time, general-purpose FMs such as GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5 were trained on virtually all publicly available data, including knowledge of the humankind on molecular properties and laws governing them. Here we compare accuracies of chemical and general-purpose FMs on tasks from various areas of chemistry. Strikingly, general-purpose GPT-6 Astra outperforms the currently eligible chemical FMs on five physicochemical and quantum-chemical regression tasks when only 60 experimental datapoints are available. Only when provided 10x datapoints do the best chemical FMs reach performance of GPT-6 Astra: on 1,000 and 10,000 points they show performance on par with Astra on 60 and 1,000 points, respectively. Thus, counterintuitively, frontier general-purpose FMs are better suited for guiding chemical research in data-scarce areas than specialized chemical FMs, suggesting that learning relevant primitives (e.g. chemical structures) does not substitute training on relevant humankind knowledge.


<div id='stat.ML'></div>

# stat.ML [[Back]](#toc)

### [285] [CausalGrade: Evidence-Guided Causal Graph Scoring with Jev](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40692)
*Jiangming Zhong, Jiawei Zhu, Guanting Wu, Qiaoxi Cai, Wanyi Chen, Jiale Li*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Evaluating the structural quality of candidate causal graphs when the true causal structure is unknown is an important problem in applying causal discovery results. This paper proposes CausalGrade, a causal graph scoring method based on the probabilistic judgment model Jev. The method jointly provides Jev with evidence of association, conditional dependence, and directionality from observational data and edge probabilities produced by the causal discovery foundation model CDFM. Jev produces probabilistic judgments about direct causal edges, and a graph-level score is obtained by measuring the agreement between the adjacency structure of the graph under evaluation and these edge-level judgments. Across 300 synthetic datasets, the score exhibits a relatively strong negative correlation with structural error in all 20 combinations of four causal discovery algorithms and five variable counts, indicating that higher scores generally correspond to smaller structural errors. In 500 synthetic graph selection tasks, CausalGrade achieves a best-candidate hit rate of 54.4\%, exceeding the 48.4\% achieved by the Self-Compatibility metric \(\kappa_G\), further supporting its ability to discriminate the structural quality of graphs under evaluation.


### [286] [The Geometry of Multitask Learning: Sharp Rates for Shared Subspace Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40713)
*Tony Cai, Abhinav Chakraborty, Samory Kpotufe*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Learning a shared subspace in multitask learning is an important statistical problem, yet exact rates remain poorly understood in many of the formal settings considered in the literature. To address this gap, which arises from limitations of existing upper-bound techniques, we propose a general multitask lemma. The lemma provides sufficient conditions for fast subspace estimation in generic multitask settings, in the form of geometric conditions on the task regression functions and their estimators. We subsequently show that the multitask lemma yields sharp minimax rates---previously unknown---for linear regression and classification in the regimes considered, and rates matching up to logarithmic factors for additive-noise regression.


### [287] [INLA for Adaptive UAV Sampling of Radio Environment Maps in IoT Networks: Prior Width Governs Accuracy and Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40723)
*Changhao He, Kaichuang Yang, Xiaojuan Zhang*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: We study adaptive UAV sampling for radio environment map (REM) construction from non-Gaussian IoT observations. Integrated nested Laplace approximation(INLA) updates a Matérn GMRF posterior, with waypoints selected by entropy pre-screening and Sherman--Morrison lookahead over the reachable set. We show that the likelihood-induced Skew-Normal correction has a sextic entropy effect, explaining its limited influence on waypoint selection. Across 19 retained ray-traced windows,Random and lawnmower sampling cost a median 18.0\% and 14.0\% more residual NMSE than posterior-informed sampling, equivalently reductions of 15.3\% and 12.3\% when referred to those baselines, while differences among informative rules remain unresolved. Widening the prior shadowing standard deviation from 6.0 to 10.025\,dB improves NMSE and credible-interval coverage, with monotone improvement over a 4--16\,dB sweep on this corpus and no observed turning point, although overconfidence persists. Online width estimation from the UAV's own counts yields about 6.5\,dB without recovering the gain. Across 30 measured LoRaWAN propagation windows paired with simulated counts and scored only on received cells, the margin over non-adaptive sampling reproduces, the training-derived width minimizes the tested interval score, and GP-UCB has nominally $3$--$5\%$ lower NMSE. The results show substantial, corpus-dependent sensitivity to prior specification, while fine differences among acquisition rules have limited or unresolved effects, the preferred width depends on the corpus, scored cells and model mismatch.


### [288] [Robust-by-Design Distributional Learning from Contaminated Samples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40884)
*Nevena Gligić, Arya Farahi*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Modern data analysis pipelines increasingly rely on large datasets assembled through retrieval, scraping, automated filtering, and post-hoc curation, where the observed sample is often a contaminated mixture rather than the target population. Standard distributional objectives either ignore contamination and converge to the wrong target, or retain only a small trusted subset and discard most of the data. We study supervised and unsupervised learning when a clean reference sample is compared with a pooled sample from $r=(1-\epsilon)q+\epsilon z$, where $q$ is the latent target distribution and $z$ is an unknown contaminant. Motivated by settings with noisy membership scores for all observations and exact labels for a small audited subset, we propose contamination-corrected maximum mean discrepancy (CC-MMD), a design-based estimator that combines proxy scores with audited residual corrections to recover the oracle MMD that would have been computed from the latent clean sample. CC-MMD requires no calibration assumption on the proxy scores and no structural assumptions on the contaminant beyond standard kernel regularity. We prove finite-population design-unbiasedness of the corrected kernel sums, establish asymptotic normality of the audit correction, and show consistency for the target discrepancy $\mathrm{MMD}^2(p,q;k)$. Empirically, CC-MMD tracks the oracle under increasing contamination, improves parameter recovery and prediction in contaminated regression, and enables generative models trained on heavily corrupted mixtures to recover the clean target distribution. These results show that contaminated data need not be discarded or treated as the estimand. With sparse trusted labels and abundant noisy supervision, distributional objectives can be made robust by design.


### [289] [Adaptive Uncertainty Quantification on Simplicial Complexes with Conformalized Simplicial Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41166)
*Lekun Wang, Jaidev Goel, Yulia R. Gel, Yao Xie*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Simplicial neural networks (SNNs) are powerful tools for prediction tasks on higher-order relational datasets stored on simplicial complexes (SCs), a higher-order generalization of graphs. However, SNNs usually lack rigorous uncertainty quantification (UQ), limiting their usefulness in high-stakes scenarios. We propose conformalized SNN (CF-SNN), a framework that, given a simplicial-complex entity with m prediction targets, generates prediction sets for all m targets simultaneously. Under mild assumptions, CF-SNN guarantees that, for each target, the prediction set contains the true label with a user-specified nominal coverage probability, and the realized miscoverage proportion across all m targets is guaranteed to remain close to the nominal level with high probability. Extensive experiments across a wide range of SC prediction tasks demonstrate that CF-SNN is a flexible UQ framework that achieves user-specified coverage while producing informative prediction sets of small size.


### [290] [Coarse-Graining Optimal Transport for Rollout-Free Post-Training of Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41269)
*Soon Hoe Lim, Aditi Gupta, Michael W. Mahoney, N. Benjamin Erichson*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Flow matching depends critically on the coupling used to pair source and target samples. Optimal transport (OT) couplings can reduce velocity conflict and straighten trajectories; but, in practice, challenges remain: the source comes from a continuous distribution, only a finite number of target samples data are accessible, and the neural network has finite capacity. For these reasons, making the coupling more OT-like may not consistently improve sampling quality, in particular in the low number-of-function-evaluation (NFE) regime. Motivated by this, we introduce ReCouple, a controllable framework that constructs coarse-grained OT couplings, to better understand rollout-free OT-based post-training. We also consider extensions of this basic framework: ReCouple+ extends this toward the continuous-source setting by increasing source resolution, converging to semi-discrete OT in the appropriate limit; and ReCouple++ further adds spatial regularization, motivated by the observation that straight trajectories need not correspond to spatially simple velocity fields. Empirical validation demonstrates the effectiveness of these methods, and it reveals an NFE-dependent interplay between coupling optimality, neural realizability, and generation quality.


### [291] [Conditional Neural SDEs for Financial Time-Series Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41405)
*Sina Molavipour, Alireza M. Javid, Björn Löfdahl, Mikhail Nechaev*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Conditional generation of time-series paths is a central problem in probabilistic modeling, where the goal is to simulate future trajectories that are both realistic and responsive to external context. It has direct applications in finance and economics. Neural stochastic differential equations provide a natural framework for this task by combining continuous-time stochastic dynamics with learnable drift and diffusion functions. However, directly modeling the target state can be unstable in conditional settings, especially when the target and conditioning variables have strong level dependence, limited training data, or different this http URL propose a residualanchor Neural SDE architecture that decomposes the target into a learned conditional anchor and a stochastic residual. The anchor captures the context-dependent baseline, while the Neural SDE (Stochastic Differential Equation) models the residual dynamics around this baseline. This separation reduces the burden on the stochastic dynamics and generalizes spread-based representations to more general conditioning this http URL evaluate the approach on real-world financial time series, including conditional interest-rate scenarios and a comparison with a time-series foundation model. The results show that the proposed model can generate plausible conditional paths, maintain competitive point accuracy, and provide calibrated uncertainty estimates.


### [292] [Reliability-Weighted Direct Preference Optimization: Penalized Weights for Robust Preference Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41588)
*Bin Luo, Param Venkat Vivek Kesireddy, Anil Vallepu*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) treats every reported comparison as correct, yet preference data contain reversed, tied or otherwise noisy comparisons. RW-DPO gives each comparison an unknown reliability weight $w_i\in[0,1]$ that enters the likelihood linearly -- DPO is $w\equiv1$, a reversed comparison $w_i=0$ -- and estimates the weights jointly with the policy under a minimax-concave penalty on $-\log w_i$. The penalty keeps every comparison the model agrees with at exactly $w_i=1$ and acts only on those it contradicts, through closed-form weight updates at negligible cost over DPO. We prove that recovering all flipped comparisons from a single annotation per comparison requires margins of order $\log n$. Furthermore, in a linear-index model, starting from an initial fit pointing in the correct direction, our estimator jointly consistently recovers the preference direction and weights, as well as identifies the flipped comparisons above this margin scale, even when a fixed fraction of comparisons is flipped. In simulations RW-DPO has the lowest average estimation error among DPO, noise-rate corrections and recent robust methods, with far more precise flags than recent label-correction methods. In DPO fine-tuning of a language model with 10-30% flipped labels, its win rate does not fall as the flip rate grows and exceeds that of DPO trained on clean labels, matching the strongest recent method with corrections concentrated on the flipped labels.


### [293] [PDE-Residual-Informed Kriging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41590)
*Yu Tan, Shichang Du, Yongxiang Li*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Physics-informed Gaussian process (GP) methods provide a probabilistic framework for PDE solving. Existing approaches incorporate PDE information in different ways: pointwise conditioning uses the governing equation only at finitely many selected locations, globally constrained GPs restrict the admissible functions to satisfy the PDE exactly, and residual-penalized methods control the PDE mismatch over the whole domain through an additional penalty weight. What is still missing is a GP formulation that directly incorporates global PDE-residual information of the entire domain into its covariance structure, while retaining model flexibility and standard GP conditioning without a separate residual-weight parameter. To solve this issue, this study proposes PDE-residual-informed Kriging (PRIK) based on a PDE-residual kernel. The PDE-residual kernel is constructed by equipping a kernel-section space with an inner product defined by the $L^{2}$ inner product of PDE residuals. This construction makes PRIK yield the posterior mean with minimum integrated squared PDE residual. It also provides direct statistical interpretations: the variance-scale estimate equals the normalized PDE-residual energy (i.e., the integrated squared PDE residual of the posterior mean divided by the number of labeled observations), and the profile likelihood balances this residual energy against model complexity. Under the stated regularity and stability conditions, we establish $H^{2}$-quasi-optimality and convergence rates for the posterior mean, together with a nonasymptotic bound for its PDE-residual norm. We further show that the normalized posterior kernel converges to a variational Green-type kernel and derive a uniform nonasymptotic contraction bound for the posterior variance. Numerical studies provide empirical evidence consistent with the theory and demonstrate the PDE-solving capability of PRIK.


### [294] [Statistical Foundations of Reinforcement Learning: A Non-Asymptotic Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41903)
*Yuxin Chen, Yuejie Chi, Jianqing Fan, Gen Li, Yuting Wei, Yuling Yan*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) embodies a foundational paradigm for sequential decision-making in the face of uncertainty. The rapid growth of model complexity in emerging applications has significantly amplified the challenges of achieving data-efficient RL, particularly in sample-starved scenarios where data collection may be costly, time-consuming, or even high-stakes. Consequently, understanding and improving the data efficiency of RL algorithms has become a central problem of both fundamental theoretical interest and practical importance. Despite decades of research, the statistical underpinnings of RL remained remarkably underdeveloped until relatively recently, particularly in the finite-sample regimes most relevant to modern practice. Over the past few years, there has been a surge of activity advancing the frontiers of non-asymptotic statistical RL theory, drawing on ideas from high-dimensional statistics and probability, information theory, and statistical learning theory. In this monograph, we present a non-asymptotic treatment for RL based on tabular Markov decision processes, focusing on three canonical sampling mechanisms --- RL with a generative model, online RL, and offline RL --- together with two algorithmic paradigms: model-based and model-free approaches. Centered around non-asymptotic sample complexity analysis, our treatment emphasizes minimax-optimal algorithm design, particularly methods that attain statistical optimality without significant burn-in costs, as well as sharp statistical characterizations of several widely used RL algorithms. Whenever possible, we provide largely self-contained analyses together with intuitive explanations. We also briefly discuss several extensions, such as robust RL, competitive multi-agent RL, federated RL, uncertainty quantification, and RL from human feedback.


### [295] [WASABI: Wasserstein Sampling via Bridge Interpolants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41958)
*Sebastian Salazar, Dilum Fernando, Michal Kucer, Emily Casleton, Luhuan Wu, David M. Blei*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Sampling from Boltzmann distributions is a fundamental problem in physics, chemistry, and biology. We introduce WAsserstein SAmpling via Bridge Interpolants (WASABI), a method that trains a diffusion model to generate samples from a target Boltzmann distribution given access only to the energy function. WASABI minimizes the KL divergence between the distribution generated by its diffusion model and the Boltzmann target using Wasserstein gradient flows. This approach builds on a principle underlying classical sampling methods such as Langevin dynamics. However, applying Wasserstein gradient flows to diffusion models is challenging because the updates require the gradient of the model's own log density, which is generally intractable. We derive a practical training algorithm that avoids computing this gradient. Once trained, WASABI can generate new samples without further evaluations of the energy or its gradient. On alanine dipeptide, WASABI outperforms the best reported baselines using an estimated 3.3 times fewer energy evaluations. It also achieves competitive performance on interacting particle system benchmarks.


### [296] [Regret-Optimal Wasserstein-Robust Linear Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42109)
*Sloan Nietert, Daniel Kuhn*

Main category: stat.ML

Abstract: Distributionally robust optimization (DRO) minimizes worst-case risk under distributional uncertainty, but its decisions can be suboptimal in terms of their excess risk, or regret. We study this gap for multivariate least-squares regression, assuming that the observed source law and unknown target law differ by an adversarial 2-Wasserstein perturbation of size $\varepsilon$. We first identify the asymptotic complexity of robust decision-making in the $\varepsilon \to 0$ limit, characterized by a problem-specific condition number $\kappa$. Here, a wide spectrum of simple algorithms, including DRO, achieve the instance-optimal rate of $O(\kappa \varepsilon^2)$. For fixed $\varepsilon > 0$, we prove that DRO still achieves the minimax rate if the problem has bounded residual effective rank. On the other hand, we identify significant failure modes where DRO is provably suboptimal by dimension-dependent factors. Fortunately, we prove that two alternative procedures, minimax regret optimization (MRO) and spectral-norm regularization (SNR), attain the minimax rate. We derive an exact convex reformulation of MRO and an accelerated proximal algorithm for SNR, along with high-probability finite-sample guarantees. We support these theoretical results with numerical experiments separating the performance of MRO and SNR from DRO.


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [297] [One Verdict Is Not Enough: Regression Testing Learned World Models and Their Test Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41130)
*Junyu Huo, Zihao Wan, Ziqi Mao, Gouri Ginde*

Main category: cs.SE

Abstract: Learned world models ship as software components whose model versions, preprocessing, and test harnesses change between releases. Work on model updates already counts per-sample regressions, or negative flips, that an accuracy check hides. Two problems remain: a test can pass under one configuration and fail under another, and a harness fault can produce erroneous scores and wrong verdicts without a crash. We present TetraState, a regression-testing protocol for paired possible/impossible video tests. Each test runs under four fixed model-native configurations; its number of correct verdicts assigns one of four outcome classes. Inconclusive tests and failed executions are reported separately alongside the verdict rate. Revisions are compared by per-test outcome transitions, and the harness is versioned as part of the system under test. On all 506 IntPhys 2 Main tests and two heterogeneous systems, Cosmos 3 Nano and V-JEPA 2, two V-JEPA 2 harness revisions move modal accuracy by only 0.2 and 2.6 points yet change 11 and 214 test labels; in the second, accuracy rises although fewer tests are judged correctly, and 43 regressions keep the modal verdict, so a modal-verdict flip check misses them. These results outline a research agenda on configuration-level regression oracles for learned components.


### [298] [Lineage Is Memory: Parent Inheritance and Feedback Disclosure in Short-Budget LLM-Guided Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41157)
*Shanglin Yang, Zhan Shi*

Main category: cs.SE

Abstract: Iterative language-model optimizers refine prompts and programs by evaluating a candidate, showing a proposer model a sample of failing cases, and keeping revisions that improve performance. Recent work attributes the limits of this loop to narrow single-round reflection, introducing full-set error clustering, targeted minibatches, or larger proposer models so the optimizer sees more failure modes per step. Yet every existing evaluation tests these mechanisms exclusively inside lineage-preserving search, where each revision edits a surviving parent, leaving the contribution of parent inheritance confounded with the breadth of per-round feedback. Across 1,750 pre-registered runs spanning nine prompt and program synthesis benchmarks, we separate parent inheritance from feedback disclosure under matched evaluation budgets. We find that greedy parent inheritance drives the vast majority of optimization progress, outperforming seed-reset resampling even when seed-reset proposals receive a clustered summary of all failures in every round or use a larger proposer model. While full-set error clustering and diverse failure selection improve single-step rewrites from the seed and accelerate the opening rounds of search, sequential parent inheritance rapidly closes this gap as solved error modes are retained in the candidate and filtered from subsequent failure lists. Moreover, decomposing token expenditure in GEPA reveals that proposer reflection accounts for only a small fraction of total cost whereas validation and minibatch gating consume nearly the entire budget, showing that greedy parent inheritance over raw failures captures the gains of complex reflective loops at a fraction of their compute.


### [299] [ROSTrans: Empirically Benchmarking LLMs for ROS1-to-ROS2 Migration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41168)
*Yichen Zhao, Minghao Shao, Zhuoyan Yu, Yijun Shen, Boyuan Chen, Yik-Cheung Tam, Muhammad Shafique*

Main category: cs.SE

Abstract: Although large language models have shown strong capabilities in domain-specific code generation, their performance in robotics software development, especially for the Robot Operating System (ROS), remains largely unexplored. ROS code generation presents distinctive challenges, including complex inter-node coordination, middleware configuration, and lifecycle semantics that go far beyond standard coding benchmarks, and many important correctness properties become observable only once migrated nodes are launched and communicate. We present ROSTrans, a benchmark for evaluating LLMs on ROS1-to-ROS2 code migration, a representative and practically important setting for ROS code generation. ROSTrans comprises 108 scaffolded completion tasks drawn from a diverse set of 70+ open-source ROS1 repositories and organized into a hierarchical taxonomy assessing interface-level, behavioral-level and system-level correctness. For every task we establish an executable reference: a verified ROS2 implementation, a runtime test suite that launches the nodes and exercises their topics, services, actions and parameters, and a container ensuring reproducibility. Candidates are scored by injecting only their target files into an otherwise fixed copy of the reference package, so a candidate can neither supply nor weaken the tests it is judged by. Evaluating six frontier LLMs over five stochastic generations per task, we find pass@1 below 10.9%, and that 56.5% of all samples build successfully and then fail at runtime,and 32.0% fail runtime assertions on the migrated interfaces, a class of error no compile-only oracle can detect. Execution-guided repair further improves task success, reaching 49.1% and 47.2% for Claude Opus 4.6 and GPT-5.5 after three rounds, respectively.


### [300] [What Carries Architectural Signal? A Code-to-Architecture Mapping Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41628)
*Rakhshanda Jabeen, Morgan Ericsson, Yuyao Duan, Anna Wingkvist*

Main category: cs.SE

Abstract: Code-to-architecture mapping assigns source files to modules of a reference architecture. Representations of implementation artifacts can capture code text, dependencies, and directory organization, but predictive gains alone do not establish the contribution of additional graph processing. Dependencies may already be encoded in these representations, while directories may closely match the reference decomposition. We investigate how artifact representations and graph context affect mapping on ten Java, C, and C++ systems with a limited labeling budget. We compare lexical, structural, and contextual file representations and assess each predictor with and without dependency neighbors. Using a Heterogeneous Graph Transformer (HGT), we compare file-level graphs extended with member nodes summarizing each file's program elements, folder nodes, or both, alongside a variant encoding folder paths directly in file representations. We compare folder-aware predictions with a directory rule using the same labeled files. Representations encoding dependency information outperform lexical representations on most systems. Additional neighbor aggregation can improve mapping, depending on the predictor and aggregation direction. Folder-aware variants yield the largest and most consistent gains across the evaluated HGT configurations. However, the directory rule achieves per-system median accuracy of 89-100%, and adding folder information does not consistently improve predictions for files the rule misclassifies. Member nodes provide no consistent improvement over file-level graphs. These findings support assessing what file representations and directory organization already provide before attributing improvements to graph neural networks.


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [301] [From Teleoperation to Long-Horizon Dexterity: Evaluating Visuomotor Skills and Offline Proxies for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40828)
*Ammar N. Abbas, Aayush Jain, Prashanth Viswanath, Nivesh Gadipudi, Giulia Vilone, Jim Gibbons*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Imitation learning policies are intended to make robotic manipulation robust to variations in object pose, appearance, and environment that challenge pre-programmed motion. However, real-world performance remains difficult to predict, as conventional optimization signals such as validation loss show no consistent relationship with robot success. To identify the design choices that govern real-world performance, 281 imitation-learning policies spanning seven policy families were evaluated in approximately 1,700 real-robot trials of RJ45 connector grasping and insertion using a five-fingered robotic hand. Controlled ablations showed that observation and action representations dominated conventional optimizer and sampling choices. For point-cloud policies, region-of-interest cropping, representation in the robot-base frame, and normalization within the point encoder each improved success by 70-100 percentage points. For the vision-language-action policy, absolute rather than relative action prediction improved insertion success by up to 70 percentage points. Skill decomposition, multi-view perception, and recovery demonstrations were associated with the strongest-performing systems, although their effects were not independently isolated. Of 67 offline metrics evaluated, only five measures of predicted-motion smoothness and directness reliably correlated with insertion success (r = -0.70 to -0.80), and only within a single policy family. These results identify representation choices as key determinants of real-world imitation-learning performance and show that offline metrics can shortlist policies, but cannot replace real-robot evaluation.


### [302] [STAR: Steering Action Representations during Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41009)
*Guangyu Zhao, Yongchang Zhang, Haoxuan Ru, Kaichen He, Haoran Wen, Kun Zhan, Zeyu Zhang, Yin Li, Yiwu Zhong*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Vision-language-action models (VLAs) map language instructions and visual observations to robot actions, showing strong capabilities across robotic manipulation tasks. However, in our probing experiment, we find that action representations near the action output do not fully preserve task-relevant visual grounding. Motivated by this late-layer grounding gap, we introduce STAR, for STeering Action Representations, a training-free inference method for pre-trained VLAs. STAR applies an exponential moving average over late-layer residual updates, allowing later action representations to inherit object-aware update directions from earlier layers without model updates or architectural changes. It further uses recent proprioceptive states to adapt the mixing strength, using conservative mixing when execution dynamics change rapidly. Experiments on robot manipulation benchmarks show that STAR improves late-layer object awareness and increases average task success rates by 4.0 percentage points. Our code is available at this https URL .


### [303] [VLA Policies Do Not Know When Not to Act: Unintended Task Execution and Start Suppression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41609)
*Minsu Jang, Jae-Woo Choi, Youngwoo Yoon, Dohyung Kim*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: A robot should act only on an instruction from a person or on an intent that the robot explicitly represents and generates. Unintended actions taken without such an instruction can cause risk or inconvenience to people and the environment. We examine how recent vision-language-action (VLA) policies respond when the instruction is missing or meaningless. We apply a paired protocol with empty, neutral, nonsense, infeasible, and wrong-task instructions to six model checkpoints in LIBERO-Goal simulation. Holding the initial state fixed and varying only the instruction, we ask three questions: whether the robot starts to move, which task it completes, and what the instruction determines. Even when the instruction was empty, the robot started moving in every episode with all six checkpoints, and with five of them it often completed a task that the instruction had not specified. On two GR00T checkpoints, the instruction determined which task was executed but not whether the robot started moving; the robot moved even on infeasible instructions. We then tested three methods for suppressing the start: prepending "if no task is given do not move" to the instruction; a gate that holds the robot when the first action chunk for the given instruction nearly equals that for an empty instruction; and fine-tuning the policy to hold still on non-task inputs. The prefix stopped the robot in none of the six checkpoints, and the gate held it on non-task inputs in five of the six checkpoints. The fine-tuned policy never completed a task on non-task inputs, but it did not reliably keep the robot still, and it failed to suppress the start on infeasible instructions.


### [304] [When the Past Shapes Action: Understanding Embodied Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41688)
*Siqi Li, Ziyi Bai, Xinrun Xu, Tinglei Huang, Börje F. Karlsson*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Recent advances in generative foundation models have expanded the capabilities of embodied AI, enabling increasingly general navigation, manipulation, and long-horizon task execution. However, effective interaction with dynamic environments requires more than only strong perception and reasoning: agents must act with partial knowledge and retain, retrieve, and update information from past observations, actions, and experiences. This survey examines embodied memory as a mechanism for preserving decision-relevant information beyond the current observation. We organize existing approaches along two complementary dimensions: six fundamental memory requirements, spanning world-state persistence, task progress, belief maintenance, and experiential learning; and eight architectural categories, including recurrent states, fast weights, visual and symbolic representations, spatial memory, and experience-based systems. We analyze their capabilities and limitations, and examine how existing benchmarks evaluate memory-dependent behavior, distinguishing genuine use of historical information from general task performance. Finally, we identify open challenges in selecting relevant experiences, maintaining consistent beliefs in changing environments, and consolidating knowledge across episodes. Our analysis provides a framework for understanding memory as an essential component of reliable, adaptive, and lifelong-horizon embodied intelligence.


### [305] [RoboArchitect: Automating the Design of Robotic Agents with Persistent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42141)
*Shengguang Wu, Suning Huang, Cherie Ho, Ria Doshi, Hao Zhu, Serena Yeung-Levy*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Recent work shows that a frozen robot policy performs far better inside an agent harness, where control code decides when to query the policy, consult vision tools, or command the arm directly. Yet today these harnesses are built by hand and redesigned for each new robot or benchmark. We show that harness design can be automated, offering a strong alternative to finetuning on a novel setup. In RoboArchitect, a coding model observes a robot's rollouts, diagnoses failures, and iteratively revises the harness around the frozen policy. The harness evolves on a set of episodes for its optimization and is evaluated on held-out scenes and tasks. Without updating any model weights, RoboArchitect raises average success from 9.7% to 43.3% on unseen scenes (4.5x) and from 12.7% to 40.2% on unseen tasks (3.2x), across multiple benchmarks and policies. The gains are largest where the benchmark is far from the policy's training data: wrapping a general-purpose policy, the evolved harness yields a specialist that often outperforms even the finetuned version. Intuitive control strategies are automatically designed, such as lifting a jammed arm clear or reaching in horizontally when an obstacle blocks the way from above. The harness also evolves its own persistent memory, carrying learned experience, such as effective grasp depths, into later decisions. These results suggest that substantial headroom in robotic manipulation lies in the agent harness, and its design can be effectively automated and optimized without weight updates.


### [306] [Hierarchical World Models with Implicit Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42146)
*Gaoyue Zhou, Yvonne Wu, Zichen Jeff Cui, Nicolas Ballas, Mido Assran, Lerrel Pinto, Yann LeCun*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Planning with world models enables test-time adaptation for embodied agents, offering superior generalization over direct policy learning. However, long-horizon tasks remain hindered by cumulative errors and search complexity. Existing hierarchical methods often use rigid macro-actions or latent skills, leading to brittle representations and limited fine-grained control. We introduce ID-WM (Implicit Dynamics World Models), a hierarchical world model that decouples state-space reachability from local control. Our framework utilizes a high-level model to sample feasible transitions across the state manifold and a low-level world model as an inverse dynamics solver to ground these transitions into precise actions. This allows the planner to identify reachable subgoals without being constrained by low-level execution details. By prioritizing local physical feasibility over global behavioral patterns, ID-WM synthesizes novel long-horizon plans from short segments, transcending the specific behavioral modes seen during training. We demonstrate its effectiveness across three navigation and manipulation suites, achieving a 38.5% improvement over state-of-the-art policy and diffusion-based planners. Crucially, by leveraging this hierarchical structure to mitigate compounding errors, our approach yields a 135% performance gain over single-level world model MPC, particularly in complex, unseen configurations for long-horizon tasks. Videos can be viewed at this https URL


### [307] [Mechanistic Interpretability for End-to-End Self-Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41185)
*Manav Gagvani, Benjamin Namikas, Sivamurugan Velmurugan, Nysa Kumar, Shrey Sharma, Thomas Peterson, Pratyush Mathur, Hyunseo Chang, Rohan Paleja*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: End-to-end (E2E) autonomous driving models achieve strong planning performance but remain largely opaque, limiting observability and trust. We apply mechanistic interpretability to E2E driving by training Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) on the internal activations of a simple, single-camera end-to-end path planning model. Unsupervised clustering of SAE latents reveals interpretable structure aligned with route intent, vehicle speed, and braking behavior, recovering route intent with 98.3% accuracy without supervision. Through paired counterfactual image edits, we further show that specific SAE features are visually grounded and systematically respond to scene changes such as traffic light state and stop sign presence. Steering individual SAE latents produces predictable, monotonic changes in planner outputs including lateral displacement, curvature, and braking. Finally, we demonstrate a practical application of these representations: we build a lightweight adapter trained on SAE latents that reduces trajectory selection error by 3.44%, outperforming an equivalent adapter that omits SAE latents, confirming that the sparse representation captures useful information for interpretability. Code and visualizations are available at this https URL


### [308] [$\nabla$Mimic: Ultra-Efficient Humanoid Control with Differentiable Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41654)
*Weyl Lu, Yubei Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Robot policy learning is dominated by zeroth-order methods such as PPO, which treat the simulator as a black box and pay for it with enormous sample counts. First-order policy learning differentiates through the dynamics, but on contact-rich whole-body control it has rarely worked: its gradients explode. A recent world-model method showed that derivative information can make humanoid motion tracking far more efficient, using surrogate gradients of a learned model. We show that exact gradients of the simulator itself do better, once two obstacles are removed. Exact gradients contain rare unbounded samples, because contact dynamics is not differentiable everywhere and its derivative diverges near some events; and they are expensive, because the simulator's adjoint has to replay the contact solve. $\nabla$Mimic bounds these samples with median-relative per-sample gradient clipping at every step of the backward pass, and reuses each costly simulated window by multistep regression onto gradient-derived action targets, which bounds every action target independently of the gradient scale. NablaEngine, a differentiable GPU simulation engine for the Unitree G1, removes the cost: it computes MuJoCo's full contact dynamics with fused per-world kernels and the adjoint of a whole control step as a single megakernel, with analytic gradients that match float64 finite differences, $11.5\times$ faster than the available MuJoCo Warp adjoint. On whole-body tracking of all 40 LAFAN1 clips with one policy on one GPU, $\nabla$Mimic reaches the score reported by the state-of-the-art world-model method, 0.82, in 20 minutes instead of 1.2 h, and 0.874 in 1.8 h. SHAC, the standard first-order baseline, stops on exploding gradients within minutes. We release NablaEngine and $\nabla$Mimic as open-source infrastructure for first-order humanoid policy learning.


<div id='physics.flu-dyn'></div>

# physics.flu-dyn [[Back]](#toc)

### [309] [ART of PIV: Agentic Real-Time Particle Image Velocimetry for Active Flow Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41304)
*Antonio Terpin, Francesco Banelli, Alan Bonomi, Raffaello D'Andrea*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

Abstract: Particle image velocimetry (PIV) measures fluid motion from images of tracer particles, but its accuracy depends on choices throughout the estimation pipeline, including image preprocessing, flow estimation, and postprocessing. These choices are typically left to expert tuning, and a fixed configuration can become less accurate as conditions change. This matters especially when PIV supplies feedback for real-time control, where measurements must be both accurate and fast, and flow conditions vary. We introduce ART of PIV -- short for Agentic Real-Time and also a nod to the art of tuning a PIV pipeline -- which automates pipeline choice for each image pair. A learned agent selects a configuration according to a chosen measurement objective from a library assembled for a specified speed budget. Across cross-correlation, classical optical-flow, and learning-based estimators, ART of PIV reduces average end-point error (AEPE) by 12.2-22.4 % relative to the best single estimator at matched speed, with gains extending to 1000-4000 image pair/s. In a simulated local velocity regulation task, it reduces mean normalized closed-loop cost by 13.24-13.59 percentage points relative to a tuned fixed estimator, with the same classical controller in both cases. In a tabletop water channel, we illustrate two ways to tune ART of PIV for a fluidic pinball of three rotating cylinders: selecting between complementary optical-flow tunings for accuracy, and selecting regions of interest with coherent rotation or shear using a common flow estimator. For the first variant, we train a DreamerV3 reinforcement learning controller directly in the physical world and demonstrate tracking of a prescribed flow field in the wake of a fluidic pinball.


### [310] [Hard Constraint Projection in a Physics Informed Neural Network (Burgers)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42106)
*Miranda J S Horne, Peter K Jimack, Amirul Khan, He Wang*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

Abstract: Physics informed machine learning provides a framework to study and simulate fluid dynamics, potentially at a fraction of the computational cost of traditional numerical methods, with the goal of improving the explainability, accuracy and robustness of purely data driven machine learning methods. In this work, we extend the hard constraint projection (HCP) method developed by Chen et al. [Journal of Computational Physics, 445, 110624, 2021] from a linear partial differential equation (PDE) to a nonlinear PDE: the 1D Burgers' equation. To strictly impose a discrete approximation of the governing equation, the Burgers' equation is first locally discretised (using a finite difference stencil) then decomposed into a constraint matrix and a prediction matrix. Using tools from linear algebra, the constraint matrix is used to construct a projection matrix. A physics informed neural network (PINN) [Raissi et al. Journal of Computational Physics, 378, 686, 2019] is used to predict the velocity, and a final unlearnable layer projects the velocity prediction to the hyperplane of exact solutions to a discretised form of the Burgers' equation. Our initial results indicate that the HCP-PINN initially trains similarly to an equivalent PINN, and as the PINN loss plateaus, the HCP-PINN loss continues to decline. When tested on a previously unseen uniform grid of points covering the domain, the HCP-PINN exhibits higher accuracy, converging to a data error approximately an order of magnitude lower than the comparable PINN. We also observe that the inclusion of the HCP layer results in better observance of the governing PDE. Our findings suggest that the HCP-PINN can be more robust to the selection of physics collocation points, and less dependent on collocation point quantity.


<div id='quant-ph'></div>

# quant-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [311] [Distilling Knowledge from Search: Decoder Model Factory for Quantum LDPC Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41398)
*Anil Bilgin, Wanda Hou, Pranav Srinivas, Yi-Zhuang You*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: We introduce GraphMP, a learning-based message-passing decoder for quantum low-density parity-check codes that transfers without fine-tuning across code families, code sizes, physical error rates, and noise models. GraphMP uses local message passing to keep computation scalable as the decoding graph grows. Guided by its predictions, beam search progressively refines candidate error patterns, trading additional decoding time for lower logical error rates. We develop the decoder model factory as a paradigm for training successive models on experience generated by earlier models during decoding. Model-guided search supplies verified trajectories for knowledge distillation, folding computation spent on decoding into reusable training data. Repeated collection and distillation improve the decoder across generations and adapt its strategy as the noise changes. These results motivate a coupled quantum--classical architecture with two interacting cycles: rapid decoding protects quantum information, while slower learning renews the models from operating experience. The long-term vision is a scalable decoder model factory that produces high-performance, low-latency decoders and continually adapts them to evolving quantum hardware.


### [312] [Predictive Maintenance at the Edge: An Embedded Anomaly Detection Pipeline Trained with Quantum Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40848)
*Filipe Chagas Ferraz*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: This article presents a predictive-maintenance pipeline that detects anomalies in bearings from vibration signals, adapted for deployment at the edge on low-cost microcontrollers, and that uses quantum optimization in two steps of its training. The pipeline consists of a step of vibration feature extraction and a step of classification with a one-class support vector machine (OCSVM). The features are selected by minimum redundancy and maximum relevance (mRMR) with the variational quantum eigensolver (VQE), under a limit on computing time. The OCSVM is adapted for inference at the edge without floating-point operations and is trained by a variational quantum algorithm with Pauli-correlation encoding (PCE). In small-scale tests on a simulator, with the Paderborn bearing dataset, the VQE found the optimal selection in 36% of the runs with Adam, and PCE chose better support vectors than chance, until a classical local search made the two equal. A classical baseline, the usual OCSVM trained by SMO with its weights rounded, matched them without any search.


### [313] [Estimation of Quantum Umlaut Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41282)
*Beomjoon Kim, Donghwa Ji, Kabgyun Jeong*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: Quantifying correlations is important for understanding how quantum states and channels support information processing. Quantum umlaut information $U$ connects such correlations to reliability in hypothesis testing and certain communication settings, but its known formulas require classical descriptions of the underlying states. To estimate correlations without these descriptions, we introduce measured quantum umlaut information $U^M$ by replacing Umegaki relative entropy with measured relative entropy. Its variational formulation leads to a quantum generative adversarial framework for estimating $U^M$ from measurement statistics of unknown bipartite states and classical-quantum channels. This framework optimizes reference states and measurements without reconstructing the target states or channel outputs. For classical-quantum channels, it also optimizes the input probabilities. We further analyze the relation between $U^M$ and $U$, deriving a state-dependent gap bound and characterizing equality for full-rank bipartite states. Numerical simulations show trained estimates close on average to independently computed numerical references for $U^M$ across the tested states and channels. These estimates could help assess how well quantum memories preserve correlations and how noise or drift affects input-output dependence in quantum transmitters and state-preparation devices.


### [314] [Leveraging Quantum Measurement Randomness for Shot-Efficient Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41721)
*Tianhao Guo, Dianxin Zheng, Yuhua Qian, Yaohui Zheng, Yuzhi Yang, Merouane Debbah, Chau Yuen*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: In quantum computing, the readout always carries finite-shot sampling noise. Suppressing this noise through repeated measurements raises the cost of a quantum neural network (QNN). Meanwhile, a stochastic diffusion sampler deliberately adds randomness at each reverse (generation) step. This paper turns the measurement noise from a liability into a resource. Specifically, we use the noise quantum measurement produces anyway to replace part of the noise the sampler would otherwise inject. We characterize the measurement error as multinomial sampling noise propagated through a trainable linear readout. To connect the circuit's Born probabilities to the denoising task, we further formulate the diffusion process on a bounded zero-sum hyperplane mapped to the probability simplex. Therefore, we can employ a compensation noise to match the mean and covariance of the shot-noise-free linear update under some minimum shot conditions. Solving this condition for the shot count gives a state-dependent adaptive budget. For the denoising diffusion implicit model (DDIM), this budget increases as the sampler becomes more deterministic. On an eight-dimensional Gaussian mixture, the schedule achieves comparable generation quality with $303$ shots per sample against a high-shot reference of $1.6\times10^{6}$, about $5300\times$ fewer. Latent diffusion experiments on MNIST further validate the proposed mechanism for generation in a learned latent space.


### [315] [Overcoming the Representational Limitations of Amplitude Encoding for Quantum Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41824)
*Vincent Gurgul, David Prinz*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: Quantum Neural Networks (QNNs) offer a framework for learning functions from classical data, but their expressive power depends critically on how inputs are encoded into quantum states. Angle encoding can require resources that scale with the number of features, whereas amplitude encoding represents $d$ features using only $\mathcal{O}(\log_2 d)$ qubits. This compactness comes at a representational cost: normalization makes conventional amplitude encoding invariant to nonzero real rescaling of the input, up to an unobservable global phase. We show that this invariance induces an exact, architecture-independent restriction on the resulting hypothesis class. No downstream quantum circuit can distinguish inputs differing by nonzero real scaling. We develop a general condition under which deterministic preprocessing can remove this ambiguity and show that nonlinearity alone is insufficient. Based on this analysis, we introduce a fixed nonlinear feature map that provably distinguishes all real inputs under subsequent amplitude encoding, without adding trainable parameters and while retaining logarithmic qubit scaling. We evaluate the resulting QNN on three canonical classification benchmarks and a nonlinear synthetic dataset against conventional amplitude and angle encoding, as well as a classical neural network. Across these tasks, the proposed encoding consistently improves classification accuracy over both quantum encoding baselines by 21$-$44 %. Our results establish normalization-induced scale invariance as a concrete representational bottleneck of amplitude encoding and demonstrate that it can be removed while preserving a compact quantum representation, marking a step toward quantum models capable of capturing arbitrary relationships in classical data.


### [316] [Empirical Evaluation of Quantum Kernel Methods for Classification: A Comparative Study with Classical Kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42029)
*Ronak Bhalgami*

Main category: quant-ph

Abstract: Quantum kernel methods are the most concrete near-term proposal for quantum machine learning on classical data, yet published benchmarks disagree on whether they outperform tuned classical kernels, and the disagreement cannot be resolved from the literature because the comparisons are not protocol-controlled. We argue that the obstacle is one of measurement, not theory: the usual setup fixes the quantum feature map's data scaling while grid-searching the classical kernel's hyperparameters, recomputes nothing per fold, and reports cost in simulator time. We therefore treat the comparison itself as the object of study and impose three controls. The bandwidth is tuned on the same grid budget as the classical $\gamma$, the kernel is recomputed inside every fold from fold-fitted preprocessing, and cost is counted in hardware circuit evaluations. On top of these we run a factorial design of 324 cells in which dimensionality, training size, class separability and data structure are explicit covariates, together with four standard datasets, a shots-versus-gate-noise ablation, and a cost sweep. The dominant effect we measure is not quantum: tuning the feature map's bandwidth is worth 0.120 accuracy on synthetic data and 0.339 on real data, exceeding every quantum-classical difference we observe. Tuned quantum kernels are then statistically indistinguishable from a tuned RBF on all four real datasets. The advantage on synthetic data is confined almost entirely to data generated by the feature map itself, and it shrinks with dimensionality (-0.0045 per qubit) while growing with sample size. Finite sampling costs an order of magnitude more accuracy than gate and readout noise, and a fidelity kernel needs $5\times10^{7}$ circuits at n = 10,000. Protocol variance is thus sufficient to explain the field's contradictory conclusions.


<div id='eess.SP'></div>

# eess.SP [[Back]](#toc)

### [317] [Pinching Antennas for ISAC: Waveguide Architectures, Solutions, and Future Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41380)
*Qian Gao, Ruikang Zhong*

Main category: eess.SP

Abstract: Integrated sensing and communication (ISAC) is a core design principle for sixth-generation (6G) wireless systems, yet fixed antenna geometry limits its performance. Pinching antenna systems (PASS) guide electromagnetic waves along dielectric waveguides and radiate them through position-adaptive antennas, allowing communication links and sensing observations to benefit from spatial reconfiguration. This article reviews PASS-enabled ISAC through single-waveguide, multi-waveguide, and segmented-waveguide architectures, comparing their feed structures, spatial degrees of freedom, operating protocols, and uplink/downlink support. We organize solution pathways around physics-consistent modeling, joint spatial-signal optimization, dynamic channel acquisition, and stable real-time control. A segmented waveguide-enabled pinching-antenna array (SWAN) case study demonstrates joint segment selection, antenna po?sitioning, and transmit beamforming using segment-hysteresis reinforcement learning. The results illustrate how waveguide loss and per-segment aperture gain create communication-sensing tradeoffs. We conclude with research directions in joint transmit?receive operation, measurement-calibrated digital twins, and trustworthy control.


<div id='cs.CY'></div>

# cs.CY [[Back]](#toc)

### [318] [AI evaluators favor job applications written by certain AI models, independent of candidate quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41866)
*Nicolò Pagan, Michael Andreas Ebling, Stefania Ionescu, Anikó Hannák*

Main category: cs.CY

Abstract: Job applicants increasingly use large language models (LLMs) to write cover letters, while employers deploy LLMs to screen them. In this all-AI setting, we ask whether the \emph{choice of model} affects hiring outcomes independently of candidate qualifications. We introduce a multi-model auditing framework pairing eleven LLM writer agents with nine API-hosted evaluator agents across ten job descriptions and fifty candidates per job. Four findings emerge. First, we identify a generative preference hierarchy: a consistent global ranking of writer models by evaluator acceptance, in which locally hosted models are systematically disadvantaged regardless of their license, compounded by self-preference bias where evaluators favour their own outputs. Second, evaluator models differ substantially in how much bias they introduce based on applicants' tool choice, offering actionable guidance for fairer screening design. Third, this hierarchy has measurable competitive consequences: in the most adverse pairing, approximately two (1.8) out of twenty-five qualified candidates are filtered out purely due to model mismatch. Fourth, a common mechanism explains the patterns above: writer preference and self-preference trace to a combination of how much each writer leans on specific stylistic cues and how strongly evaluators, often unanimously, reward or penalise those same cues. A further check finds little evidence that an unmeasured "writer fingerprint" remains once these same features are accounted for. We discuss implications for algorithmic fairness in employment, regulation of AI-assisted screening, and the design of auditing frameworks that can be reapplied as the model landscape evolves.


### [319] [On-Chain Behavioral Signatures for Classifying Centralized Exchange Addresses on Ethereum Using Proof-of-Reserves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41888)
*Seongchan Park, Hyunho Jeon, Byungsoo Kang, Gaeun Kim, Minseob So, Seongyong Kim*

Main category: cs.CY

Abstract: Centralized exchanges (CEXs) are key gateways between traditional finance and digital asset markets, yet identifying their blockchain addresses remains challenging. Existing approaches often rely on opaque attribution labels or treat exchanges as secondary classification targets, leaving generalization to unseen exchanges insufficiently examined. This study develops a classification framework using Proof-of-Reserves (PoR) disclosures, with 3,033,857 Ethereum addresses from 15 CEXs and five Non-CEX categories. We derive 58 on-chain behavioral features and evaluate LightGBM against logistic regression (LR) under a leave-one-exchange-out protocol. Across five lookback windows (30-730 days), LightGBM consistently outperformed LR, with macro PR-AUC increasing from 0.6611 to 0.9594 using the selected 17 features, compared with 0.7697 for LR at 730 days. Robustness checks showed comparable performance when feature selection and hyperparameter optimization were repeated across splits and held-out exchanges, while exchange-balanced reweighting improved macro PR-AUC. SHAP analysis and feature-group ablation identified gas behavior, ERC-20 token activity, and internal transactions as key predictive signals in the constructed dataset. These findings support interpretable CEX address screening for blockchain monitoring, while the released feature dataset enables reproducible follow-up research.


<div id='math.OC'></div>

# math.OC [[Back]](#toc)

### [320] [Near-Optimal Second-Order Convex Finite-Sum Optimization under Individual Smoothness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42040)
*Xinliang Zhang, Lesi Chen, Zonghan Li, Jingzhao Zhang*

Main category: math.OC

Abstract: We study the stochastic second-order oracle complexity of solving smooth convex finite-sum optimization with $n$ components. Very recently, Tsedenov \& Kovalev (2026) showed that the minimax optimal complexity under averaged smoothness and convexity is $\tilde{\Theta}(n + n^{6/7} \epsilon^{-2/7})$ by establishing nearly matching upper and lower bounds. In this paper, we show that individual smoothness and convexity provably enable further acceleration, where the lower complexity bound is $\Omega(n+ n^{5/7} \epsilon^{-2/7})$ under this stronger assumption. Then, by establishing a novel second-order self-bounding inequality, we design a new Taylor-corrected lazy Newton method, which can lead to a better complexity of $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n + n^{5/7} \epsilon^{-2/7})$ by applying the optimal Monteiro-Svaiter acceleration, matching our lower bound up to only logarithmic factors.


### [321] [Learning Stabilizing Latent Feedback Controllers via Multi-Step Backward Losses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40931)
*Paul Lutkus, Lars Lindemann, Stephen Tu*

Main category: math.OC

Abstract: Can learned latent dynamics models support the design of feedback controllers that provably stabilize the original system? For linear time-invariant systems, we show that learning linear autoencoders and latent dynamics with multi-step prediction-reconstruction losses $\unicode{x2014}$ which we term "backward losses" as they encourage latent trajectories to reconstruct full state trajectories $\unicode{x2014}$ provably recovers the structure needed for stabilization. Specifically, a sufficiently long reconstruction horizon ensures that a minimum ${H}_\infty$-norm controller designed using the learned latent model stabilizes the original system through output-feedback via the corresponding encoder, provided the latent dimension is large enough to capture all non-stable modes of the underlying system. We also show why both reconstruction and a multi-step horizon are essential: forward prediction losses that require only low prediction error along latent trajectories admit global loss minimizers that discard unstable modes regardless of horizon length, while single-step reconstruction losses can yield latent models whose stabilizing controllers fail to stabilize the original system. Finally, experiments on a non-linear flexible-arm system illustrate the practical implications of our results for learning non-linear autoencoders and latent dynamics.


### [322] [Stochastic and Noisy Oracles in Smooth Continuous Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41086)
*Albert S. Berahas, Liyuan Cao, Katya Scheinberg*

Main category: math.OC

Abstract: We review models of inexact computational oracles in smooth continuous optimization and their implications for the convergence, computational complexity, and attainable accuracy of optimization algorithms. We adopt a framework in which zeroth-,first-, and second-order oracles are characterized by quantitative error bounds and may accept additional control parameters that influence both their accuracy and computational cost. We introduce deterministic and stochastic oracle models and illustrate their properties through a variety of examples, including finite-difference approximations, stochastic gradients, and randomized derivative estimators. Particular attention is given to the distinction between absolute and relative errors, bias and variance, and stochastic function evaluations with independent or coupled randomness. A central theme of the review is the use of common analytical tools to recover and compare convergence and complexity guarantees under different oracle models. For deterministic and stochastic first-order methods, we show how a general descent inequality leads to complexity bounds under various assumptions on the objective function and all proposed oracle models. In the stochastic setting, we develop bounds on expected stopping times and the accuracy that can be guaranteed under different oracle conditions. We also examine the total computational complexity of algorithms relying on specific oracle constructions and briefly review models of inexact second- order information and their use in optimization algorithms.


### [323] [Variance Reduction for Decentralized Stochastic Optimization with Multiple Local Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41927)
*Jie Liu, Nicola Bastianello, Wenying Xu, Daniel W. C. Ho*

Main category: math.OC

Abstract: Multiple local updates are widely used in decentralized optimization to enhance communication efficiency, often in conjunction with stochastic gradients when exact gradient computation is costly. However, such algorithms generally converge only to a neighborhood of the optimum, whose size depends on the stepsize and the variance of gradient noise. Variance reduction has been incorporated into ADMM-based methods to achieve exact convergence in expectation, but their stepsizes typically scale inversely with both the square root of the network size and the cube of the number of local updates, leading to slow convergence as the network size or the number of local updates increases. In this paper, we propose a novel primal-dual-based decentralized optimization algorithm that integrates variance reduction with multiple local updates, with a stepsize independent of the network size and inversely proportional to the number of local updates (rather than its cube), thereby enabling significantly larger stepsizes and faster convergence. Moreover, to the best of our knowledge, we establish the first linear convergence guarantee in expectation for decentralized optimization with multiple local updates under stochastic gradients in the strongly convex setting. Beyond the theoretical guarantee, the proposed method exchanges only one variable per round, halving the communication cost compared with gradient-tracking-based methods. Numerical experiments further demonstrate its advantages in convergence speed and accuracy.


### [324] [Exact Theory and Optimal Stepsizes for Stochastic Gradient Descent in Smooth Convex Minimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42048)
*Benjamin Grimmer, Qining Shen*

Main category: math.OC

Abstract: This work considers the design of stepsize schedules for stochastic gradient descent in smooth convex optimization. Throughout, we assume that gradient noise is conditionally zero-mean with bounded variance. Among problems with dimension $d\geq N+1$, we provide an exact worst-case analysis of the expected suboptimality of stochastic gradient descent's final iterate. Our analysis applies to any (potentially nonconstant) schedule of $N$ stepsizes in $[0,1.5]^N$. Here, exact means that we provide a matching objective function and noise distributions where our guarantee is attained. Whenever the variance bound is at least $\Omega(1/\sqrt{N})$, our theory provides the minimax optimal stepsize schedule for stochastic gradient descent and proves its uniqueness. These optimal stepsizes monotonically decrease, ruling out any accelerations in expected final suboptimality from fixed fractal schedules observed in the deterministic case.


<div id='cs.IR'></div>

# cs.IR [[Back]](#toc)

### [325] [Context as Infrastructure: Knowledge Compilation for Retrieval in Agentic Systems with Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41172)
*Zhichang Yu, Baining Cao, Sbaaoui Idriss, Qi Wang, Gangqiang Xu, Ling Qin, Xiaoling Jin, An Liu, Shaohao Cai, Xiaoyang Bao, Shengle Chen, Changchang Ding, Pengfei Zhu, Jingxiang Xu, Yifeng Mao, Lu Liu, Hai Jin, Yingfeng Zhang*

Main category: cs.IR

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation is becoming agentic. A search harness writes queries, reads results, reformulates, and decides when to stop. This largely solves accuracy: a harness with only BM25/grep and MiniMax M3 answers 85% of BrowseComp-Plus and 91% on multi-hop FRAMES in our runs, without compiled or vector indexes. It exposes a context problem: an undisciplined loop repeats queries, rereads documents, and fills the window with unused passages. The missing layer is knowledge compilation: a configurable ingestion pipeline where LLMs enrich documents with context no chunk contains alone--navigation hierarchies with claim-evidence leaves, entity graphs, timelines, mind maps, ontologies, wikis, and memory. Compilation is not a better retriever: under a competent harness, structural artifacts gave no detectable accuracy gain on FRAMES, and a PageIndex-style outline degraded at scale. It is a supply layer. A navigation tree whose leaves are claim-evidence pairs--the mechanism we measure--cuts query tokens by 91% when evidence suffices and adds 1.9% when it does not, a 38% average reduction; this implies a 2% break-even sufficiency rate and a measured rate near 43%. A decision gate cuts latency from 140 s to 30 s, though substitution's token saving is bought with retrieval rounds and carries its own latency cost. Other artifacts--ontology, graph, wiki, timeline, mind map, memory--pay off in deployment (governance, integration, onboarding, temporal orientation, continuity) where accuracy benchmarks cannot see them. We describe RAGFlow's pipeline and harness (open source at this https URL ), which turns the loop into five disciplined stations and keeps the context entering the model the smallest sufficient set; together they form an agent-native database: a store that hands the agent compiled, governed context instead of a ranked list of passages.


### [326] [ProSearch: Benchmarking Multi-Constraint Protocol Retrieval in Experimental Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41396)
*Wenliang Liang, Zhiyuan Ning, Haolin Chen, Yuanchun Zhou, Wenjuan Cui, Yi Du*

Main category: cs.IR

Abstract: In AI for Science scenarios, experimental design and method reproduction require retrieving protocols that satisfy multiple interdependent constraints, yet existing benchmarks lack systematic evaluation of this capability. To address this gap, we introduce ProSearch, a benchmark for multi-constraint protocol retrieval in experimental science. ProSearch has three key features: (1) realism, with queries grounded in real experimental records and expert-defined templates; (2) complexity, with 60 experimental fields and their dependencies; and (3) diagnosticity, with 3-5 explicit constraints per query, including numerical and negation constraints. We construct ProSearch through LLM-based extraction, data-driven constraint selection, controlled query generation, and evidence-based relevance validation. The resulting benchmark contains 1,515 queries across six experimental science domains. We conduct extensive experiments on ProSearch with 19 representative retrievers and complementary analyses. The best-performing retriever achieves an average nDCG@10 of only 0.290, which improves to 0.474 with reranking. Structured diagnostic analysis shows that explicit constraint matching provides a strong ranking signal, while manual error analysis identifies joint constraint binding as a key bottleneck. ProSearch provides a testbed for systematically evaluating scientific retrieval systems.


<div id='eess.IV'></div>

# eess.IV [[Back]](#toc)

### [327] [With foundation-model features, aggregator complexity does not improve cross-hospital discrimination in gastric whole-slide image classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41328)
*Nheng Vanchhay, Ali Hussain, Khan Ziaullah, Moon-Il Joo, Md Ariful Islam Mozumder, Rashedul Islam Sumon, Won-Ae Lee, Min-Sun Cho, Hyeong-Chan Shin, Yujun Park, Baek Hwan Cho, Nam-Hoon Cho, Heejung An, Heung-Kook Choi, Haeyoun Kang, Hyunki Kim, Hee Cheol Kim*

Main category: eess.IV

Abstract: Whole-slide image classifiers are usually developed within one hospital and often go untested at unseen hospitals. We assembled 5,036 haematoxylin-and-eosin-stained gastric slides from five Korean hospitals, scanned at 20x and 40x, and compared multiple-instance learning aggregators under leave-one-hospital-out validation on frozen pathology foundation-model features. A hierarchical patch-region-slide graph aggregator (CHIME-MIL) with site alignment and two-scale fusion reached a mean area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of 0.837, above six conventional attention and transformer aggregators trained without these steps. Under a matched recipe it did not exceed attention-based pooling, and global max pooling given the same alignment and fusion was better (AUC 0.855, P = 0.0002). The alignment, which subtracts each hospital's mean feature vector without labels, onboarded a new hospital from ten unlabelled slides and cost 0.002 AUC without held-out-hospital data, but its accuracy gains were small and inconsistent. The model had low top-label calibration error (0.053) but under-confident malignancy probabilities, and removing its most-attended patches lowered the malignancy probability far more than removing random patches. With these features, more complex aggregators did not improve discrimination; what a deployable system adds is label-free onboarding, calibration checks and auditable evidence.


### [328] [CalibCause: A Quality-Gated, Interpretable and Calibrated Deep Learning Framework for Diabetic Retinopathy Grading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40699)
*Sohaib Sulman, Muhammad Atif Saeed, Akhtar Jamil, Alaa Ali Hameed, Roland Aydin*

Main category: eess.IV

Abstract: Most deep learning systems for diabetic retinopathy (DR) grading are evaluated on a single data source, with limited evidence of cross-site generalisation, uncertainty calibration, and interpretability. We propose CalibCause, a quality-gated mixture-of-experts framework that combines per-expert visual explanations, label-free image-quality conditioning, knowledge distillation, and calibrated confidence with deferral. We evaluated the framework using 51,310 fundus images from EyePACS, DDR, and APTOS 2019, with IDRiD and DeepDRiD for external testing. Evaluation included pooled testing, leave-one-environment-out experiments, paired bootstrap confidence intervals, and local grade-threshold adaptation. On the pooled test split, the label-free distilled student achieved quadratic weighted kappa (QWK) of 0.863 and accuracy of 0.865 with 27.8 million parameters, 52% fewer than its teacher. Across held-out environments, the label-free model achieved mean QWK of 0.741 compared with 0.743 for a plain ConvNeXt-Tiny baseline, while adversarial invariance did not improve cross-site grading accuracy. Refitting grade thresholds using 200 labelled images increased held-out EyePACS QWK from 0.533 to 0.621 for the label-free model. After vector scaling, expected calibration error ranged from 0.025 to 0.036 across CalibCause variants, and deferring the least confident 20% of images increased student accuracy from 0.865 to 0.923 on the remaining images. These findings demonstrate that quality-aware grading, interpretable expert routing, model compression, and calibrated deferral can be integrated without sacrificing cross-site performance relative to a plain baseline, although reliable deployment still requires local validation and adaptation.


### [329] [Enhancing Medical Lesion Segmentation via Several Simple Modules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41652)
*Ruichen Li, Zhao Yao*

Main category: eess.IV

Abstract: This work introduces three lightweight enhancement modules for medical lesion segmentation to improve clinical applicability, particularly under data-scarce and computationally constrained environments. Current mainstream methods struggle with the lack of high-quality annotations for rare pathologies, which often leads to severe morphological distortions during inference, despite achieving seemingly ideal DSC scores. Furthermore, the isolated development of individual segmentation tasks restricts cross-domain methodological innovation, underscoring the need for leveraging shared knowledge across different lesions within the same anatomical region. However, constrained access to historical data and annotations hinders efficient knowledge extraction and triggers catastrophic forgetting when adapting to new classes. To address these challenges, we first propose DT-Morph Combined Loss with continuous spatial pooling to optimize differentiable topological structures and boundary continuity. Second, we introduce 3D Group Prompt, which extracts domain-universal knowledge via curated volumetric image-mask alignment prompts together with text prompts built on SAT baseline. Finally, we replace conventional output layers with Many-to-One Conditional Segmentation Head equipped with synonym-resolved routing, which brings negligible computational cost and enables fast incremental learning for new categories without sacrificing performance on old tasks. Ablation studies on abdominal and extra-abdominal datasets covering both organ and lesion segmentation verify the efficacy of our modules.


<div id='cond-mat.mtrl-sci'></div>

# cond-mat.mtrl-sci [[Back]](#toc)

### [330] [Shock Loading Accesses Reaction Pathways beyond Accelerated Thermolysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41955)
*Mingjie Wen, Jiahe Han, Zihan Zhou, Hao Wang, Xueyong Guo, Qingzhao Chu, Dongping Chen*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

Abstract: Thermolysis and shock loading can produce similar major stable products in energetic materials, despite different chemical evolution and reaction pathways. Here, we develop EMFF-MACE, a reactive machine-learning potential for C/H/N/O energetic materials, enabling thermolysis simulations of 20 materials and shock simulations of 10 on a common potential energy surface. Comparisons at matched normalized reaction progress reveal divergent chemical states and local structural environments. Mean reaction-state similarity decreases from 94.0% at 20% progress to 25.9% at trajectory endpoints, while 47.1-92.6% of shock local environments lie outside corresponding material-specific thermolysis reference domains. Structural and reaction-network analyses reveal enhanced intermolecular reorganization and additional association and rearrangement branches under shock loading, despite partially shared early decomposition chemistry.
